# **Commentary on *Vorlesungen über Ethik und Wertlehre 1908–1914* (Husserliana XXVIII), pp. 3–162**

This is a paragraph-by-paragraph commentary on Part A of Husserliana XXVIII — the lecture course *Vorlesungen über Grundfragen zur Ethik und Wertlehre* of 1914, which Ullrich Melle places first in the volume as the latest and fullest redaction of the core 1908/09 ethics course. The range covers the four Abschnitte of that course (§§ 1–21) together with the two appended Beilagen (I and II). Citation is by Husserliana XXVIII pagination, given in the margins of the German edition. German technical terms are retained with an English rendering on first use and then used in German thereafter. Headings are translated.

A few characteristic traps beset this text, and the commentary guards against them throughout. First, the central *Parallelismus* between logic and ethics is a structural analogy, not a reduction: Husserl never makes ethics a branch of logic, and he insists that *wertende Vernunft* (valuing reason) and *praktische Vernunft* (practical reason) are autonomous reason-domains with their own apriori. Second, *formale Axiologie* and *formale Praktik* are formal disciplines in exactly the sense formal logic is: they abstract from all value-matter (*Wertmaterie*), so they are not yet a material ethics and settle no concrete question of what is good. Third, the *Gefühl*/*Gemüt* (feeling/emotive) side of the analogy is not psychologistic: Husserl is extending his anti-psychologism from the *Prolegomena* into the axiological sphere, so value-laws are ideal and objective, not empirical generalizations about how people happen to feel. Fourth, the *kategorischer Imperativ* that emerges late in Part A is Husserl's own formal-axiological reconstruction ("do the best attainable"), not Kant's, and his recurring quarrel with Kant should not be read as endorsement. Fifth, *objektivierende Akte* (objectivating acts) versus *Gemütsakte* (emotive acts) is the load-bearing distinction of the whole course; the question whether emotive acts can themselves "objectivate" (constitute objects) is left open and pressing, and must not be prejudged. Finally, *Wert* (value) is treated as a genuine objectivity correlative to valuing, not as a projected feeling-tone — the realism about values is deliberate.

## **I. Section. The Parallelism between Logic and Ethics**

### **§ 1\. Logic as Art-of-Method and Pure Logic**

Husserl opens from the traditional triad. Truth, goodness, and beauty are set up as coordinate philosophical ideas, and to them are assigned three parallel normative disciplines: logic, ethics, aesthetics. He flags at once that this *Parallelisierung* rests on motives that are deep but insufficiently clarified, and that it harbors large philosophical problems. The pursuit of these problems is motivated twice over: by the interest in a scientific grounding of ethics specifically, and by a more general philosophical interest. The triad is thus not taken as a settled framework but as a clue whose ground needs excavation.

The second paragraph fixes the precondition for everything that follows. For the grounding of a scientific philosophy it matters greatly that the old idea of a *formale Logik* (formal logic) be determined in a "natural" way — more precisely, by exhibiting the apriori demarcations that the field itself prescribes — and then that such a logic actually be developed in its apriori purity. Husserl anchors this in his own prior work: the *Prolegomena zur reinen Logik* (the first volume of the *Logische Untersuchungen*) and the first book of *Ideen*. The point is methodological: the parallel disciplines can be understood only once the model discipline, pure formal logic, is itself in clear view.

The third paragraph states the program. Following the parallels of logic and ethics back to the parallel of the underlying *Akt-* and *Vernunftarten* (act- and reason-kinds) — *urteilende Vernunft* (judging reason) on one side, *praktische Vernunft* on the other — the thought presses itself forward that to formal logic, taken in the strict and narrow sense, there must correspond an analogously formal and likewise apriori *Praktik*. The same holds for the parallel with valuing reason, where "valuing" is meant in the widest sense and not merely the aesthetic. This yields the idea of a *formale Axiologie* (formal axiology), interwoven for essential reasons with formal Praktik, as an apriori formal discipline of values, value-contents, and value-meanings. To delimit these new formal disciplines, never conceived in the philosophical tradition, and to realize them in part — that is the chief theme of the lectures. Husserl adds the further horizon: just as formal logic has correlative to it a phenomenology and theory of formal knowledge, grounded in the fundamental structures of *Glaubensbewußtsein* (belief-consciousness, the doxic), so formal axiology and Praktik have correlative to them a phenomenology, a theory of valuing and willing, where "theory of valuing" is meant analogously to "theory of knowledge." The analogy is thus pitched at three levels at once: disciplines, act-kinds, and their phenomenological correlates.

Husserl then turns to the model discipline itself. Logic, he recalls, arose from practical needs of *Urteilsleben* (judgment-life) and its norming toward correctness and truth, and historically grew out of the struggle against the skepsis that threatened the newborn Greek science with its subjectivist excesses. Aristotle, the father of logic, founded it as a methodology of scientific knowledge — as a *Kunstlehre* (art-of-method, technical doctrine) of knowing. This Kunstlehre standpoint dominated the tradition for millennia, and most philosophers still hold that the materials treated under the title "logic" gain unity only as a Kunstlehre that seeks all the norms and prescriptions useful for guiding cognition, especially scientific cognition. With each new science the field of this methodological logic has widened, and its present aim is taken to be a general methodology of scientific knowledge together with special methodologies for the various groups of sciences. As a Kunstlehre, Husserl notes, this logic is in its theoretical foundations dependent on psychology — the concession that sets up the dialectical reversal to come.

The next paragraph performs that reversal. Husserl grants that the idea of a general, psychologically founded logical Kunstlehre, and of special Kunstlehren attached to particular sciences, is fully legitimate. But the claim that the Kunstlehre standpoint is the *only* one conferring unity has rightly been disputed. The correct formulation is this: the practical purpose of norming human cognition does indeed unite much that is theoretically heterogeneous, as Kunstlehren generally do — for they serve not the investigation of a unified subject-matter but the realization of a guiding aim. Yet if we look at the *content* of traditional, general, "formal" logic, we can strike out everything that belongs to practical purposing and with it everything empirical-psychological, and we are left with coherent groups of cognitions that reach beyond any particular science and close together into a science of their own. This science is the essential theoretical foundation of the practical Kunstlehre. The argument is the one familiar from the *Prolegomena*: the unity logic actually exhibits is a theoretical unity of content, not the external unity of a practical aim.

Husserl makes this concrete by pointing to the actual stock of logical truths. Look at the Aristotelian *Analytik* — the so-called logical principles (contradiction, excluded middle, the later-formulated principle of double negation), and the whole syllogistic down to the mathematizing logic of recent times — and all these truths display a self-enclosed and, in a certain sense, theoretical character. The thought of an empirical-practical norming of cognitive functions is foreign to them, or hangs on them as an external garment that can be stripped off at any time and apriori. They behave exactly like arithmetical truths, which often meet us in normative dress yet are plainly theoretical propositions of a theoretical science. His example: in the theoretical attitude arithmetic states (a+b)(a−b) \= a²−b²; in the practical attitude we say, to multiply a sum by a difference, form the difference of the squares. The normative turn is a mere reclothing of the theoretical proposition. Likewise "either A is b or it is not, there is no third" can be turned normatively into "you must not assent to two contradictory propositions." The same holds for the syllogistic: that from "all A are B" and "all B are C" follows "all A are C" is a theoretical proposition containing nothing normative. And, as the *Prolegomena* showed, any drawing of psychological thoughts into the content of such propositions falsifies them. This paragraph is the hinge: it certifies that logic has a theoretical core extractable from its practical-normative wrapping.

From such propositions, taken together with all essentially kindred propositions of the syllogistic, one sees at once — provided one is not misled by psychologistic prejudice — that a peculiar scientific field opens up whose natural delimitation is an important, indeed a philosophically important, task. One thereby rises to the idea of a *reine*, specifically *formale Logik*: a quite peculiar apriori discipline, sharply separated from all other actual and possible sciences and yet related to them all, since apriori every possible science is a possible field of application for it. The phrase "pure and formal" carries the whole weight: pure because freed of the empirical-psychological, formal because indifferent to any determinate subject-matter.

Husserl now specifies the first, narrower idea: a *Logik der Apophansis* (apophantic logic), a science of *Sätze* (propositions) and judgments in general. He equates Satz and Urteil and immediately purifies the term. "Satz" here does not mean the grammatical sentence but the ideal-identical *Bedeutung* (meaning) of declarative sentences, however it comes to empirical-linguistic expression in German, Chinese, or any other tongue. In this sense we all speak of the one self-same Pythagorean theorem, whatever language clothes it. The Satz so understood is an ideal-identical unity, insensitive to who happens to judge and to whether the judgers are humans, angels, or other beings. When logic speaks of judgments rather than propositions, it does not mean judgment-experiences in the psychological sense, as they occur empirically in animals of the species *homo*; nor is "judgment" a general concept individualized in judging acts; it lies in the judged *Was* (what), the meaning of the assertoric sentence. What I mean in judging, what I assert in asserting, is the Satz, the meaning of the assertion. This is the *Prolegomena* doctrine of the ideality of meaning, restated as the foundation of apophantic logic.

Apophantic logic, then, is directed upon Satz or logical judgment in this ideal sense, and can be grasped as a science of ideal meanings. At its lower level it is a science that sets up the possible *Satzformen* (propositional forms) belonging apriori to the idea of meaning, regardless of whether the propositions are truths — a *reine Formenlehre der Sätze* (pure form-theory of propositions). Upon this builds, as a higher level, a *Geltungslehre* (theory of validity), which investigates the validity-laws grounded apriori in the apriori-possible propositional forms. This proper apophantic logic thus treats the truth and falsity of propositions, but on the basis of their mere form, and to it attaches the doctrine of the modalizations of truth — the formal theory of possibility and probability. The "formal" generality of this logic consists in there being no talk of any determinate *Sachsphäre* (subject-sphere) to which actual propositions might refer. The two-storey architecture — form-theory beneath, validity-theory above — will be the template for the parallel formal disciplines of value and will.

Husserl contrasts this with the material sciences. What holds for determinate things and for objects of determinate domains is the task of material sciences — astronomy, philology, physics, geology — and into these belong all assertions about geologically, philologically, physically determinate propositions. Logic, by contrast, speaks of propositions in general; its formal generality leaves the *termini* of propositions, through which propositions refer to their determinate subject-spheres, in indeterminate generality, just as arithmetic leaves the units of counting indeterminate and algebra speaks of propositions about numbers in general by way of letters. In application the letters are replaced by determinate numbers. Apophantic logic is thus, as it were, an algebra of propositions, and as with any algebra the determining factor is the mere *Bildungsform* (formation-form), here of propositions. This makes intelligible the universality of formal logic's bearing on every actual and possible determinate science: each determinate science, in every step, stands under the formal laws logic states for propositions and truths in general on the basis of their mere form. The basic concepts figuring in the immediate axioms of formal logic Husserl calls the *Bedeutungskategorien* (meaning-categories); formal logic is the apriori discipline grounded in them and containing nothing psychological.

The next paragraph opens the correlation that will widen the whole conception. The Bedeutungskategorien are essentially interwoven with the *formale gegenständliche Kategorien* (formal object-categories). It is apriori clear that every proposition refers to objects and that every possible object is the object of possible true propositions; here the category *Satz* and the category *Gegenstand* (object) are set in an essential relation. Likewise predicate-meaning, relation-meaning, connection-meaning are formal Bedeutungskategorien essentially connected with concepts like property, relation, and so on. The interweaving is such that every valid proposition about truths — every apophantic-logical proposition — can also be regarded as, or converted into, a *formal-ontologischer Satz* (formal-ontological proposition). The apophantic and the ontological are two faces of one formal apriori.

Pursuing this, Husserl draws the consequence that it would not be good to separate formal logic and *formale Ontologie* (formal ontology). Once the thought of formal ontology is grasped, all the propositions it spans must be recognized as one in essence with the logical ones, and the idea of a formal logic is thereby extraordinarily widened. One comes finally to see that the disciplines standing under the ill-fitting titles analysis, number-theory, and *Mannigfaltigkeitslehre* (theory of manifolds) — in short, the whole of mathematics, insofar as it excludes all materially determinate concepts — belong here and close together with certain original holdings of traditional logic into one essentially unified body of science, unfolding into many special disciplines. The talk of a "formal" ontology is to be understood just as the earlier talk of a formal theory of propositions: as that one treats of propositions in general and their validity, so this treats of objects in general.

Husserl then specifies the correlative definition. *Gegenstand überhaupt* (object in general) is to be defined, correlatively to the Bedeutungskategorie Satz, as anything that can occur as the subject of a true assertion. From this original category "object" derive further formal categories — *Sachverhalt* (state of affairs), *Beschaffenheit* (constitution), relation, connection, plurality or set, cardinal number, series, ordinal number, magnitude. All truths grounded apriori in such formal object-concepts make up the content of formal ontology. The realm is thus generated systematically from a single root category by correlation with the apophantic side.

Because of this correlation, Husserl continues, it is indispensable — as already in the *Logische Untersuchungen* — to take the idea of formal logic so broadly that it spans the whole apriori grounded in both kinds of categories. He notes that Leibniz, in his scattered thoughts on a *mathesis universalis*, seems to have had this same discipline in view, only here determined more deeply, which is why Husserl likes to borrow the Leibnizian expression for it. The historical reference is not ornamental: it certifies that the unified formal science is a recovered and clarified old idea, not an invention, and it foreshadows the claim that formal axiology and Praktik are likewise recoverable apriori disciplines that the tradition glimpsed but never conceived.

The relation to all possible sciences holds of itself for the formal-ontological propositions as well. Every science has a domain, has its objects; every material science has its sphere of objects bounded by a material concept. But formal ontology, investigating the apriori lying in the formal idea "object in general" apart from all material particularity, finds possible application in every conceivable science. There can be no science, for instance, in which objects could not be grouped and counted, so that the formal-ontological theory of sets and numbers must necessarily be applicable in every scientific sphere. Universality of application is thus a mark of the formal as such — a feature the value-disciplines will be required to match if the parallel is to hold.

Husserl closes the section by securing the autonomy of apophantic logic within this union and adding a third correlation. Despite the essential unification of apophantic logic and formal ontology, which demands a comprehensive science embracing both, it remains clear that apophantic logic — as a science of the forms of possible propositions and of the laws of possible truths on the basis of mere pure form — can be grasped for itself and delimited in its peculiar idea; and it is this discipline that will especially concern the following inquiries. He adds a note that anticipates the noetic dimension: in virtue of the correlation between *Urteilen* (judging) and *Urteil* (the judgment "passed" in judging), every apophantic-logical proposition can be turned into a formal *noetischer Satz* (noetic proposition), one that makes an apriori statement about the formal correctness or incorrectness of judging. So the model discipline already shows three correlative faces — apophantic, ontological, noetic — and the reader is to expect the value-disciplines to display the same threefold structure.

### **§ 2\. The Opposition between Ethical Empiricism and Absolutism**

Husserl now crosses over to ethics, and the crossing is structured to mirror § 1\. Ethics too arose historically as a normative and practical discipline, and the practical standpoint has remained dominant in its treatment. This is intelligible from the practical motives that have always occasioned ethical reflection and made the cultivation of an ethical discipline appear, again and again, an important practical desideratum. The opening move is deliberately the same as for logic: name the discipline's practical origin first, so that the theoretical core can later be extracted from it.

He fills in the phenomenon the practical discipline answers to. We accompany our own conduct and that of our fellows with constant appraisals — right and wrong, expedient and inexpedient, rational and irrational, moral and immoral. As in the theoretical domain, here too there is dispute and conflict of appraisal. We fall out with ourselves, disapprove now what we approved before, find ourselves in painful conflict with others, or are called as judges to settle the quarrels of others. Unlike theoretical conflict, this conflict generally stirs no theoretical interest but moves the *Gemüt* (heart, the emotive life) all the more. Our whole weal and woe often hangs on such stances and their agreement or disagreement, for what is at stake is self-respect and the respect of others. So for everyone who strives higher the question becomes burning: how shall I order my life and striving rationally, escape the tormenting discord with myself and the justified censure of others, and attain — in the traditional phrase — true *Eudaimonie*, true happiness? Husserl thus locates ethics' practical urgency in the affective stakes of self-assessment, not in disinterested curiosity.

These questions, once they become the focus of reflection, lead first to an ethics as a practical discipline. Husserl stresses "first," because in modern times the effort presses ever more urgently to set alongside this practical ethics, which does not disown its empirical-anthropological character, an apriori ethics: a system of absolute and pure principles of practical reason that, free of all relation to empirical man and his empirical circumstances, are nonetheless to supply absolutely normative measures for all human action, whether formal or material. The analogy with the situation in logic leaps to the eye. Here too one will not deny the usefulness, even necessity, of a Kunstlehre — of rational action — but will hold that its essential theoretical foundations lie not in a psychology of the cognitive and emotive functions but in certain apriori laws and theories called by their very sense to function as reason-norms for all ethical as for all logical appraisal, the lodestars of every rational practice.

Husserl gives the idealist's formulation. Just as the idea of truth cannot be drawn from the psychology of knowledge, so the idea of moral goodness and of practical rightness in general cannot be drawn from the psychology of the emotive and practical functions: from facts no ideas can be pressed out. And the moment we strike the ideas out on both sides, the normative and practical disciplines traditionally called logic and ethics lose their peculiar core and sense. He adds a historical note that confirms the parallel: the idea of an apriori ethical doctrine of principles emerges at the threshold of the modern age in connection with the renewal of Platonism, namely in the Cambridge School, and the current of intellectualist ethics issuing from it likes to parallelize ethics and pure mathematics — a parallelization still acknowledged in Locke. This is the same parallel Husserl is reconstructing, now traced to its early-modern source.

The opposing party is empiricist ethics, which turns against ethical apriorism — an apriorism that Kant too represents, in his own way, in the critical period. Husserl makes an important methodological stipulation here. The opposition between pure and empirical ethics has historically been cast in the misleading form of an opposition between *Verstandesethik* (intellectualist ethics) and *Gefühlsethik* (ethics of feeling); but it is best to set aside, at first, the question of the *origin* of ethical concepts — whether they spring from the intellectual or the emotive sphere — and to grasp the opposition exclusively as that between apriori and empirical ethics. By apriori ethics is meant a discipline that, in the manner of pure mathematics, precedes all experience and yet prescribes its norm. As pure arithmetic fixes the laws grounded in the pure essence of number and valid in unconditioned generality — laws that may not be violated if the numbers posited and computed in empirical counting are really to be numbers — so the pure ethical laws are to stand with respect to the pure correlates of the ethical concepts, the rational resolves and actions. As pure arithmetic is the essential foundation of the practical art of reckoning, so a pure ethics would be the essential foundation of an art or Kunstlehre of rational human action. The idea of a pure ethics thus offers itself, in analogy with pure logic and pure arithmetic. On the opposing side stands ethical empiricism, as psychologism or biologism, which refers everything the apriorist claims as pure principle back to the particularity of human nature and the human life of feeling and will, and so admits ethics only as a technology leaning on psychology and biology. The deferral of the origin question is strategic: it lets Husserl run the refutation of skepticism formally, before any commitment about whether values are felt or understood.

The dispute, like its logical parallel, touches the highest philosophical interests. As the consequence of logical psychologism and anthropologism leads to theoretical skepticism, so the consequence of ethical anthropologism leads to ethical skepticism — which means the surrender of the genuinely unconditioned validity of ethical requirements, the denial of any really obligating duty. Concepts like "good" and "evil," "practically rational" and "irrational," become mere expressions of empirical-psychological facts of human nature as it happens to be, as it has formed itself culturally under the contingent circumstances of human development and, further back, biologically in the evolution of the species in the struggle for existence. On the empiricist's view they express no absolute ideas carrying their general ought-sense for every willing and feeling being, of whatever world, actual or consistently conceivable. Accordingly all ethical norms have merely factual validity: "ethical norms hold" means that humans feel themselves factually obligated thus and so, feel from psychologically causal grounds a certain inner urge to behave practically in a certain way and thereby escape an otherwise unavoidable discomfort. It is a matter of biological usefulness that something like a conscience-function has developed, a way of appraising actions, dispositions, and characters under the categories good and evil.

Husserl extends the empiricist picture to its temporal horizon. We do not know how further development will run; it may be that one day this conscience-function proves biologically superfluous, that it atrophies and is replaced by another that, for all the commonality of form, has divergent principles — so that good and evil, say, exchange their positions against each other. The point is the radical contingency the empiricist must accept: nothing in the content of "good" is fixed beyond the accidents of a developmental history.

He draws the metaphysical consequence and names the position. With this, these and all absolute ideas and ideals lose the metaphysical significance for total reality ascribed to them by the idealists. Man projects his contingently and temporally grown ideas and ideals into the infinite universe, fictions for himself an absolute reason as the ultimate teleological principle of being, and feels himself secure in his status as a child of God. This hypostatization and absolutization of ideas biologically useful to him may itself have a biological value, perhaps even a biological usefulness of especially high dignity; but to take the hypostatization seriously would be to engage in *Begriffsmythologie* (concept-mythology). Here as everywhere, Husserl says — speaking with Vaihinger — the true philosophy is to be the philosophy of the "As-if": it is good to proceed practically as if these fictions had absolute validity, but theoretically one must be clear that everything is only relative, only anthropological, only valid in a thought-economical and conservation-economical sense, only relatively useful. Husserl is here stating the skeptic's position in its strongest and most self-aware form — the position the rest of the section, and the next, will undertake to refute from within.

### **§ 3\. The Anti-Ethical Consequences of Ethical Empiricism**

This dispute, Husserl continues, evidently touches the highest *practical* interests as well. The skeptical theory demands a skeptical praxis, and that means an anti-ethical praxis. However eagerly the ethical pragmatists are bent on denying just this, they can deny it only by letting go of real consequence. The section poses the test question: granting strict consistency in theory and practice, should it really make no difference whether one professes, as empiricist, skeptical relativism, or, as idealist, ethical absolutism? The strategy is to show that the two theories, consistently lived, issue in opposite practices — and thereby that the empiricist theory is self-undermining as a guide to life.

Husserl works the first horn. Suppose the agent stands on the ground of ethical absolutism: he believes in a Good-in-itself, an absolutely valid good that holds in the case at hand because it conforms to an idea valid apriori — independently of all facticity of the willing and acting subjects, their contingent psychophysical constitution, and so on. Suppose this idea, with all its components, is evidently knowable in its absolute validity, and likewise the absolute principles grounded in its pure essence, and likewise, for the empirical individual case, the extent to which an action conforms to these principles or runs counter to their pure sense. Then the agent knows that he is absolutely bound — bound in his practical conduct to the ethical principles as firmly as, in reckoning, to the arithmetical. As the reckoner tells himself that, so long as the sense of 2 and 4 is held fast, 2 × 2 \= 4 regardless of whether it is biologically useful to think so — it may even be biologically useful to reckon falsely, but false reckoning stays false reckoning and is made true by no biological usefulness, for the identical sense prescribes the law — so the sense of the ethical and practical ideas, if there are such (as the absolutist is convinced), prescribes principles. Good stays good, bad stays bad. So long as the sense of such concepts is held fast, everything holds that belongs purely to this sense and can be given up only by giving up the sense. And this validity is not theoretical but practical. As to truth — including ethical truth — belongs theoretical acknowledgment in belief, so to the good belongs valuing approval and, where called for, willing and practical realization; to the bad, disapproval — on both sides in an ideal, not an empirical sense. A judging that acknowledges the non-true as true is incorrect and at once worthless; an acting that realizes the non-good is incorrect, irrational, and at once worthless. To see the practically good fully and wholly, to have it clearly before one's eyes as something, where applicable, commanded, is — at least in the moment of insight — to turn toward it willingly. And to be firmly convinced that in all action an absolute norm is judge is already, in a general way, to tend the will toward the good. This does not, of course, guarantee actual and consistent execution; but it fixes the direction consistency imposes on the absolutist.

The second horn stands quite otherwise. For one who draws the consequences of skeptical anthropologism, talk of a Good-in-itself is mere prejudice. Why, on this conviction, should we let ourselves be determined by the so pretentious modes of valuation that make so much of themselves under the titles good and evil and yet express only contingencies of human formation and development? Why should we not, when we feel like it, set ourselves against the feelings stemming from these sources, and show the much-praised conscience — which is, after all, only the voice of historical instincts — our subjective superiority? Why let ourselves be chained by it? Let us do what suits us and prove ourselves free. If the environment reacts, fear of such reactions may factually compel us, but perhaps it suits us to defy a world of suffering. If told this is foolish, we answer that there is here no absolute idea of folly any more than of reason — otherwise one would inconsistently stand at this point on absolutist ground. Concealment of our action may, where applicable, protect us from dangers. If biological usefulness is invoked, the skeptic may answer: certainly, that is precisely his principle, the good is the biologically useful, which is why this mode of valuation prevailed and entrenched itself in feeling; but what if it now suits us to do the biologically useless, even the harmful? Perhaps we care nothing for our life, nothing for the whole of biology, for the existence of the animal and human world. Then it stands free to us to act as we please. One will surely not come again with the objection that doing the harmful is irrational, that acting against one's self-preservation and that of the whole human community is foolish, blameworthy, and the like — for what would "foolish," "irrational" mean here? The skeptic's replies are made to expose that, on his own premises, every reason-laden reproach is unavailable to him.

Husserl draws the conclusion and sharpens the contrast with a careful qualification. It is clear that the empiricist, hence anti-ethical, theory demands an anti-ethical praxis just as the idealist theory demands a genuinely ethical praxis. The ethical idealist (absolutist), if consistent, cannot but follow his reason; he cannot help striving, fully consciously, to order his life by ethical measures, thereby choosing what on his conviction a practical reason requires of him as the one and absolutely right thing. The ethical subjectivist, by contrast, rejects every practical reason and thereby every obligation that comes forward with a claim to reason. One cannot strictly say that he *must* act immorally, in the manner of moral evil; for whoever does not recognize a genuine — that is, absolute — validity of moral requirements can have no consciousness of transgressing. The Ovidian "video meliora proboque, deteriora sequor" (I see the better and approve it, yet follow the worse) cannot occur in him. From the absolutist standpoint one would therefore say: the ethical empiricist can act only ethically erroneously, not evilly — but, for that very reason, also not ethically good in the sense opposed to evil. Both require insight and the practical tendency toward the good. The evil one acts in defiance of this insight, doing violence to the practical tendency given with it; the good one, where he acts morally, acts consciously yielding to it. This is why Husserl spoke earlier of the "anti-ethical": the skeptic's praxis falls outside the good/evil axis altogether, and with this the whole mode of esteeming one's fellows and the so-called ideals of humanity changes — for what could reverence or admiration mean for the skeptic, what the talk of the highest values of personality?

The dispute has its analogue in the logical sphere. To the question of the objective and absolute validity of ethical norms corresponds the question of the absolute validity of logical norms; the dispute over the idea of a Good-in-itself has its parallel in the dispute over the idea of Truth-in-itself; the dispute over the relation of the ethical Kunstlehre to psychology, its parallel in the dispute over the relation of the logical Kunstlehre likewise to psychology. The parallelism is now exact at the meta-level: the very structure of the skeptic/absolutist controversy is shared by the two domains.

Husserl reconstructs the psychologistic case so as to meet it at full strength. Psychologism draws ever-new force, on both sides, from the same arguments, which at first strike us as self-evident and seem entirely compelling. First, the argument from foundation: scientifically grounded norms for a psychic activity presuppose scientific knowledge of that activity, so rules of thinking and rules of willing rest, as a matter of course, on a psychology of thinking and a psychology of willing; the essential foundations of ethics therefore lie in psychology. Second, the argument from the origin of the normative basic concepts: all concepts arise by abstraction from intuitions of concrete individual cases; the logical basic concepts — truth, falsity, concept, judgment, inference, proof — evidently arise in the domain of judgment, the psychology of the intellect; to grasp what truth means I must go back to judgments, to grasp what inference means I must transport myself into an inferring; and likewise with good and bad I must go back to certain emotive activities, to approvals and disapprovals bearing on volitions or dispositions, while virtue and vice are plainly names for psychic dispositions. On both sides, then, I am led back to the psychic, and psychology appears as the foundation for logic and ethics alike. Husserl states these as the strongest arguments precisely because his refutation will not deny the psychological genesis but the psychologistic conclusion drawn from it.

Absolutism, conversely, looks to the consequences of the psychologistic doctrine and seeks in their untenabilities strong arguments for its own standpoint. In the ethical case this proceeds by considerations of the kind already run through: it is shown that an anti-ethical theory would have an anti-ethical praxis as its consequence. One may try, in similar fashion, to show that the parallel denial of the absolute validity of the logical would demand an anti-logical praxis. If the logical relativist — the psychologist and biologist — is consistent, he would have to surrender all the sciences; scientific striving would lose its proper goal.

Husserl spells out the logical version. If logical rightness or validity means nothing more than that our human nature is factually so constituted, and has so grown under the given biological conditions, that we humans must approve certain principles, theorems, and proofs as "scientific" and disapprove others as "unscientific," then further development may bring changes, and it may be that humans then behave in their logical valuations just oppositely to now. To speak of a really objectively valid truth would then have no sense; to set its discovery as a goal, to strive for knowledge of how things are in truth, would be a chimerical, a practically senseless goal.

Yet, Husserl insists, one can and must grasp the arguments from consequences in another, sharper and deeper way. It is of considerable interest to go back to the ultimate, root *Widersinn* (countersense, absurdity) that attaches to skepticism. In particular it is of great interest to see whether and how far what has already been achieved against logical skepticism in antiquity can really be achieved, with equal compelling force, against ethical skepticism. Whoever knows the Platonic and Aristotelian analyses of sophistic subjectivism and skepticism comes to the insight that every logical negativism cancels itself through countersense — and this quite apart from all praxis. This marks the decisive turn from the argument-from-consequences to the argument-from-self-cancellation, the strategy of § 4\.

Husserl closes by raising precisely the doubt that the analogy must overcome. One might doubt whether an argument from practical consequences — a deriving of anti-ethical praxis from ethical negativism — amounts to an equally strong refutation, or whether something like a countersense emerges in it. A *practical* countersense? But what is that? Is not countersense something theoretical, a contradiction, a material incompatibility? Is the "practical countersense" at bottom merely a matter of unpleasant, fatal consequences before which we recoil, against which our feeling rears up? But feelings can prove nothing; and who says other living beings are not equipped with other feelings, so that the very same consequences might seem quite agreeable to them? In any case the method of analogy that Husserl means to follow requires that one begin just here, and through exact analysis seek to establish whether and how far the peculiar self-cancellation of logical skepticism really has an analogue in a self-cancellation of the ethical, and wherein this self-cancellation consists. The doubt is left standing as the precise task of the next section: to find, if it exists, a genuinely *practical* countersense.

### **§ 4\. The Refutation of Skepticism and Psychologism**

#### **(a) The Cancellation of Logical Skepticism through its Formal Countersense**

Husserl establishes the logical model that the ethical analogy must match. The various special forms of relativistic anthropologism do not matter at first; he ties on to the oldest and simplest. Protagoras: man, the individual in his individual state, is the measure of all things; true for each is what appears true to him, so everything anyone asserts as appearing to him is true. Gorgias: nothing is true, and if there were a truth it would not be knowable. These are the limit cases skepticism gives itself.

Taken as extremely as Plato and Aristotle took them, the refutation runs in the familiar and fully satisfying way. The subjectivist puts up a thesis and grounds it; in doing so he raises the claim that what he asserts really holds and that his grounding really grounds — and this claim is meant to exclude the opposing opinions about the cognitive situation. The skeptic may personally declare that he raises no claim; it does not help. For inseparably belonging to the sense of his assertion, and of every assertion and grounding whatever, is that there is objective truth and knowledge of objective truth, and that *not* whatever appears true to each is true. But the very opposite makes up the content of the skeptic's theses. There is thus a conflict, seen in full evidence, between what the skeptic asserts and purports to ground and what every thesis and grounding as such, whatever its content, presupposes by its general sense. In this consists, as Plato already recognized, the characteristic *Widersinn* of skepticism. The decisive feature is that the refutation is not empirical or rhetorical but reads off a conflict internal to the sense of assertion itself.

This type of Widersinn, suitably generalized, characterizes a whole class of theories skeptical in a wider sense, as worked out in the first volume of the *Logische Untersuchungen*. If a thesis is grounded by a theory of a certain logical type, and the validity of that theory as a theory of that type presupposes precisely what the thesis denies, then the theory cancels itself through Widersinn just as extreme skepticism does. So: skeptical are all theses and theories that deny any conditions of the meaningful possibility of truth in general, of theory in general; skeptical are deductive theories for theses that deny what deductive theory itself requires to be meaningful, inductive theories for theses that deny what inductive theory requires, and in general any theory for a thesis that denies what theories of its own logical type require. The criterion is formal and self-referential: a theory is skeptical when its content undercuts its own conditions of sense.

Husserl gives the worked example of the psychologistic experience-theories of Hume's school. Their type is this: the principles under which the validity of all experiential inferences stands — hence on which the validity of all experiential sciences depends — lack rationality; they cannot be seen as necessities but only grounded psychologically, as mere indications of features of human nature expressed in the laws of association and habit. Such theories try to show in detail how habitual judgment-tendencies grow up in the play of associative forces, finally a blind judgment-compulsion toward the content of those experience-principles, and how this blind compulsion is confused with the genuine rational necessity that is here entirely lacking.

All theories of this kind, Husserl says, are skeptical in his sense and cancel themselves through the Widersinn of the indicated type. For in building such theories the empiricist supports himself on psychology and treats it as a really valid science. If psychology holds, it must itself be rationally grounded; if it cannot be rationally grounded, it cannot hold and cannot serve as the basis of a valid theory. But the thesis the empiricist theory wants to ground is precisely that experience-principles, and so experiential sciences in general, are irrational, lacking generally rational validity. Presupposed, however, is the rational validity of psychology, which is itself an experiential science. He restates the point sharply: Hume's theory rests on psychology; if psychology is without rationally insightful grounds, so is Hume's theory; but if the theory claims to be rational, to ground rationally that no experiential science is rational, it presupposes that in the special case of psychology what it aims to prove invalid in unconditioned generality does not hold — and there is the pure Widersinn.

One might think this is a peculiarity of Hume's reduction of the causal law to habit. But the same Widersinn lies — however little most psychologists are aware of it — in every psychologistic interpretation of the logical, and especially in every general dissolution of logic into psychology, of the kind popular since Mill and Beneke: that psychology furnishes the essential theoretical foundation of logic, that logical laws are at bottom nothing but psychological laws. Here the demonstration of skeptical Widersinn is relatively easy. If the logical laws, including those of the Aristotelian *Analytik*, are essentially referred to human nature and express peculiarities of the human psychic constitution, then they have only temporary validity and themselves fall into the flux of development — as extreme psychologists with the laudable courage of consequence have expressly admitted. But every assertion, insofar as it raises a claim to truth, presupposes the law of contradiction. It says "so it is," and belonging to its sense is that thereby "it is not so" is excluded. That a "so it is" excludes the corresponding "so it is not" — this and no more is what the law of contradiction states; and so without its validity every truth-claiming assertion loses its sense. Hence the psychologistic conception is widersinnig: to say it is possible that the law of contradiction will someday no longer hold is as much as to say it is possible that the law does not hold at all, and any such proposition is widersinnig because by its sense it presupposes the law. All the truths the biologistic theory rests on are truths only insofar as the law of contradiction holds, since that law states nothing but what belongs to the sense of truth as such.

So Plato's refutation of extreme sophistic skepticism can be suitably reshaped and transferred to all forms of empiricism and psychologism. However much they seek to dazzle with the imposing apparatus of the newest psychology, biology, and sociology, it avails nothing: the Widersinn cancels them. On logical ground, then, one can bring to evidence in the strongest form the necessity of an absolutism and soon also of an idealism — one can make it evident that every opposing conception cancels itself through Widersinn, and through the most universal Widersinn, the formal. This is the result the ethical inquiry must now try to match.

#### **(b) The Analogue of the Skeptical Countersense in the Practical Sphere: the Practical Countersense**

Husserl poses the question directly: can one proceed so in ethics, discrediting ethical skepticism first in its coarser and then in its finer forms? There is a strong way of fighting it from the consequences, already discussed; but it exhibits no theoretical Widersinn, only weighs the unacceptable practical consequences. And one might object that it thereby appeals to the *Gemüt*: it presupposes that we care about morality, that we are at bottom convinced a practical reason makes evident to us the difference of right and wrong, duty and sin, and demands right action of us with insight. When it is then shown that extreme ethical skepticism, and also the finer psychologistic skepticism, leads practically to an absolute amoralism, this appears to us inadmissible and we recoil before the consequence.

But, Husserl grants, a theory that so confuses a person that he no longer understands the voice of conscience and loses interest in realizing the high values he is called to realize is a sad theory, wholly unacceptable — and yet the theory may prejudice our Gemüt against itself through its practical consequences, and may at the same time appear false to us, without being on that account *formally* widersinnig. The formal Widersinn that lies in theories denying, in thesi, conditions of the possibility of truth or theory in general cannot come to light here, because in the ethical-skeptical negation no such conditions are denied. This is the precise gap the consequence-argument leaves open, and it is why a feeling-based recoil cannot count as a refutation.

Husserl examines and rejects Münsterberg's attempt (in the *Philosophie der Werte*, and earlier in the *Grundzüge der Psychologie*) to construct an ethical argument parallel to Plato's. Münsterberg argues: whoever asserts that there is no duty unconditionally binding him wants, by this assertion — which is itself an action — to reach a goal, namely the hearer's acknowledgment of the moral denial. But the hearer who would trust the skeptic must doubt from the outset whether the asserter even feels obligated to express his real conviction; so the skeptic can expect no belief and undertakes an action whose goal he makes unreachable by his own deed.

Husserl cannot accept this. The skeptic asserts, and the hearer may ask whether he can trust the speaker as to truthfulness. Here as elsewhere he will reasonably trust the speaker if he finds no motives suggesting probable untruthfulness, or, conversely, finds motives that positively speak for truthfulness in the given case. There is no reason such grounds of trust should be excluded when the speaker declares that on his conviction there is no unconditional duty, and no reason the skeptic could not reasonably expect to be trusted in such an assertion. We believe even notorious liars when we have grounds to suppose it would now much suit the liar to speak truly; and the skeptic is far from being a liar. He has the theoretical conviction that there is no duty in the genuine sense and wants to communicate it — not necessarily because he wants to fulfill a duty by doing so. Nothing in the fact of communication implies that the communicator follows, or must follow, a duty; perhaps it merely gives him pleasure to express himself, to instruct, to show others his superiority. Münsterberg's argument thus locates the Widersinn in the wrong place — in the speech-act's pragmatic presuppositions rather than in the sense of what is demanded.

Münsterberg's attempt having miscarried, Husserl asks whether there is nonetheless a way to refute the ethical-skeptical thesis by exhibiting a Widersinn analogous to the logical one — and answers that there is parallel Widersinn and parallel refutation. To gain it, one must take, instead of theoretical propositions, *demands*, ought-propositions. "There is no Good-in-itself," "there is no unconditional duty," "there is no reason in action" are assertoric, theoretical propositions. But a demand says: thus you ought to act, do your duty, act rationally. Such propositions — not mere theoretical assertions but practical demands — ethics utters insofar as it would be a practical discipline offering us norms of action. The ethical skeptic can avoid the formal Widersinn so long as he asserts theoretically that there is no duty. The situation grows more dangerous for him when he utters *demands* — when he denies ethics, or any essential conditions of the possibility of an ethics as a regulative art of action, while at the same time remaining a practical ethicist who utters demands and rules of action. Even here no contradiction need appear if the skepsis is not unconditional and general but limited to a particular sphere of praxis: one who understands ethics as morality and confines moral action to a special group of actions — say those bearing on assumed original motives of neighbor-love — falls into no formal Widersinn if he denies ethical duties with respect to them, rejecting demands that come forward with the pretension of absolute validity, while still continuing to utter demands of his own.

The parallel with logic holds exactly. A skeptic about the possibilities of experiential knowledge as knowledge of external reality can avoid all Widersinn so long as he is not a skeptic about knowledge in general. Hume fell into no Widersinn by contesting the rational justification of experiential-scientific knowledge; he fell into it because he at the same time gave a *psychological* theory of this knowledge which, as itself experiential-scientific, presupposed the possibility of experiential-scientific knowledge. He held fast to the objective and unconditionally objective validity of analytic logic and the corresponding analytic knowledge — to the objective validity of knowledge in the sense of "relations between ideas" — and thereby even distinguished himself favorably from the later empiricists à la Mill. Whoever, however, dissolves analytic thinking psychologistically and becomes a skeptic in this sphere cannot avoid the Widersinn, because the analytic principles, like the law of contradiction, imply the most universal conditions of the possibility of valid thinking in general. Whether we think analytically or empirically, insofar as we assert, insofar as we utter propositions raising any claim to truth, we are bound to the self-evident laws that belong unsublatably to the sense of truth in general; the principles of the special domain of analytic thinking are conditions of the possibility of every valid thinking, including the experiential-scientific, since this too must finally move in predications.

It is similar in ethics. We may be especially interested in a morality in some restricted sense, but we must observe that moral willing and acting is first of all simply willing and acting, and that if differences of rational justification belong to the moral sphere, these differences must first be pursued in the most general sphere. We cannot do scientific ethics without having first raised the most general question of reason in the practical and sought the principles of practical reason in general as formal reason-principles of praxis. What is rational action at all, and what are the principles under which rational action as such stands, whether or not it is judged moral? Not everywhere is morality at issue: if I waver whether to listen of an evening to a shallow farce or to Beethoven's sublime *Eroica*, I may decide rationally for the latter, but no conscience-question in the moral sense is present. If we give ethics its natural boundaries, then, we must grasp it as the Kunstlehre of action that is rational in respect of both form and matter. Such an ethics will of itself investigate not only, as to form, what belongs to the general sense of rationality, but also, as to matter, the various orders or levels of practical values, and will seek to determine the action that is rational in the highest degree and its appertaining rules. With the analogy to logic suitably established, it is easy to see that the difference between formal and material ethics must then be found, and that the genuine skeptical Widersinn in the practical sphere shows itself in demands that express themselves with the sense of a rational demand while at the same time, in their content, generally surrendering rationality itself.

Husserl analyzes the force of the imperative "Recognize this as a rule\!" To follow a rule does not mean to act so that one's action merely conforms to the rule — so that a comparative survey of one's actions would show them all to have a certain general type, the way natural events fall under a natural law — but to act so that one *lets oneself be led* by the rule, obeys it. The demand can be meant merely suggestively, as when the hypnotist utters a general demand to his medium, or the master speaks to a slave reared in blind obedience, or one addresses the not-yet-rational child. But "Recognize this rule\!" means more than this suggestive operation: recognize the rational validity of this practical rule, recognize that to act thus is right action, recognize that accordingly to let oneself be willingly led by this rule is rational — and more still: actually let yourself be so led. As the commander conceives the one under his command-will as one who will translate the command into deed, and for the command's sake, so in the sense of every practical demand lies the expectation that the demand will work motivatingly toward the corresponding action precisely through the addressee's having understood it. The point isolates the *rational* claim built into a genuine (non-suggestive) practical demand, which is what the skeptic's own demand cannot consistently make.

He then runs the negative cases. "Recognize no rule as valid for your action\!" — how does it stand? To say "Do not recognize a certain rule\!" means: recognize that it is not rationally binding, do not let yourself be positively determined by it, do not take it as a rule. But that itself contains a rule: to see, on the one hand, that to act as that rule demands is not rational action, and, on the other, that the contradictorily opposed will — not to bind oneself, in the relevant group of actions, to that rule — is rational, so that the negative will-rule of not proceeding by the original rule is itself rationally to be recognized. Now "Recognize no practical rule\!" contains the rule, to be recognized as rational, that no practical rule at all is rational, that every will bound by any rule is irrational, that the will to follow no rule is the rational one. Yet in saying "Recognize no practical rule\!" I utter a practical rule — one whose recognition would be rational and the practical following of which would be right; that lies in the sense of every rule of this form, every more-than-suggestive rule. And in its content this rule sets up as practically irrational just what the rule by its sense presupposes as rational. There we have the exact analogue of the skeptical Widersinn.

Husserl tests the construction against a tempting misformulation in the logical case. What kind of Widersinn attaches to the rule "Recognize no logical rule as binding for your judging activity\!" — to which must be added, "insofar as you want to reach the goal of truth with it"? One cannot here argue: every rule contains an assertion about an ought, every assertion presupposes the law of contradiction, therefore the rule cancels itself. For the rule denies no logical rule as a logical *truth* (and asserts nothing about one either); it says only that, rationally, no logical truth should normatively determine truth-striving as a rule. The completion is rather: if the law of contradiction holds — as belonging to the sense of every assertion, including the ought-assertion — then it would be irrational not to recognize it practically as a judgment-rule, for "Do not bind yourself to it\!" would mean "Disregard it\!", which is evidently perverse, since contradictory judging would then not be excluded from the will-goal, whereas the will-goal aims precisely to recognize true judging exclusively. Purely logically no practical rule can cancel itself; such a rule cancels itself through a *practical* Widersinn, which may indeed connect with a logical one, but no more than connect. The practical countersense is thus a genuinely distinct species, parallel to but not reducible to the theoretical — exactly the result the doubt at the end of § 3 demanded.

#### **(c) Recapitulation. The Analogy between Skeptical Assertion and Skeptical Demand**

Returning after the long Pentecost break, Husserl resets the context. The task was to carry through the analogies between pure ethics and pure logic, between ethical and logical idealism or absolutism. The dispute over the idea of a Good-in-itself, of values-in-themselves, has on the whole its analogue in the dispute over a truth-in-itself, and to ethical psychologism corresponds logical-theoretical psychologism.

Following these analogies, the question is whether the refutation of psychologism can really be made as radical on the ethical-axiological field as on the logical-theoretical. On the latter the necessary critical considerations have been carried far enough: the root Widersinn that refutes psychologism in the sphere of truth from the ultimate ground has been exhibited. As to the ethical sphere, earlier lectures discussed the unacceptable practical consequences of psychologism and skeptical relativism in general, but the doubt remained whether this suffices. Does ethical and axiological psychologism, too, cancel itself through something like a Widersinn — a *practical* Widersinn? And again the question: what is a practical Widersinn? Is it merely a matter of practical consequences before which we recoil emotionally, against which our feeling rears up? But feelings refute nothing; a refutation is a theoretical matter and must manifest itself theoretically as a theoretical Widersinn. Husserl keeps the demand for rigor in view: the practical countersense must be a genuine countersense, not a disguised feeling.

He recapitulates the clarified logical situation: the way extreme relativism and skepticism cancel themselves, and the way the concept of an openly or covertly skeptical theory can be fixed by a most general type of Widersinn. Theses and their theories are extremely skeptical when they deny conditions of the meaningful possibility of truth in general, of theories in general, thereby implicitly denying what they themselves, as theses and theories, presuppose. In an extended sense, theories are skeptical when they want to prove theses that in turn deny something without which theories of the very type the given theory falls under would be senseless. As soon as reflective consideration makes evident that a theory's thesis denies this without the thesis itself losing its sense, we are done with it; it is for us definitively finished. So it stands with all psychological or biological theories that are supposed to ground the possibility that the principles of formal logic have only factual-human significance and change with the development of humanity.

With respect to logic, then, we have firm ground. However we bound the idea of logic, so long as we recognize the Aristotelian *Analytik* — the so-called formal logic, with the law of contradiction and the syllogistic principles — as its fundamentally essential core, we are sure of the objectivist, absolutist, idealist position, at least for the whole domain of formal logic and soon also for that of the *mathesis universalis*. The question is now whether one can proceed so in the ethical sphere too: whether one can refute ethical relativism, anthropologism, and psychologism in their coarser and finer forms just as forcibly and brand them with a Widersinn that is skeptical in the genuine sense.

Münsterberg's misfired attempt is recalled once more, with its premise that the ethical skeptic's assertion, being an action aimed at the hearer's acknowledgment, makes its own goal unreachable. A fleeting analysis of the psychological motives that determine the hearer's trust or mistrust regarding the speaker's truthfulness showed the argument's untenability and, above all, that no Widersinn is at issue either on the side of the speaker, who presupposes trust, or on the side of the hearer, who extends it.

To reach a better result, Husserl says, a twofold reflection is needed. First, to refute a psychologistic logic, one must above all refute the radical, wholly extreme skepticism and subjectivism that denies or implicitly cancels the objectivity of truth in general. This is a first and unconditionally necessary step, and it joins at once with the establishment of the psychology-purity, the pure ideality, of analytic logic. The principles of analytic logic are evidently demonstrable as unsublatable conditions of the possibility of a truth in general and of a deductive theory in general, and this transfers to all syllogistic laws — in short, to the whole content of formal logic, even up to the extension into formal mathesis. One then sees without further ado that psychologizing the analytic laws cancels their character of ideality and thereby of unconditioned objectivity, so that to be a psychologist with respect to formal logic is wholly equivalent to being an extreme skeptic, that is, to surrendering the objectivity of truth as such.

The analytic-logical law-sphere is the only sphere of laws of an absolutely universal validity for all conceivable spheres of being and knowledge. Laws such as that of contradiction express nothing but truths that belong unsublatably to the sense of true propositions as such. Insofar as we assert at all, about whatever propositions raise the claim to hold, we are self-evidently bound to laws that do nothing but unfold the sense of valid propositions as such, the sense of truth in general. Hence, although analytic thinking is a special domain of thinking, the principles under which its validity stands are conditions of the possibility of valid thinking in general — including, for instance, experiential-scientific thinking, since this too must move in predications. The radical refutation of logical psychologism demands, in clear natural order, first its refutation in the most general form, that of extreme skepticism; only when this is accomplished does one set about uprooting restricted forms of psychologism, for example the psychologism of an experience-logic that wants to ground the principles on which the possibility of experiential-scientific knowledge hangs through psychology, itself a particular experiential science.

If the analogy really carries as far as suspected, then a radical ethical skepticism must correspond to the radical logical one, and its radicalism will consist in denying, in the widest conceivable sphere, the objective validity of the ethical and of the principles that might delimit it together with the pure consequences contained in them. Further, a universal ethical psychologism will consist in deriving all ethical validity in this widest sphere from psychological sources; and the Widersinn that affects the extreme ethical skepticism as such will eo ipso link itself with psychologism, insofar as — corresponding to the parallel — universal psychologism and extreme skepticism in the widest ethical sphere prove equivalent.

The further steps would then consist in showing, within more narrowly bounded spheres of the ethical — bounded not arbitrarily but from principled motives — that here too psychologistic and anthropologistic theories of validity are widersinnig. Perhaps it can be shown here, as in the logical sphere, that already the consideration of the most general ideas of logical and practical validity suffices to bring out that ideality and objectivity are inseparably bound and so neither to be surrendered, so that no special analysis of psychologistic and anthropologistic theories is needed to refute them — at most to exhibit the motives of the psychologistic temptations. From there the inquiry would go on, on both sides, to the radical problems of consciousness, the epistemological and the will-theoretical problems, which are not yet in view here. Husserl is sketching the architecture of the whole course: formal refutation first, then the phenomenology of reason that grounds it.

Following these analogies, we must ask what the ethical in the widest conceivable sense is, the correlate of the logical in the widest sense. It is plainly a restriction to take ethics as morality. In every sense ethics relates to action as logic relates to thinking: as logic goes to right or rational thinking, ethics goes to right or rational action. Moral action, however we determine it more closely, is a restricted sphere of action in general; so ethics, if we want to win the most comprehensive concept, must be coordinated to reason in praxis in general. Extreme ethical skepticism must accordingly mean, first, the denial of a practical reason in general, the denial of any unconditionally objective validity in the whole field of praxis. Here we find the analogy. Skeptical assertions had the characteristic that in their content they generally negated what they meaningfully presupposed as assertions. Skeptical *demands* are, in exact parallel, demands that in their content generally negate what they, by their sense as demands, rationally presuppose. Demands too, not only assertions, can be negative: to "it is not" corresponds "do that not\!" If now a general "do not\!" would forbid what is the presupposition of every rational demand as such, that would be a skeptical Widersinn.

Husserl gives the concrete form. It would be such a Widersinn if the skeptic said — as the well-known Protagorean "Good is what seems good to each" wanted to say — : do not let yourself be talked into there being any action that has, over against another action, the advantage of a supposed rationality; act so that you never recognize in your action an advantage of rationality; or, perhaps better still: never act so that you grant any possible action the advantage of rationality and let yourself be practically determined by it. The demanded "never act so" is itself the demand of an action, and this demand comes forward with the claim of rationality. It is as if the skeptic said theoretically: the only rational action is to recognize no reason in action. Now if the skeptic asserts merely theoretically that to recognize (theoretically) differences of rational and irrational in action is (theoretically) irrational, that is no skeptical Widersinn in forma. But if he speaks practically — if he says it is practically rational to give no room to practical reason in action, and casts this in the form of a rule, "Act so that you never let differences of practical reason take effect" — then the skeptical Widersinn is there. It would evidently be the same if one uttered quite generally the demand "Recognize no demand as valid": the thing uttered is itself a demand, which by its sense raises the claim to recognition and validity in conflict with its unconditionally general content.

All this, Husserl notes, concerns the normative propositions uttered with the sense of rational validity. It stands otherwise with command-propositions that utter merely suggestive demands: the speaker expects the utterance of the command to be psychologically effective, but raises no claim to rational validity and its recognition. The qualification keeps the countersense located precisely where a rational claim is made, not in mere causal influence.

After this inquiry, Husserl draws the result. There are extreme skeptical practical demands, practical norms, that cancel themselves as "formally widersinnig" in a strictly analogous way to extreme skeptical "theoretical" assertions. The one kind does so through a *practical* contradiction, a contradiction between the content of the practical demand and what the formal sense of a practical demand as such presupposes; the other through a *theoretical* contradiction between the content of the assertion and what the logical form of an assertion as such presupposes. Having found a genuine analogy at a point that is itself radical, we shall seek it in wider and widest spheres. But it is striking that in the traditional dispute between ethical skepticism and ethical absolutism the whole layer of discussions is missing that would offer the strict parallel to the dispute between theoretical skepticism and theoretical idealism — and not only the parallel of extreme ethical to extreme theoretical skepticism. Missing too are all the discussions that would have to present themselves as exactly parallel to the psychologistic interpretations of the content of traditional logic.

Husserl closes by naming the methodological asymmetry that the rest of the course must repair. The radical refutation of psychologism, and its demonstration as a skepticism of extended sense that cancels itself apriori as widersinnig — so that the building of a logical doctrine of norms on empirical-psychological ground appears a perversity — proceeds by recourse to the sense-analysis of the formal-logical principles. These were given in advance by tradition, so that it was easy to tie on to them and, by a simple appeal to their sense, to lay bare cleanly the skeptical perversity of psychologism. In ethics, faced with ethical psychologism, we are not in this favorable situation; here we feel a great lack. The lack is the missing formal Praktik and Axiologie — the analytic of the practical sphere that tradition never built — and supplying it is the task Husserl turns to next.

### **§ 5\. Formal Praktik and Axiology as Analogues of Formal Logic**

#### **(a) The Desideratum of a Formal Praktik for the Refutation of Ethical Empiricism. The Sense of the Logical Principles**

Husserl explains why the psychologizing of logic was so cleanly refutable: it needed only the constitution of a pure formal logic and a bare sense-analysis of its principles. One had only to look to their pure and clear sense, and with complete evidence the ideal and absolute character of these laws sprang out, and with it the Widersinn of their psychological interpretations. That sufficed to make fully evident that logic and psychology are sharply separable disciplines, and further that there is such a thing as an absolutely cognizing reason, an idea whose essence is to be circumscribed by purely ideal laws — whereby the stamp of Widersinn is fixed on all logical empiricism.

In ethics the matter stands badly precisely because, as ethics has been traditionally formed since antiquity, we look in vain for the analogue of formal logic. Aristotle was the father of logic because he was actually the creator of the logical *Analytik*, of what we also call formal logic — however much the purity of his interpretation of the principles he at any rate formulated leaves to be wished. Through his *Nicomachean Ethics*, for all the beautiful things it offers, he did not become father of ethics in the same sense. If the conjecture guiding the analogizing considerations is right, there must also be, in the ethical sphere, in the sphere of rational praxis, something like an analytic, something like a formal Praktik — a complex of principles and laws that, in an analogous sense, abstract from the "matter" of praxis and express lawfulnesses of the pure form, as the formal-logical laws do with respect to cognition, abstracting from the so-called matter of cognition.

The conjecture spells itself out. Theoretical reason must have its analogue in a practical reason; to the essence of every kind of reason, or of its correlates, there must belong in an analogous sense distinctions between form and matter; and as on the one side apriori conditions of the possibility of truth lie in the logical form of possible judgment-contents, so in the practical form, the form of possible *Willensinhalte* (will-contents), there must lie conditions of the possibility of the analogue of truth — conditions of the possibility of practical validity, practical goodness. Husserl adds a historical diagnosis of why logic fared better: the grounding of analytic logic, the turning of interest toward pure logical form and its lawfulness, was favored by the fact that already in Aristotle's day pieces of theoretical science lay ready, especially pure mathematics, on whose firm proofs and theories the regularities of form fairly forced themselves; ethics fared less well because the sphere of praxis lacked analogous fixed shapes of objective validity, lacked an analogue of theoretical science. He notes that these analogizing considerations led him, years ago and even before the *Logische Untersuchungen*, to the problem of constituting a formal Praktik, and that in his Göttingen lectures since 1902 he tried to show that here lies a serious, genuinely fundamental desideratum whose fulfillment is by no means hopeless.

To get the situation clear, Husserl insists that although ethics has always spoken of ethical principles, what is there called an ethical principle is nothing less than the genuine analogue of what stands in logic under the title logical principle — and as with the principles, so the dependent theories on the two sides have no genuine analogy. What are called traditional logical principles are formal laws; what are called ethical principles are non-formal. He therefore reconsiders more closely the sense of the logical principles and of logic itself, in what respect it is formal. In analytic apophantic logic the "principles" are general propositions stating what lies immediately in the sense of truth in general, and the whole apophantic-analytic logic wants only to set up principial laws, and laws and theories dependent on them, that lie in the essence of the assertion with respect to its meaning. Logic treats of *Sätze* in formal generality on two sides: morphologically, as a *Formenlehre* of possible propositions regardless of the particular objectivities the propositions concern (the question being from what categories of elements propositions are built up, what their basic and complex forms are); and then — the properly logical task — by asking what conditions must be fulfilled for truths in general. Formal conditions are conditions grounded in the mere form: a contradiction violates formal conditions of possible truth, the material content of the assertion not mattering at all.

He illustrates with geometry. A geometric proposition can be false because it sins against specifically geometric *Sachverhalte*, truths grounded in the particular nature of space; but it can also be false for reasons having nothing to do with space, reasons that hold equally in every domain and stand in equal indifference to each — for instance contradiction. If a proposition has the form "A is B" and I see Not-A included in the B, so that I have "A is not A," the proposition is false on formal grounds; I need not look at A more closely, and if it means something geometrical I need not enter into specifically geometric considerations. The same holds for proposition-complexes that as wholes again raise the claim to truth: an inference or proof can be wrong because it has claimed something materially inadmissible, but also wrong because it runs in a form that, regardless of domain, excludes the validity of the inference — and then the inference is called false in the specifically logical sense, formal-logically false. After this, it is clear that formal logic determines, with respect to no contentful sphere, what is true; it says only what conditions propositions, simple and complex, must fulfill in their form to be able to be true at all. Its universal significance lies in the fact that every science wants to set up truths, and truths are just true propositions. Husserl then states the practical counterpart he is about to scrutinize: under the title of ethical principles and laws one has always sought to formulate propositions, norms, that say in a manner fitting all cases what is good, that is, what is rationally to be striven for — and, given the distinctions good/better and good-in-itself/good-for-another, the principles wanted to say which are the highest goods, or which is the highest good.

#### **(b) The Traditional Sense of the Ethical Principles. Formal Correctness According to Kant's Categorical Imperative versus Logical-Formal Correctness**
