Husserl resumes from the agreed sense of the logical principles and laws: they are laws bearing on the mere meaning-forms — proposition-forms, concept-forms, inference- and proof-forms — that express the conditions of the possibility of truth grounded in mere form, modalized correspondingly for untruth, possibility, probability. Apriori, what is thought in a possible thinking, judged in a possible judging, is so constituted that, regardless of the material particularity of the judged, conditions of the possibility of truth — of the correctness of the relevant judging — lie in the merely categorial form of the judged objectivities. Whatever I judge about, the judgment can be valid only if no open or hidden contradiction in forma lies in it; the form of contradiction in its various shapes excludes the "true" and prescribes, regardless of the judgment-matter, the "false." Syllogisms may occur in any material domain and in all forms, but for the inference to be valid it must not, say, have the form "All A are B and all B are C, so all A are not C." On certain inference-forms possible truth lawfully hangs; the possibility of truth makes, as it were, a selection within the totality of possible inference-forms, the excluded ones lawfully requiring the verdict of falsity.

What is true and false in a contentful sphere, formal logic prejudges only in the form of analytic necessities. The generality of the formal logical laws can be particularized by determining the algebraic termini materially — exactly as the pure arithmetical 3 \+ 3 \= 6, applied to apples, takes the shape "3 apples and 3 apples are 6 apples." So I can say analytically that, of two chemical or optical or philological propositions one of which affirms what the other denies, one is true and the other false. But no one will believe that with these self-evident transfers of the formal-logical laws to chemistry or physics something like chemistry or physics itself is accomplished. The analytic necessities arise from the formal-logical of themselves by mere transfer, by mere introduction of material termini; whereas what material knowledge, especially as science, wants to bring out are the specifically material truths — truths that are true not on the basis of mere categorial form but precisely for this material, on the basis of its general material genera and species. The analytic necessities leave infinite truth-fields untouched, the fields called synthetic in contrast to them, and these are the fields of the extralogical sciences. Logical laws enter the material sciences only as aids, as "a chain in the method," in Kant's expression. So it makes good sense to say that what is true in any determinate cognition-sphere (the analytic excepted) we cannot read off from the logical laws; they give us only a bulwark against formal falsity, not against every falsity.

Turning to the practical sphere, Husserl marks the contrast that controls the whole section. Here, under ethical principles and laws, one has sought to formulate *normative* laws that positively prescribe, in a generally valid way, what the good is, what is rationally to be striven for; and given the distinctions good/better and good-in-itself/good-for-another, the "principles" wanted to say which are the highest goods or which the highest good — to bring out positively, in the total range of practically attainable goods, that good which deserves to be striven for its own sake and not merely for another's, and which holds so privileged a position that all other goods could come into account, if at all, only as serving means to it. The situation here is thus other than in logic: logic with its principles cannot and will not positively decide what is true in every possible cognition-sphere; only analytic, not synthetic truth can it prejudge in its lawfulness. Ethics, and especially the ethical doctrine of principles, by contrast, attempts — rightly or wrongly — to prescribe positively, for every possible practical sphere and every individual case, what the practically good and best is, even though the principial generality includes nothing of the particularities of the individual cases.

Husserl clarifies what this aspiration amounts to. Ethics in the height of its scientific generality of course does not speak of the concrete individual case in which I, as agent, am to decide hic et nunc; but under the title of general "principles" it sought universally applicable criteria from which one could nonetheless read off the ethical goodness or badness of the given individual case, or determine positively, before the practical decision, when the decision would be ethically right and when not. The analysis of the individual case and the correct execution of the subsumption may have their special and very great difficulties, but the main point is that it is supposed to come down to a *mere subsumption*: no evaluation is to precede the principle, but is first to be drawn from the principle; or at least the full legal ground of the evaluation is to be contained in the principle in principially general fashion, so that the practically rational of the given case could be won by mere subsumption under the principle. This is the structural disanalogy with logic that the section is exposing: the ethical principle is meant to be materially decisive in a way the logical principle never is.

He drives the point home against the supposed exception of a "formal" ethics. It makes no difference in this respect whether, in the historical tradition, ethical principles are sought and held possible as so-called material or as so-called formal. Whether pleasure, general welfare, perfection, and the like are set up as the highest practical goals, or whether, with Kant, all such principles are rejected as "material" and a categorical imperative is declared the sole admissible "purely formal principle" — it is all one. How far such an opposition of "material" and "formal" has analogy with the like-named logical opposition is the very question and needs closer investigation; but to the connoisseur of Kantian ethics it is clear from the outset that, in the way the opposition is introduced and the formal principle put to use in Kant, the analogy fails at the essential point that interests us. For Kant wanted, with the categorical imperative, to give the one and only criterion of morality — not merely necessary but sufficient. He himself instructs us how to proceed with the categorical imperative in the given case in order to determine the morally good in it, the truly dutiful, or to judge ethically a volition and action already performed. Purely and exclusively the general valuation from which the demand of the categorical imperative wells up determines the value of the individual case. However formal the principle is, however much it may exclude the material in Kant's sense, it is a principle of *positive* decision, completely sufficient for the given case taken in its full concretion and individuality. This formal correctness according to the categorical imperative leaves no room for a material incorrectness. The complete opposite, Husserl recalls, holds in the cognition-sphere: there logical-formal correctness does not at all prejudge material correctness; cognition-form (judgment-form, proposition-form) separates from proposition-matter, represented by the contentful concepts of the given proposition; we have double truths, including double apriori truths, the one formal or analytic, the other material or synthetic — and precisely for this reason pure and formal logic separates, in the cognition-sphere, from the manifold material sciences.

This contrast generates the guiding chain of questions. Is the previous division between material and formal ethics, and Kant's grounding of a formal ethics, sufficient, or perhaps the only possible one? Is the historical parallelization of logic and ethics — which surely rests on not merely historical but factual motives — not to be carried out further and deeper, or otherwise than has historically been done, in such a way that not merely a superficial but a radical and thoroughgoing analogy shows itself? The progress already made by exhibiting the exact parallel between formal-logical and ethical skepticism speaks for this from the outset. The Kantian "formal" principle, in short, is not formal in the logician's sense at all, and the genuinely formal Praktik remains to be found.

#### **(c) On the Possibility of Formal-Practical and Formal-Axiological Principles**

The foregoing leads to more determinate questions. Must not the division between logic and material science, or between merely analytic and synthetic truths, also have its strict analogue in the practical sphere? Must there not, accordingly, be in the sphere of praxis too a strict analogue to what in the theoretical sphere is called pure or analytic logic? On the theoretical side we had the relation between determinate judgments and the most general idea of judging in general; we had further to distinguish apriori between judging and the What of judging, the *Satz* or judged judgment; in outermost pure generality we could consider the possible proposition-forms generally prefigured in the idea of the proposition, and then, with regard to the logical predicates of truth and falsity belonging apriori to the idea of the proposition, the apriori laws expressing the conditions of the possibility of truth that belong to the possible judgment-forms. Are not the parallels to all this to be exhibited in the practical sphere? The determinate practical acts — determinate volitions and actions — stand under an idea of willing and acting in general, investigable as an idea according to apriori holdings and laws. Then it is to be considered whether to willing as such there belongs apriori a willed What, in a sense similar to that in which the judged What, the passed judgment, belongs to judging. We would coordinate to the act of resolving, as correlate, the *Entschluss* (resolve); to the judgment-proposition "So it is\!" the will-proposition "So it shall be\!", "That shall happen\!" And as judgment-propositions, propositions in the theoretical sense, have their evaluating predicates true and false, predicates of theoretical right (apart from the modalities probable, possible, and so on), so the practical propositions would have parallel predicates of practical rightness and wrongness, as it were practical truth and falsity. As, noetically, judging is to be appraised as correct and incorrect judging, so willing would correlatively be appraised as correct and incorrect willing. And as the theoretical propositions have their changing forms on the one hand and, varying in a quite different direction, their matters — the termini — on the other, so we would have forms for the ought-propositions, for propositions of the form of the practical "Let it happen\!", "A shall be\!", "A shall be B\!", "If A is, B shall become\!", the letters again standing for termini.

If the analogy is continuously feasible, then as to the pure judgment-form there belong conditions of the possibility of theoretical truth (the pure logical laws), so to the apriori-considerable forms of practical propositions there would belong parallel conditions of the possibility of practical rightness, expressing themselves again in apriori law-form — and these would be the *formal practical laws*. As the pure logical laws can also be designated conditions of the possibility of judgment-rationality (noetically: formal norms of the rationality of judging behavior), so the pure practical laws would be formal norms of practical reason, of formal reason in willing. That is to say: every willing, and consequently every acting, would be apriori and formaliter irrational — irrational without regard to the will-matter, in mere regard to the practical form — that violated the rightness- or validity-laws prefigured precisely by the form.

Husserl maps the resulting architecture, which doubles that of the theoretical sphere. In the domain of theoretical, judging reason, the formal laws and their pure consequences join into the unity of formal logic, the *Analytik*; mere transfer to arbitrarily given matters yields the realm of analytic truths, truths insightful — apart from the demonstration of the being of the application-objects — by mere subsumption under formal-logical laws. Beside this, the theoretical sphere has the infinite realm of the non-analytic, if you will synthetic, truths distributed among the various research-fields, whether apriori or empirical-existential, whose grounding can proceed only by penetrating the contentful of the judgment-meanings. Likewise in the practical sphere there must be, on the one hand, a practical Analytik, to which a sphere of analytic praxis would correspond — a closed will-domain in which rationality is to be grounded purely formally — and then a properly contentful will-domain must attach, a will-sphere in which rationality can be grounded only out of the particular matter of the willing, whether immanently and apriori out of its essential connections, or empirically-existentially in relation to the encompassing nexus of actuality so far as it is the field of actual and possible volitions and actions.

Were this feasible, we would have exact parallels: over against formal or analytic logic would stand, as exact parallel, a formal Praktik, for which the expression "ethical analytic" or "analytic ethics" would also be fitting. Its principles would be of the most universal practical significance, since no rational volition or action could violate them — namely, as regards its form. On the other hand, if the analogy were really perfect, nothing whatever would thereby be said in the proper contentful sphere into which everything specifically moral belongs — nothing about the value of actual willing with respect to its goals, about the gradation of goods, and finally about the content of the highest practical good that is to shine before all willing as goal. Husserl is careful to keep the formal discipline's reticence in view: it secures form, not the matter of the good life.

He then widens the result to axiology. What has been worked out about an ethics or Praktik in the widest sense — beyond the specifically moral sphere — concerning the separation of an analytic or merely formal from a material ethics can be transferred without further ado to the domain of axiology; and it is to be foreseen that, if there is a formal Praktik, it must be essentially interwoven with a formal Axiologie in general. So far as one rightly speaks of a reason, so far the question of the division between an analytic-formal and a material reason-sphere can be raised, and hence the question of an analytic or formal axiology as against a material one. That this domain is intimately united with the formal-practical — that there is a higher unity which, besides ethics, spans further part-domains such as aesthetics — is easy to see, and the manner of this unity even suggests the possibility of a formal Axiologie in an extended sense.

Husserl sketches the apriori order of the value-correlates that this extended axiology would systematize. Excluding for the moment the domain of desire and will, he separates off a domain of *non-existential values*, as a domain of beauty-values in the widest sense — the values for which the existence or non-existence of the value-giving object is irrelevant to the sense and validity of the value-predication — and sets over against it the sphere of *existential valuations*, where the reverse holds; the values that form the correlate of rational existential valuations of the genus *Freude* (joy) he calls *Güter* (goods), the corresponding negative values *Übel* (evils). Then the apriori connections become visible. Every beautiful object is at once a good one: what proves beautiful in rational aesthetic valuation must necessarily prove good in rational existential valuation; that is, if it exists, or stands as existing in conviction, it is necessarily — generally rationally — the object of a justified joy, and in the reverse case of non-existence the object of a justified grief. Again, if the character of existence is lacking for consciousness but the assumption of existence rationally grounds an assumptive pleasure — if, thinking ourselves into the existence, the thought "supposing it were" grounds a rationally justifiable quasi-joy, "that would be a real joy\!" — then the same object or state of affairs would be the justified bearer of a rational wish, and, if it stands as realizable, of a justified will. Calling the value-correlates of the rational wish *Begehrungswerte* (desire-values) and those of the rational will *Willenswerte* or practical values, it is then clear that all these values cohere apriori: the beautiful is at once a good; every good, if it does not exist, is desire-worthy; every desire-worthy, if realizable, is a will-value — so that the concept of the good as a rule expands, the rightly desirable too being called good, and the practical value being practically good.

Husserl closes the section by registering that these connections, holding in unconditioned generality, already seem of formal nature: the generality is such that the "matter" of beauty and goodness does not matter, so that something like formal laws expresses itself in the propositions stated. In any case it is certain that principles independent of this matter of valuing — independent of the particularity of the objects valued — and bearing on the value-categories, on the mere "form," claim a great universal axiological significance; and this holds above all for the propositions bearing on the most general category "value in general," regardless of the particularity of the value-domains in their categorial distinctions. At any rate the thought of a possible formal Axiologie, in analogy with a formal logic, here opens up.

#### **(d) The Distinction between Act and Content in the Practical and Axiological Sphere. The Normative Turn of the Practical and Axiological Laws**

Husserl adds a point indispensable for carrying through the analogy. In formal logic, purely and theoretically grasped as he has shown it must be, we deal with meanings — in particular with proposition-meanings with respect to their form — and with the validity-laws dependent on form; and each such law can be *normatively turned*, so that to each there corresponds equivalently a norm of correct judging in general, a norm of the most general validity, holding in whatever cognition-sphere judging may be exercised. The law of contradiction states theoretically that of two contradictory propositions one is true and the other false; normatively this corresponds to the judgment-rule that whoever has judged it to be A may not judge it to be not-A, and conversely. Likewise each inference-law theoretically set up in formal logic — that from two premiss-propositions of such and such form there follows a proposition of such and such form — corresponds to the judgment-norm that whoever passes premiss-judgments of those forms may rationally infer that the judgment of the corresponding form holds. The theoretical laws speak of propositions but say nothing of anyone's judging or ought-to-judge; the normative turns of the laws, by contrast, are meant precisely to be norms for the judger and say how he ought to judge in order to judge rationally. This equivalence — that to each theoretical-analytic law there corresponds an evidently equivalent, universally valid norm — rests on the fact that every judgment has an ideal judgment-content, namely what is called the *Satz* in the logical sense, and that in the most general of judgment-content in general there ground ideal lawful conditions of the possibility of judgment-correctness, of the possibility of insightful judging.

Following the guiding analogy, the same must hold in the practical and axiological sphere: there too there must be apriori formal laws and, running parallel and evidently equivalent to them, apriori norms — now not norms of rational judging but norms of rational valuing, of rational wishing or willing. The analogy then requires that, corresponding to the division between judging and judgment-content (between thinking and the meaning-content of thinking), we may and must distinguish in the practical sphere between *willing as act* and the *Willensinhalt* — the will-content as, so to speak, will-meaning, the practical proposition. To the form of the will-content, to the basic shapes lying in the essence of such content in general, there must belong the theoretical laws that would run parallel to the formal-logical, analytic laws; and the normative turn of these laws must yield equivalent rules of rational willing in general — norms not to be violated if the willing is not to be irrational from the most radical ground, irrational because it would offend against the "sense" of willing in general, against what its "meaning-content" in general demands. Willing, moreover, orders into a wider sphere; and rather for the widest sphere of the axiological all this would have to be distinguishable, or such distinction would have to be possible.

To make the demand clearer Husserl dwells a moment on the parallel of meaning. Judging is *Meinen* (meaning) in the sense of being convinced; the judgment-meaning necessarily has a meaning-content or content, the logical *Satz*. When one contrasts the judging and the judgment, the latter is nothing but the logically self-sufficient *Satz*; judging is the meaning of the content "This S is P," "S in general is P," and the like. The wish- or will-consciousness, on the other hand, is, as it were, a wish- or will-meaning, and this practical meaning too has a "content" — not the content "This S is P" but the content "This S shall be P" or "An S in general shall be P." In this way, in willing, we express the resolve — yet not as the expression of the fleeting resolving, the will-act, but of the content; likewise acting and action, wishing and wish. Equally, valuing as liking and disliking (*Gefallen* and *Missfallen*) is a value-meaning and has its content, which we cannot, however, designate clearly in language — we can only use the ought-expressions. As to the judgment-content belong truth and falsity, and correlatively to the judgment-act logical rationality and irrationality, so in the axiological domain the value-contents have the predicates value- and disvalue-being, or goodness and badness, while the acts have axiological rationality or irrationality. Thus the resolve is good or bad, but the resolving, the will, is practically rational (possibly morally rational) or irrational; likewise the wishing is rational or irrational, the wish a good one or its opposite.

Husserl rounds the parallel off on the side of the correlate-object. On the intellective side: logically the meaning relates to a meant objectivity; every judging has a judgment-content, the judged What, its meaning, but an object does not always correspond to it; the object is "in truth," it is said, if the judgment is correct, rational. Likewise on the ethical side: to the will-meaning there always belongs a sense, to the resolving the resolve, to the acting the action; but only if the will is a rational one, the resolve a good one, does the resolve have practical validity and the practical value ethical reality, axiological reality — the concept of reality being here, of course, a transferred one. With act/content and the normative turn secured on both sides, the formal parallel is complete in its scaffolding, and Husserl can proceed to look for the actual parallel laws.

### **§ 6\. The Formal Relation between Means and End Is No Logical Relation. The Omni-Dominion of Logical Reason**

Looking for an actual fund of propositions in the axiological-practical sphere parallel to formal logic, Husserl says, we will most readily think of the formal relations that obtain between means and ends, and so too between mediate and immediate values. The means–end nexus is the obvious first candidate for a formal-practical law.

He notes that Kant himself occasionally felt this analogy. In the *Grundlegung zur Metaphysik der Sitten* (Academy edition IV, 417\) Kant remarks that the proposition "whoever wills the end also wills the indispensably necessary means to it" is, as concerns the willing, *analytic*, since in willing an object as my effect my causality as an acting cause — the use of means — is already thought; whereas (420) the categorical imperative is a synthetic-practical apriori, a "practical proposition" that does not derive the willing of an action analytically from another, already presupposed willing but connects it immediately with the concept of the willing of a rational being, as something not contained in it. Husserl takes Kant's hypothetical-imperative remark as the very test case for whether the means–end relation is logically analytic.

He cannot approve Kant's claim in every respect. The willing of the end is not, in the proper sense — which could only be the psychological or phenomenological sense — a complex that *really* implies the willing of the single necessary means; and if not in the proper sense, then in what sense, which would need determining. To say the willing of the means is "co-thought" — properly co-thought? Surely not. We may well will an end without being in the least clear about the means, hence also not about any "single necessary" means. Willing is, to be sure, impossible without some conviction of attainability, though how this conviction looks would itself be a question. Every willing, at least, goes to a goal, and in the representation of the goal there is given, necessarily given, the being-the-end of a way toward it: the will to the goal is necessarily the will to the goal *through the way*. But this does not say that the way is represented as a determinate one. There are determinate and indeterminate representations, and both can underlie the will; so the representation of a goal as one "somehow to be reached" can found a willing, the "somehow" remaining possibly very indeterminate. The subsequent cognition of a determinate way — even of one that is at the time the sole possible way — may become a later will-foundation: the earlier will, now reproduced and "determining itself more closely," passes over into the new will, the indeterminate action set in the resolve passing into the action now set with a determinate way. One may then say the will is "the same," only more closely determined, and that the will of the goal necessarily "implied" a way to be taken, and so also the single one. But all this is to be taken improperly.

The parallel with the analytic inference makes the point exact. The premiss-judgments are said to enclose the conclusion "analytically"; but it would be fundamentally perverse to believe that one finds the conclusion really, phenomenologically or psychologically, in the premisses as lived experiences or experience-correlates. The relation is not psychological but *logical*. Rationally one cannot judge the premisses and deny the conclusion, for the conclusion lies "logically" in the premisses, purely in the propositions: in the premiss-meanings, posited as valid meanings, grounds the validity of the conclusion. I need only deepen myself into the sense of what is asserted and I find it — and so I say, figuratively, that the judging of the conclusion lies therein. Just so in the will-sphere: in the sense of goal-willing grounds, rationally, the willing of the means; but the willing of the means does not lie really enclosed, explicitly or implicitly, in the willing of the goal. The "lying-therein" is on both sides an ideal grounding-relation read off the contents, not a real containment in the act.

Husserl sharpens this with the case of *false* inference. Inferring is not always rational; we often infer falsely, so the conclusion does not always really lie rationally in the premisses. Yet, judging the premisses, we mean that the conclusion lies in them — not that our psychic acts of judging the premisses psychologically-really include the act of judging the conclusion. When we infer we are not psychologists; we do not observe and analyze our psychic phenomena. We judge: all men are mortal, Socrates is a man, "therein lies" that Socrates is mortal; and because we see this lying-therein — seen in the direction of the judgment-contents — we say reflectively that if someone wanted to pass the premiss-judgments and not acknowledge what the conclusion says, that would be irrational. All this belongs to the sense of inferring, to its meaning as the meant-in-it, even when the inferring is false: in false inferring too, if I really infer, I "see" the containment of the consequence in the ground, only this seeing is mere semblance, much as we often perceive sensuously and afterward recognize the perception to have been illusion.

This transfers wholly to the will-sphere. We will a goal and mean that a means is needed for its attainment, indeed quite indispensable; we then will this means. In the essence of a will-goal as such, Husserl repeats, lies the being-the-end of a way toward it; so what necessarily belongs to the way, hence every necessary means, is set with the goal and in a certain way co-enclosed in it. Accordingly the willing of the means is rationally demanded by the willing of the goal, and goal-willing "implies, in the sense of reason," means-willing. This belongs to the sense of every will-situation as such and of every will-content, regardless of whether what is there posited really obtains — regardless of whether the meant means really belongs to the way, is really a suitable or even the sole suitable means.

Husserl now states the section's central negative thesis: it is of special importance to bring oneself to the insight that the will-situation is not a mere special case of the intellectual, logical situation, and so that will-reason is not a special case of logical reason in inferring. Above all it is evident that the rational enclosure of the means in the end — of means-willing in end-willing — is not an "analytic" relation, not something like a logical inference-relation. This emerges when we bring the will-situation to predicative expression. From "I will the end Z" and "M is a necessary means to Z" no one can infer, by formal-logical principles, "I must will M, that is, I ought rationally to will M," unless he inserts the general proposition: whoever wills any end "must" will the necessary means to it. But just this proposition states the principle of the inference and is itself in question. It is plainly no logical-analytic proposition, none evident from the essence of judgment in general, none that would be valid regardless of its particular judgment-matter from the form of judgment alone. Analyzing what the proposition says — first, what "means and end" says — we are led to a *causal* relation: the becoming-real of the fact M draws after it, or would draw after it, the becoming-real of the fact Z. To this is added, for the inference in question, that the agent cannot realize Z directly but can directly realize a causal way to Z, and that he can realize no other sufficient cause for Z than one that implies M as a necessary member. Where all this obtains we can indeed infer that in such cases we must will M — "must" naming here no psychological but a reason-necessity, a rational ought. But after this analysis we see all the more clearly that this inference from the willing of Z to the rational ought-to-will of M is no analytic one, none grounded in the mere judgment-form; for then it would have to remain valid if we replaced all the concepts here occurring — cause, effect, will, in short all the non-purely-logical concepts — by indeterminate ones, which is out of the question. It is no logical contradiction, for all the unreason, to will the end and not will the means.

If, then, the inference is no analytic-logical one and the reason at work in it no analytic reason, is it not logical reason in the wider sense — experience-logical reason, the reason exercised in the knowledge of facts, in natural-scientific and psychological knowledge? Clearly not, though this reason too is certainly involved. Between end and means there lies, by the conviction of the one resolving or weighing a will-inference, a causal relation; he has to judge about empirical facts and their causal relations. But that alone does not do it. Husserl's example: I have an important personal communication to make to a friend and must therefore seek him out. If I consider this real process in itself — the bodily movements, the psychic phenomena of communicating, the causal relations — these may have real properties presupposed in talk of rational means to the end; but in themselves the real properties contain nothing of means and end and of reason. Even with special regard to the relevant will-acts little is achieved toward doing justice to the rational ought of means-willing for the sake of end-willing. Under the mere aspect of the experience-logical consideration — the psychological, physical, psychophysical — the addition of the will-acts has only widened the sphere of facticities, but will-reason is not to be found in it. The physical facts, the leg-movements and the like, order into physical nature by physical laws; the psychic processes of communicating and willing-to-communicate, of willing-to-go and the like, into the sphere of the psychic; both together into psychophysical nature. What is truth here, the experience-logical reason determines. But that the communication-will demands that, if I will it, I must rationally will the going-to-the-friend — this the experience-logical reason cannot tell me. The rational ought is no fact of experience; the factual will arising in the psychological nexus is no ought-to-will. Even the consciousness of ought-to-will that arises in the end-consideration cannot help, for it is not itself an ought-to-will. More generally, value and the consciousness of a value differ just as truth and holding-true, judging, differ. The arising and passing of judgments in the consciousness-nexus, the causally grounding establishment that in the given case just this judgment, this truth-consciousness, even this evidence-consciousness, must appear, is not the rational grounding of the truth. Just so here: the experience-logical consideration can establish only that the will-consciousness and the ought-consciousness appear, and must appear by empirical necessity under these circumstances, as a causal-real fact; it cannot establish that this means-willing is one rationally demanded in view of the willed end. The rationality of this demand has its grounds in a wholly different dimension than the facts; the matter-of-fact grounding of the fact that one wills so, that such a demand-consciousness is lived, is something totally other than the grounding of the demand itself as a rational one — one that would be rational even if the agent disregarded it, even if it did not appear as a fact. Indeed it is clear that the ought-law "whoever wills the end must rationally will the necessary means" is apriori, evidently valid in unconditioned generality and necessity, whereas all experiential establishments and laws found by experience are empirical. This is the decisive result: an apriori practical law that no factual-causal inquiry could yield.

Husserl then voices, at length, the objection that would collapse the result back into logicism — the thesis that all reason is only one, namely logical reason, so that practical and axiological reason are merely a special application-field of the logical. Certainly, the objector grants, experience-logical, natural-scientific consideration does not yield the rational ought and the ought-law. But did we not say *law*? Do we not speak of its apriori validity and of its grounding, which may indeed lie in a quite different line than a natural-scientific one? Now a law is an assertion and so something logical; and it is logical reason that establishes its truth, demonstrates its apriori validity, grounds it. As logical thinking bears on other domains, so on the domain of valuing and willing; and as it elsewhere discovers and grounds analytic, empirical, or synthetic-apriori truths, so on this domain too. That is striking. And indeed, Husserl concedes, it strikingly proves the self-evident: that insofar as anything is known it is known; that wherever objects are posited, predicates attributed to them, general laws established, in all this cognitive activities in the sense of logical reason go forward. So when we speak about axiological demands and demand-laws, and speak rationally about them, we judge logically-rationally, and when we ground we ground by logical reason. This universal dominion of logical reason no one can deny. But — Husserl's rejoinder — must that mean that a practical and axiological reason in analogy to the logical has no sense? The omnipresence of logical reason as the form of all knowing does not show that there is only one *kind* of reason; logical reason knowing the value-domain is not the same as the valuing reason whose lawfulness it knows.

He marks the two directions of logical cognition that must be kept apart. Logically thinking and cognizing, we relate, first, to the cognition-sphere itself, setting over against each other judgment and truth, judging as psychological fact and judging insofar as it is directed, as it were, upon an ideal goal, the goal of truth; in most general formal generality we consider judging in general and goal-directed judging that conforms to the judgment-goal, the truth-norm, and we set up formal laws for this ideal goal-correctness, laws for rational judgments in general, or formal laws for truth as such and its essential constituents. Logically thinking and cognizing, we relate, second, also to the spheres of valuing in the pregnant sense — the spheres of liking and disliking, of wishing, of willing — and here again we set over against each other the valuing as psychological fact and the value, as it were the ideal goal upon which valuing is directed, factual valuing and norm-conforming valuing, valuing that values as it ought; and here too we set up most general ideal laws for this ought, laws for value as such, not for the valued but for the rationally value-bearing as such. The same logical reason operates in both directions, but in the second its object is a valuing reason with its own ideal lawfulness.

Husserl closes the section by turning the concession into the opening for the whole positive program, and by diagnosing the temptation it resists. Should it not, despite the omni-efficacy of logical reason — whose field is just the all of the knowable in general — be able to turn out that there are two or more kinds of ought or validity, related to several act-genera that yield as many different forms of reason-consciousness? And should it not turn out that, as judging, liking, wishing, and willing are essentially different by genus, so the oughts or validities becoming conscious in them are likewise generically different? And further: if everywhere a grounding is needed to demonstrate the ought-claim, and grounding is, as it were, the fulfillment of the respective kinds of "intentions," of "meanings" — judgment-meanings, liking-meanings, wish- and will-meanings — should it not turn out that these grounding fulfillments are fundamentally different, corresponding to the genus-differences? It is intelligible against this background, he notes, that one has sometimes tried outright to assume that judging belongs as a species within the extent of valuing in the ordinary sense — that it is something will- or feeling-like — or that what carries the idea of truth into the judgment-domain, and constitutes the difference between proper knowledge and mere judgment, consists in the accession of a valuing feeling, an added necessity- or ought-consciousness that constitutes the value-differences. There the properly intellective, the specifically logical reason, is reinterpreted into a species of valuing reason in the narrower sense — the very emotionalist confusion the next section will diagnose and correct.

### **§ 7\. The Analogies for the Judging Position-Taking of Belief in the Classes of Emotive and Will Acts as the Ground for the Parallelism of the Kinds of Reason**

Husserl restates the principial difficulty that closed the last lecture. The parallelism of axiological and practical reason with the logical is meant to issue in parallel laws: to the formal being-laws are to run parallel formal ought-laws, and to the formal validity-laws for judgment-activities and judgment-propositions are to run parallel formal validity-laws for emotive and will activities and their proposition-analoga; formal logic and ontology are to have their exact analogy in a formal axiology and Praktik. But one may take offense that all these laws are still laws, all these disciplines still disciplines, standing as such under the formal-logical laws — so that all domains order, quite self-evidently, into the frame of logical reason. There would then really be only one reason, which judges and grounds judgments, and the difference of reason-kinds could refer only to material particularizations of various judgment-spheres.

This *Allherrschaft* (omni-dominion) of logical reason, Husserl grants, is undeniable. It lies in the fact that all the ideas we grasp under the title reason are themselves ideas, logical graspabilities, designating domains for possible judgments, which in turn stand under reason-principles, those of logic; the investigation of reason and of any reason whatever is of course a logical activity. But — the section's guiding question — is it thereby said that every reason is itself logical reason?

Husserl locates the root of the parallelism of reason-kinds in the parallelism of the basic kinds of acts, in each of which we find a basic kind of meaning, in a widest sense a *Stellungnahme* (position-taking). The cognition-sphere, the representation-sphere in the widest sense, is characterized by all its acts grouping around the acts of belief: the position-takings here are takings of "belief," of holding-for-being or holding-for-true. Knowing is a meaning — meaning that something is, or is constituted thus and so. The doxic acts have here the central position, and all others are mere modalities of belief: acts of negation, or in another direction acts of surmising, of holding-for-possible, and the like — other position-takings, but characterized in essence as modifications of original belief, of belief-certainty. Over against the class of cognition-acts stands, as an essentially new class, that of the *Gemütsakte* — the acts of feeling, desiring, and willing, which itself falls into closely connected genera. Here new basic kinds of position-taking appear. In aesthetic liking something is conscious to us as beautiful; and it must be noted that it is one thing to *judge about* beauty and another to *have liking in* the beautiful. Living in the enjoyment of the beautiful we perform a liking-meaning — repelled by the unbeautiful, a disliking-meaning — and this, as meaning-behavior, is the analogue of judging-meaning, more generally of the being-meaning of the kind of belief. Likewise every wishing and desiring is a kind of meaning; in it lies a holding-for-good, or in negative desire a holding-for-bad. This holding-for, lying purely in the Gemütsakt, is the analogue of the judging holding-for-being or holding-for-being-thus. Only on the basis of the beautiful- or good-valuing Gemütsakte does a judging eventually build up: a position-taking of belief is performed, concepts and words are drawn in, and predicative judgments about valuings and values arise, among them law-judgments. This is the crux: valuing is itself a meaning, prior to and independent of any judgment about it.

Husserl distinguishes the two directions cognition can take here. Cognition-acts — acts of simple perception and experience, or of ideative intuition, and at a higher level acts of predication that bring under concepts and relate logically — can of course direct themselves upon any consciousness-sphere. We can direct ourselves, thinking logically, upon the logical consciousness itself, making the cognition-sphere a scientific domain, and subject it to an essential investigation both on the side of the acts and on the side of their "contents" and "objects": we then do phenomenology of cognition-consciousness in all its lower and higher levels, including the specifically logical levels, and, on the other hand, with regard to the meaning-contents of this consciousness and its objects taken in formal generality, we do pure logic, apophantic logic, and formal ontology. But we can also direct our cognition upon the general formations of the emotive and will consciousness, and here the sole question is whether one has made analytically clear to oneself what the earlier ethicists failed to clarify: that in the acts themselves *as position-takings* that radical analogy really obtains — that valuing too is a holding-for, a meaning, and this as emotive consciousness in itself and before all supervening judging. As to the judgment-acts, more generally the acts of belief, there belong ideal appraisals by ideas of validity and invalidity, and correlatively the ideas of true being and non-being, so to the Gemütsakte as position-takings there belong ideal appraisals by ideas of validity and invalidity and correlatively the ideas of the beautiful and the good, and the unbeautiful and the bad, in the validity-sense.

He draws out the apriori symmetry. As, in the judgment-sphere, the judging as psychological fact can be considered in connections of psychophysical reality, so of course can the feeling, desiring, willing with their proper position-takings; and on both sides the real-causal consideration lies in a wholly different direction than the consideration of ideal appraisal, which investigates in the different spheres different connections of the apriori. Apriori, judging as holding-true separates from correct judging, from judging that, so to speak, reaches the ideal goal of truth; that every judging whatever falls, in unconditioned generality and so apriori, under the normative ideas correctness and incorrectness is axiomatically evident. And the exact parallel holds for the meaning-acts of the Gemüt: they stand under ideas of correctness and incorrectness, so that apriori an arbitrary holding-for-beautiful-and-good separates from one that is correct, that reaches its ideal goal, that values as it ought to value; and here, in apriori consideration, we can set up most general ideal laws for this ought and correlatively ideal laws for possible values as such according to their form, in analogy to the logical laws.

Husserl now states precisely what the omni-dominion of logical reason does and does not establish. Its omni-efficacy consists in its spanning the whole field of the knowable in general, including the knowable we designate as the essence of the reason-kinds; it then recognizes precisely that there are apriori essentially different genera of meaning-acts, of position-takings, and belonging to them — as we believe we see — basic kinds of validities, or normings, of oughts, with basic kinds of normative correlates: valid propositions, objects, values. The essential analogies obtaining here have again and again pressed themselves on philosophers, but the deeper-penetrating analysis was lacking that would have advanced to the ultimate sources of the analogies and really demonstrated the parallel structures of acts and act-correlates. Hence the conflations, so popular in our time, of judging and valuing reason: one likes to view judging outright as a kind of valuing, valuing in the sense of an emotive act.

He grants the kernel of truth and isolates the error. In a certain widest sense one may indeed say that all meanings, all intentional position-takings, are to be designated "value-holdings," all standing under ideas of ought, since to all of them, insofar as they mean, the question of correctness or incorrectness — the value-question, if you like — is to be put. But instead of distinguishing radically according to basic kinds, and convincing oneself that within the highest genus "act" there are irreducible genera to which irreducible genera of position-takings, and accordingly of oughts or validities, correspond — in other words, instead of convincing oneself that under the title "value" an extended general concept is now won that divides into irreducible basic genera — one mixes everything confusedly together. One confuses the generalized value-concept with the usual narrower one referred to the emotive sphere; one grasps judging itself as something emotive, or seeks to make us believe that what carries the so-called value-difference of truth and falsity into the judgment-domain consists in the accession of valuing feelings, feelings of a transcendental ought, supervening feelings of thought-necessity. There the specifically logical reason is reinterpreted into a valuing reason manifesting itself exclusively in feelings. While intellectualism is inclined to explain away all reason in the emotional sphere and dissolve it into activities of logical reason, this emotionalism proceeds conversely: logical reason is reinterpreted into an emotional one, rational evaluation and evaluation in general being made a mere affair of feeling — while all closer analyses are lacking that could in turn make intelligible how feeling could constitute objective validity at all.

Husserl names the targets and corrects the overshoot. This critique is directed against the theories of Rickert and Windelband, so popular in our time. In them the axiological reason scorned by the tradition breaks through, in a certain way; its very peculiarity becomes so strongly felt by these Fichte-leaning researchers that they fall into the exaggeration of reinterpreting logical reason itself axiologically. But — returning to his own starting point — the omni-dominion of logical reason need not make us waver about the parallelism of the reason-kinds. In that dominion is expressed a certain essential interweaving of the doxic consciousness with the emotive consciousness, and so with every consciousness whatever, by which every position-taking, every beautiful- or good-valuing, can apriori be converted into a judging position-taking. By this means not only does the manifold of Gemütsakte — as phenomenological data, or in the empirical-psychological attitude as facts of psychophysical nature — become an object of judgment-activities and specifically of scientific activities; the right and wrong of the emotive position-takings, and the right-unities correlatively belonging to them — the values and value-connections, the material and formal value-laws — also become fields of establishments carried out in logical acts. The bringing-out, establishing, determining, the objectivating in the specific sense, is the affair of logical reason. Axiological reason with its holdings is, so to speak, hidden from itself; it becomes manifest only through the cognition carried out on the ground of the Gemütsakte. But cognition does not invent — it only draws out what is in a certain way already there. Were the Gemüt not a domain of meanings, did it not already, in its own emotive manner, make decisions and cast its vote, cognition would find before it nothing of values and value-contents; it would find only blind experiences, like the experiences of sensing red and blue. A mere sensing, a mere living-through of a sensuous datum, means nothing; but a liking means, a wishing means. Meaning is not always judging in the ordinary sense, a mode of believingly holding-for-something, but it is an analogue of judging.

The will makes the point vividly. The will certainly does not judge — willing as such is not believing, not knowing; it does not perceive, does not predicate, performs no deducing or inducing. And yet, in a certain sense and figuratively, it does judge: it is not merely there as a psychological fact like any other, about which logical reason eventually judges and which it ranges into the nexus of nature; rather, whether or not it is logically judged about, it itself *speaks*, it gives, as it were, a vote, it is a valuing and a deciding — and we call judging too a deciding. There are thus different kinds of deciding, of voting. The judgment is the consciousness of "so it is," the will the consciousness of a certain "so it shall be." But the will alone cannot utter, cannot assert, what is consciously meant in it in its own way: that is its *Unselbständigkeit* (non-self-sufficiency). It needs logical acts in order to come to word, and the result is the ought-judgment, which is a judgment and no will. Logical reason must therefore look, as it were, onto the field of the practical, must lend it the eye of the intellect, and only then can what the rational will demands, and what lies necessarily in the sense of its demands, step forth objectively. There then arise norms and norm-laws which on the one hand have their logical truth, insofar as they bring to faithful expression what practical reason means and what belongs to the sense of this meaning, and which on the other hand, in the form of their logical rationality, refer to something that is not logical rationality, while yet having something in common with it that may, precisely, be called reason in a new sense.

Husserl closes the section by marking the status of these results and naming the master-problem. These are, to be sure, no fully sufficient groundings; they are clearly seen possibilities still in need of deeper confirming analyses. But they suffice to keep our parallelistic conception from appearing inadmissible from the outset, and we have come far enough that the problems to be solved have moved into graspable nearness. We see the basic difficulty that obtains here — the interweaving of logical reason with the pretended practical and axiological reason — and the basic problem is, within this interweaving, to separate the components.

### **§ 8\. Recapitulation. Formal Logic as the Clue to the Discovery of the Parallel Structures in the Emotive Sphere. The Privilege of Logical-Predicative Reason**

Husserl recapitulates the inquiry of the weeks before the break. From of old one parallelizes theoretical with ethical skepticism, as one parallelizes logic and ethics themselves; he pursued these analogies, searched for their deepest sources and their truest, most pregnant formulation, separated spurious from genuine analogies, and really found genuine ones. In these inquiries lay already deep motives for assuming that there must be a discipline parallel to pure formal logic — an apriori formal ethics, generally a formal axiology and Praktik. For the debates with logical skepticism referred specifically to the scientific field of logic in a narrow and firmly bounded sense, to the analytic, so-called formal logic; and so ethical skepticism, insofar as it was constructed as the exact analogue of the logical, pointed to a formal ethics in the corresponding sense.

Although logic and ethics had from of old been regarded as parallel philosophical disciplines, the parallelism was far from having been carried through with strict scientific rigor. The division historically achieved in the logical sphere — between apriori and aposteriori, formal and material logic, a division admittedly blurred again and again by the empiricist philosophies — these fundamental divisions, Husserl says, had never been completely, never in the scientifically required way, carried out for ethics. Kant, to be sure, who here gave the most significant impulses and recognized as no other the foundational importance of the radical divisions to be made, had tried to ground an apriori formal ethics. But we convinced ourselves that this Kantian formalistic ethics cannot count as the analogue of formal logic. Were Kant right, the analogy between theoretical (cognizing) and practical consciousness, between the logical in general and the ethical in general, would rather break apart, and with it the analogy between the formal-logical and the formal-practical.

He spells out why. With Kant material ethics was given up — the analogue of material logic — and formal ethics reduced to a single formal principle, the categorical imperative, which, although it had excluded all matter of valuing and willing, was nonetheless supposed to be fit to prescribe, for every concretely given, materially determinate case of action, what the dutiful good, the ethically required, is for it. But a logical principle in the sense of formal logic is wholly incapable of deciding, by mere subsumption, for every concretely presented cognition-case what the positively true is for it. The Kantian "formal" principle thus does precisely what a genuinely formal principle cannot do, and so is not formal in the logician's sense. Husserl adds that Kant's formal ethics was not even designed as an analogue of the formal, analytic logic: although the opposition of form and matter was supposed to be analogous on both sides, Kant did not let himself be led in a radical way by the thought of a thoroughgoing parallelism between theoretical and practical reason-consciousness, nor by the thought of a discipline analogous to analytic logic, referred to valuing and willing with respect to their pure forms.

Husserl then reviews his own positive procedure. We set energetically about tracking down what must be exhibitable in the sphere of the emotive consciousness and its correlates if a genuine analogue, in the form of a formal axiology and Praktik, were to exist. We sought to procure a deepened idea of the essence of formal logic and used it as a parallelistic clue to the eventual discovery of the parallel structures, forms, and formal laws conjectured in the emotive sphere. Logic relates to the consciousness we called belief, more distinctly the doxic consciousness, which characterizes itself as a meaning that something is; more specially it relates to the predicative *Doxa*, whose correlate is the predicative proposition, and which, when we strip away the linguistic form, is the passed judgment — and this has its objective correlate in the *Sachverhalt* (state of affairs). Three things are thus to be distinguished: the act of judging, that is the judging-meaning, which stands under the jurisdiction of doxic rationality or irrationality; the judgment itself, the *apophansis*, the *Satz*, which was the proper theme of traditional formal logic — which we therefore called apophantic logic, taking up the Aristotelian term; and the *Sachverhalt*.

He recalls the chain of formal disciplines this yields. Propositions are true and false, and to express formal truth-laws is the aim of formal logic; these laws let themselves be turned into laws for objects and states of affairs in general, which, like the apophantic laws and in a correlative sense, leave all "matter" in indeterminate generality — and with this turn we enter formal ontology. So we have here really a chain of formal disciplines related to one another through correlations: formal noetics, formal apophantics, formal ontology. We then sought the parallels in all these relations and found them: we found the valuing consciousness, be it beautiful- or good-valuing or practical, so analogous to the judging that we could speak everywhere, in a more general sense, of a meaning, and the parallels on the side of the act-correlates were not lacking either — the analogues of propositions and the analogues of states of affairs, here as *Wertverhalte* (value-states) and practical states. We did not, to be sure, allow ourselves the time for an exact and deep-going execution of these parallels; we pressed straight on. Having seen enough to be assured of a far-reaching analogy, we looked about for parallels of the formal-logical laws, and here Kant helped us further with his occasional remark in the *Grundlegung* on "analytic" will-relations, drawing in the law that whoever wills the end must will the means that alone leads to it. Here it mattered first not to miss the legitimate sense of this talk of "analytic" and to make clear that this "analytic" does not, as Kant meant, say the identical same as the "analytic" in the formal-logical sense, but that here a mere analogue of the logical-analytic is present.

Husserl marks the threshold thereby crossed. Here was a point at which we entered the genuine and sought-for parallel-sphere to the formal-logical. The axiomatically evident laws that regulate the relations of ends and means are plainly, in the genuine sense of the word, apriori laws referred to the sphere of the will and its essence-correlates; they hold plainly in unconditioned necessity and generality, and they are formal laws, since they include nothing whatever of the matter of the willed — only the form, as will-form, comes into question. Whatever the will may relate to, there belong to its essence as will the possible differences of goal-willing and means-willing, and to these correspond, on the side of the correlates, of the willed-nesses as such, certain forms understandable only in this correlation, indicated by the words end and means. To these pure forms relate laws of will-rationality, which are plainly parallels to the formal laws of the rationality of judging in mere regard to the judgment-forms.

But here, Husserl recalls, a serious difficulty crossed our path, one whose complete clarification entangles us in very deep phenomenological investigations: in short, the difficulty of the omni-dominion of logical reason. In itself and spoken from above it is quite self-evident that logical thinking is in action wherever propositions or laws are set up, and that if we proceed rationally there we proceed, of course, logically-rationally. But we may not draw from this the conclusion that there is only a single reason, the logical, the reason that manifests itself in belief and in the higher activities of conceptual determining and predicating.

He fixes the sense of "reason" that lets the conclusion be resisted. For clarity, the word reason here is not understood in the sense of a human faculty of soul, psychologically in the ordinary sense, but comprehends a title for the essentially closed class of acts and their appertaining act-correlates that stand under ideas of rightness and wrongness, correlatively of truth and falsity, of obtaining and not-obtaining, and the like. As many basic kinds of acts as we can distinguish for which this holds, so many basic kinds of reason. In the first line come the doxic acts, the acts of belief-certainty with their modalities; but then we must go further. Every act that has the character of a meaning, of a holding-for in the widest sense of the word — and we do see that this generalization has its good sense — stands under such normative ideas, which are corresponding generalizations of those occurring in the logical sphere. Logical reason, however, has the unique privilege that it formulates right not only in its own field but in the field of every other genus of meaning, in every other reason-sphere: it determines rightness, predicates and expresses right-laws as laws. Valuing and practical reason are, so to speak, mute and in a certain way blind. Even seeing, in the narrower and wider sense, and so also in the sense of "insight" (*Einsehen*), is a doxic act. A right can be there without being seen, can be there without being brought under right-concepts, explicated in judgment-form, and uttered as a law in assertoric form: it is there as a right of valuing reason when valuing acts of a certain essential kind and formation are performed or performable. But mere valuing reason does not see, does not grasp, does not explicate, does not predicate. With it, therefore, acts of the logical — in the widest sense doxic — sphere must interweave. Only in the performance of such acts can acts in general, and what they mean, come to objective givenness for us, and can we then see that valuing acts are "meaning," holding-for-beautiful and \-good, and further that they stand under ideal predicates of rightness and wrongness. So the torch of logical reason must be lit, that what is hidden of forms and norms in the emotive and will sphere may step into the bright light. But the logical acts only shine in and make visible what is there; they constitute only the logical forms, not the peculiar reason-contents of the parallel reason-spheres. To understand from the deepest grounds how doxic and higher logical acts can accomplish what is here required of them, and on the other side what it means that Gemütsakte are originary sources for the reason-values peculiar to them, which can afterward receive logical formulation and determination, requires very difficult demonstrations in the field of the general essential structures of consciousness. If time permits, Husserl says, we shall later attempt to treat a piece of this ultimately-clarifying phenomenology of the reason-structures; for now we return to the main line of our discussions. With this the first Abschnitt closes, its result secured: the parallelism is genuine, logical reason is sovereign in formulation but not the only kind of reason, and a formal axiology and Praktik are owed.

## **II. Section. Formal Axiology**

### **§ 9\. Apriori Motivation-Laws as Laws of Rational Consequence. Motivational Connections between the Logical and the Axiological Sphere**

The second Abschnitt begins to build the formal axiology promised, and Husserl opens it from the means–end result of § 6, now generalized into a theory of *Motivation* (motivation) and *Konsequenz* (consequence). Determinate analogies emerged between analytic-logical thinking, as it holds sway in syllogistic inference, and the quasi analytic-practical willing that holds sway in all will-behavior according to ends and means. As on the logical field, especially in the relations of mediate thinking, we spoke of analytic ground and analytic consequence, so here we speak of analytic-practical grounds and analytic-practical consequences: the practical "ground" for the means lies in the end, and the goal-resolve, the goal-intention, is to be designated the will-premiss for the means-resolve as the will-consequence-proposition.

He sets the logical norm beside the practical. Normatively-logically, with respect to the judgment-acts: the judging of the conclusion is rationally motivated by the judging of the premisses — nota bene, when the inference is one rationally carried out; or, more generally, the judging of an inference is theoretically motivated by the judging of the premisses, and this motivation is either rational or not, standing under the theoretical question of right. In this appraisal of right the formal-logical laws grounded in the form of the judgment-contents hold, functioning normatively with regard to rational judging in general so far as its rationality is determined by the form of the judgment-contents. Parallel, in the practical sphere with respect to the connections of the will-acts: the willing of the means is practically motivated by the willing of the premisses. To judgment-motivation corresponds will-motivation, which likewise must submit to the appraisal of right and stands under rules of practical rationality; these norms too must lie in a "content," in the analogue of the judgment-content or logical proposition. When the logical act of right-predication here draws out the grounds of right, it only draws them out — they lie in the will-domain as its peculiar will-right.

Husserl widens this to the most general axiological sphere. So far as values and value can in any way be spoken of, so far reaches the distinction between value-grounds and value-consequences, between presupposed values and values posited or derived upon them. The latter are values for the sake of other values; the former are, as it were, premiss-values or basic-values. The grounds here are everywhere not logical grounds but value-grounds. As for the connections of the valuing-acts, in all spheres there are connections of motivation: the basic-valuing motivates the valuing for the derived values. And motivation in all its kinds stands under the appraisal of right; it is rational or irrational and stands under norm-laws, in particular formal norm-laws that abstract from the matter of the respective end-values and means-values. Everywhere we can here speak of laws of consequence — everywhere laws of rational motivation — and correlatively the word "motive" is everywhere in place. *Konsequenz*, the rational following of value from value, is thus the first formal-axiological theme.

He notes that motivation-connections reach across the spheres. Already in the intellective domain one counts not merely judgments in the sense of certain convictions, but surmises, and also questions and doubts — various modal kinds of intellective *Urakte* (primal acts), the acts of certain belief. Motivation-laws connect not only certain belief with belief. His examples: if I believe that if A holds then B holds, and believe that B does not hold, I may not rationally believe that A holds — and so for the normative turns of all formal-logical laws. But we also have sphere-connecting motivations: whoever is convinced that A holds cannot rationally doubt whether A holds; whoever is certainly convinced that A does not hold cannot rationally surmise that A holds; whoever is sure that A is cannot rationally pose the question whether A is or is not. All this is a matter of rational consequence; and such consequence connects the intellective domain with the emotive domains as well — theoretical and valuing reason are everywhere interwoven. Here the theoretical is much the freer and more independent, for valuing reason is *necessarily* interwoven with the theoretical. This already hangs together with the fact that to every valuing act there necessarily underlie intellective, "objectivating" acts — presenting, judging, or surmising acts — in which the valued objectivities become presented and possibly stand as being or non-being, in certainty or probability. And this being-founded is not merely psychological: rather the valuing act is, in its very essence, founded in the intellective act precisely insofar as it constitutes the value-appearance. Here Husserl plants the doctrine of *Fundierung* (founding) that the third and later sections will press: the emotive act builds upon, and cannot float free of, an objectivating substrate.

He warns at once against the misreading this founding invites. The interweaving easily leads astray, since it suggests that the valuing acts are intentional only in the sense of being directed upon the presented objectivities. But, as he must still carry out, in the proper sense only objectivating acts are directed upon the objective, the being or non-being, whereas valuing acts are directed upon *values*, and more closely upon positive and negative values. The two are, to be sure, essentially fused in the matter and in the acts: values have their object-side and at once their specific value-side, the former founding the latter, and when values themselves become objects of judging cognition the value-side itself is objectivated. But with these somewhat difficult relations there hangs together a reason-motivation that links the intellective and the axiological: whoever is surely convinced that A is cannot will A; whoever is convinced that A is not cannot will that not-A be; and likewise if instead of "whoever is convinced" we say "whoever surmises with convincing probability." The talk is of course not of a psychological can or cannot but of an apriori of reason. This guards the key distinction the whole course rests on — value-directedness is its own intentionality, not a special case of object-directedness — even while affirming the founding relation.

Husserl then accumulates examples to exhibit the apriori of motivation and something of its range, tying them to the distinction of existential and non-existential liking, beautiful-valuing and good-valuing. Whoever has non-existential liking — a beautiful-valuing — in an A must rationally rejoice in the certainty that this "beautiful" is, and must grieve in the certainty that it is not; joy and grief are here rationally motivated acts. To rejoice or grieve in such cases is demanded, as it were, by rational consequence; it is a feeling-self-evidence, and indeed a rational one, deviation from which would be feeling-countersense. No normal person, of course, behaves otherwise, but the fact does not concern us: a contrary fact, in a madman, would mean as little as the occurrence of the conviction that 1 \+ 1 \= 3 in some anomalous intellect. Joy and grief are here motivated, on the one hand by the presupposed beauty-valuing, the valuing-presupposition, and on the other and in one with it by the conviction, the judgment, that the beautiful is. In similar fashion a surmise, possibly a probability-judgment, can function motivatingly under the same valuing-presupposition, and the joy then passes over into hope; apriori the degrees of probability rationally "determine" the fullness of the hopeful joy, and similarly, in the opposite case, of fear. Replacing the surmise with a doubting wavering as to the being or non-being of the beautiful, this intellectual wavering rationally motivates the feeling-wavering between hope and fear; and complete undecidedness in equilibrium of the probability-weights motivates a corresponding emotive equilibrium. Wherever, under the same valuing-presupposition, the certainty for the being of the beautiful is lacking — rather, the consciousness of uncertainty obtains — it rationally motivates the wish that the beautiful may be, and in the case of a disliked thing the negative wish that it may not be. Further motivation-propositions: if a positive judgment motivates joy, the corresponding negative judgment motivates un-joy, and conversely; and similarly for the other valuing-acts. The upshot is a whole field of apriori laws governing how, given a valuing, the doxic modalities of its object rationally prescribe the emotive responses.

### **§ 10\. Value-Laws as the Objective Expression of the Motivation-Laws. The Formal-Axiological Laws of Consequence**

Husserl now performs for the value-sphere the same move that, in logic, turns noetic norms of judging into apophantic laws about propositions: he converts the subjective motivation-laws into objective value-laws. Recalling the last lecture's run of motivation-laws, and how intellective and axiological motivation combine to function unitarily as premisses of an axiological *Motivat* (the motivated), he stresses that to each norm for the keeping of consequence — a norm meant for rational valuing — there runs parallel an ideal and objective expression that bears on axiological propositions, on the values, on what the sense of the respective kind of valuing purely ideally demands.

He gives the conversions. Subjectively we said: whoever is convinced that A is cannot rationally will A, and whoever is convinced that A is not cannot rationally flee A; to this corresponds, objectively, the proposition that it belongs to the sense of a resolve that only a not-yet-being can be its goal. Whoever values non-existentially, for whom A stands as beautiful, must rationally rejoice in the certainty that this beautiful is real and grieve in the certainty that it is not; overwhelming probability in the surmise that the beautiful is motivates hope, the fullness of the rational hopeful joy determined by the strength of the surmise. To such subjective propositions correspond objective propositions that go to the correlates and do not address the subject and its single acts. Objectively: if A is a beautiful and is in actuality, then it is a good, or it is good that A is. Now there is no more talk of the acts and their norming; "good" is here understood as the axiological correlate of rational joy, the joy-worthy. Likewise: if an unbeautiful is in actuality, it is an evil, and that it is real is bad, "of evil" — a grief-value. The non-being of an unbeautiful is good, joyful; the being of an unbeautiful is of evil. The possibility, and again the probability, of a beautiful is good, its improbability un-good; the beautiful, so far as probable, is hope-worthy, and in the opposite case regret-worthy; if an unbeautiful is probable, it is regrettable, grief-worthy. Husserl remarks that here sufficiently objectively coined expressions are lacking — ordinary language has no settled vocabulary for these value-predicates — but the objective degree of justified hope is determined by the degree of probability of the beautiful, and likewise the objective disvalue correlative to justified fear by the degrees of probability of the unbeautiful; and the non-being of a beautiful grounds the objective wish-value "the beautiful may be," the being of an unbeautiful the objective negative wish-value "it may not be."

Husserl turns to the hypothetical and causal modifications of the emotive acts, parallels of the hypothetical modifications of the intellective acts, and states a new law of transfer. If someone rejoices hypothetically — in the thought that W be — and takes into account that W would be if A were, then rationally the joy transfers itself to A, and A gains value for him; the hypothetical thought that A be then also motivates a hypothetical joy, with regard to the fact that then W too would be. So joy transfers itself hypothetically and then also thetically: if one really rejoices over W and knows that because A is, W also is, then rationally the joy transfers to A, and consequently one ought so to feel, given that one has so judged and felt. To this motivation-law there stands over against it the objective lawfulness: if W is a value, and it holds that if A is then W is, then with regard to this an A too is a value, the value-signs at A and W being correspondingly the same. The value-property transfers, in continued application of the proposition, in an ascending line, when we have existential chains — if M then N, if N then P, if P then Q, finally if Q then W — and all this holds apriori. Here we have a *Wertschluss* (value-inference) in which the one premiss is a value-fact and the other an intellectual situation, the two taken together in the value-inference through the form of the "together," the "and": W is a value, and it holds that if A then W.

Husserl insists on distinguishing this axiological "and" from the merely logical one that unites the premisses of an intellectual inference, and the analysis is among the section's most delicate. Going back to the consciousness on both sides in inferring, in the carrying-out of consequence: in the theoretical inference-consciousness, the "together" of the premisses presents a unified theoretical content. In the valuing case, by contrast, there lies on the one hand a valuing of W and on the other the judging "if A holds, W holds," and this gives in itself no unity; an intellectual unity-situation would arise if we intellectually united the judgment "W is a value" and the judgment "if A holds, W holds," but we make no intellectual inference at all.
