The unity between the act of valuing and the judging comes about through what we express as "bringing into axiological consideration (*Erwägung*)": we value W and bring into consideration that W would be if A were — and this "consideration" is no theoretical act. To bring into axiological consideration is precisely this: the theoretical conviction gains an emotive function and becomes emotion-motivating, not theory-motivating. The inference thus runs: if B is a value, then in consideration of the situation that, if A were, B would be, A too is a value. Instead of "in consideration" we also say "with regard to that," but it is by no means a simple "and" and no logical "with regard to" — and this is to be observed in the same way in all similar cases, for instance with disjunctives. The point protects the autonomy of the value-inference: its synthesis is an emotive, not a logical, uniting.

Husserl then develops the value-laws of part and whole, cause and effect. The stated value-law admits closer delimitations. If W is a value and it holds that if A is then W is, then A is, in the sense of derived, a relative value to W, a derived value; and the premiss "if A is then W is" is what needs delimitation, since the existence of W is to be necessarily bound with the existence of A, so that the being of A draws after it that of W. This holds at any rate when A is a whole that includes W as a real part, and we then have the law that the value of a part lends the whole a relative value, lends it value for the sake of the primarily valuable part. But care is needed: a whole can contain a part that is properly only valuable, that first unfolds its value-properties, when it is *not* in the whole but separated from it and functions in new connections; then the whole has value only with regard to the part's being separable from it, and has no transferred value if the part is simply unseparable — as the gold on the sun gives the sun no use-value, being not separable and not usable for the ends for which gold is valuable. It is otherwise when a whole contains parts that unfold their value-properties already in their being woven into the whole: every part that is an actual value in the whole lends the whole itself a derivation-value, and that is what the law says; while the whole also gains derived value with regard to possible causal processes of splitting off valuable parts — though here another value-law competes, of which presently.

He extends this from concrete parts to properties. By parts here were meant concrete parts; usually one does not think of non-self-sufficient moments or properties. But concrete objects are valuable for the sake of certain properties that "lend" them value — properties like a beautiful color or a pleasing shape, which have their being non-self-sufficiently in the object, are primarily valuable, and if they are valuable the whole too is transferred-valuable. Hence the general value-law: if a property is valuable, every object having it is valuable for its sake. Combined with the previous law, it follows that value-constitutions of parts of a whole lend the whole itself derived value; and a further consequence is that such derived value-wholes lose their derived value when the value-grounding properties or parts have, through change, lost their value-grounding character — how the difference of positive and negative value is to be respected here needing no saying. Taking the existential dependence specially as real-causal consequence yields the law: if W is a value-object, a valuable state of affairs, and A is a cause of W (the state of affairs W a real consequence of the state of affairs A), then the value of W transfers to A, from the effect to the cause, from the real consequence to the real ground; so what causes a value, even merely co-causes it, is by virtue of this relation itself a derived value, and of like sign; and in constant application the value of an effect transfers to the whole ascending series of causes — though nothing is thereby said about value-magnitudes, about relations of value-increase or value-decrease, which have so far been left wholly out of account.

Husserl rounds out the formal laws with their negative and equivalence forms. Modifying the general law: if W is a value and it holds that if A is then W is *not*, then A is a relative disvalue — so that a hindering, negative cause for W is a derivation-value with respect to W but with opposite sign; or: if W is a value, then under the presupposition that only one of W and A can be, A is a disvalue, and under the presupposition that only both together can exist, A too is a derived value of like sign — the laws then evidently containing superfluous components. Apart from causality: if it is value that S is P, it is disvalue that S is not P; if a state of affairs is value, one incompatible with it, excluding it, even logically excluding it, is disvalue; and if a state of affairs is value, every equivalent state of affairs is value with like sign — though one cannot say that every analytically contained state of affairs is value, since not every such must contain the value-grounding moments. We find here, then, formal laws grounded in the essence of value: at least the most general formulas, which speak only generally, without drawing in real thinghood, of value and disvalue, of being-conditioned, compatibility and incompatibility, are purely formal laws grounded purely in the most general idea of value and value-objectivity. They treat, in such generality, of derived values — values by virtue of other values, values on the presupposition that something is already value. So there are evident laws of value-following, evident laws for value-inferences, and correlatively norms for valuing inference, which say: having held a W for value and bringing such and such existential connections into consideration, one has consequently to behave further, in one's valuing, thus and so; and under the axiological presuppositions there always appear also motivating judgments, motivating convictions or surmises, and possibly other intellective acts.

To these laws Husserl adds the law of finitude and then, crucially, marks the limit of the whole consequence-axiology. To these laws belongs also this: that every motivation-connection, every connection between basic-values and derived values, is *finite*. Value-grounding cannot degenerate into an infinite regress, no more than in the sphere of theoretical inferences; the premisses of an inference can themselves be inferred, and the premisses of the new inference again, but this cannot go on in infinitum — we must come to first grounds that are not themselves again deduced grounds. So too all value-grounding must lead to first value-grounds that are no longer, so to speak, "inferred" values, to values that are not again values for the sake of other presupposed values. But — and this is the decisive reservation — it must at once be added that one may doubt whether with the necessity of assuming non-derived values the question of the *unconditioned objectivity* of such values is already decided. Someone who has let himself be convinced that consequence in valuing is not merely an empirical peculiarity of the valuing-experiences but stands, just like consequence in judging, under apriori norms to which apriori laws for the ideal value-states correspond, may still try to maintain the following standpoint: if I value A, then under the given motivation-situations I have to value B and so on in the descending direction — reason in consequence demands this, the formal laws of consequence demand it — and I must, if A is not already value in itself but only derived, finally come to a first, in-itself-valuable A; but it is not thereby said that A is value in itself in the sense of genuine objectivity, of independence from the contingently valuing subject and its contingent mood. There are in each case unmotivated valuings, and there must be such; but one time I value, unmotivated, A and another time non-A, and just so one person values A and another non-A. One could reject skepticism in the theoretical respect and yet be a skeptic in the axiological respect, and that even though one acknowledges the consequence-axiology; that I should also have to behave consistently in the sense of having to value the same A again and again, for all eternity, no reason prescribes. What matters here is that the axioms so far do not exclude axiological skepticism; with the clarification of axiological reason in the form of axiological consequence we are not yet at an end. At any rate this is a first and important field, and we shall see how we get further. The formal-axiological consequence, in short, secures rational coherence among values but not yet their objectivity at the root — a gap the law of contradiction in the value-sphere must now be brought to address.

### **§ 11\. The Law of Contradiction in the Axiological Sphere**

#### **(a) The Reference to the Motivation-Situation in the Analogue of the Law of Contradiction in the Emotive Sphere. Value-Freedom and the Appertaining Axioms**

Husserl now seeks, in the value-sphere, the analogues of the two great apophantic principles — contradiction and excluded middle — and the search at once exposes a deep disanalogy that will become the key result of the section. Dividing, per the laws learned, non-derived and derived values, he notes that one and the same A can, according to the value-derivations in which it stands — subjectively, according to the motivation-connections in which its valuing stands — take now positive, now negative value. The fundamental fact of the value-domain is that values can *collide*: if A is a value, then relative to it everything that excludes the existence of A is a disvalue; but this excluding thing can, for other reasons, be a value. Wherever two values collide in existence, the one is, relative to the other as existentially sublating it, a disvalue, while in itself it is a value; and the balance is given by laws of value-connection and value-weighing not yet reached. This collision-fact is what blocks a simple transfer of the law of contradiction.

He states first the rough analogue. A basic law somewhat resembling the law of contradiction would run: if A is a positive value, it is not a negative value, and if a negative value, not a positive — referred to the same kind of valuing, the same value-quality. But in the axiological domain the law refers, crucially, to the respective and identically-held motivation-situation. So: with the same valuing-matter, positive and negative valuings of the same quality-genus do not without qualification exclude each other, but they do exclude each other given the same valuing-matter *and* the same motivation-situation. To cover the case where a thing stands as in itself valuable, Husserl says the valuing is then "in itself motivated," which expresses that it is not motivated by a second valuing — correlatively, that the meant value is not consciously a derived value. In this wide sense the law holds: same valuing-matter and same motivation-situation presupposed, if the positive valuing is rational the negative of the same genus is irrational, and conversely. Correlatively, with the same value-presuppositions, "contradictorily" opposed values exclude each other in the same content — for instance that, in the same content "S is P," it be joyful and unjoyful; it would be irrational, an axiological countersense, to rejoice that S is P and, under the same motives, to grieve that S is P.

He sets the judgment-sphere analogue beside it and marks the asymmetry. With the same matter "S is P," taken as bare matter, the judging positing "It is so\!" and the judging negating "It is not so\!" rationally exclude each other — and here we do *not* add the restriction "with reference to the same motivation-situation," though it is right that to judge consequently both from the same premisses would be irrational. Here the law of excluded middle supplements: to every propositional matter one of the two really and necessarily holds, either "it is so" or "it is not so," and if the one, then not the other; the deduced "it is so" has at once the value of "it is so simpliciter." But is the situation in the emotive sphere the same — does an analogue of excluded middle hold here, in a sense ensuring that derived validity and validity simpliciter are equivalent? Here the reference to the motivation-situation must be added restrictively, and it is clear that if something like an analogue of the law of contradiction can be exhibited, its function cannot be the same; for here opposed validity-predicates with the same content are quite compatible, namely with reference to different valuing-premisses.

Husserl draws out the fundamental difference. However great the analogy — and on both sides we really found relations of consequence, of ground and consequence — it is a fundamental difference that on the logical field we know that if we derive an opposed consequence from different premiss-systems, either the one or the other premiss-system must contain something false; we know this from the principles of contradiction and excluded middle. There is no relative truth that S is P, grounded on valid premisses, compatible with a relative truth that S is not P. But where opposed relative value-validities are inferred on the basis of different value-presuppositions, it is *not* to be said that either the one or the other system of value-presuppositions must be invalid: beauty-validity or good-validity in one respect is compatible with unbeauty-validity and bad-validity in another. Connected with this: if we judge that S is P, the judging may turn out erroneous, but we then know that the opposite judging is correct — it cannot rationally turn out that no judging position-taking at all is demanded. It is otherwise in the emotive domain: there it can turn out that the valuing related to the P-being of S is not only irrational in the determinate value-quality but irrational in *every* quality, namely insofar as a value of the content "S is P" does not exist at all, neither as value in itself nor as derived value. To every judging-meaning there corresponds a true judgment, either of like or of opposite sense, independently of all relation to premisses; but not so for every valuing-meaning. Here we can state a law only to the effect that every possible valuing-matter, every value-capable content, if it is to be valued positively is not to be valued negatively — more precisely, that for every value-category, if a positive valuation under a given motivation-situation is rational and valid, the negative is invalid, and conversely — together with the law that to the essence of valuing belongs the very difference of positivity and negativity, so that if a content is rationally valuable at all it is positively or negatively valuable and value according to the relevant value-category, though only under the same motivation-situation do the positive and negative value-predicates exclude each other.

Husserl names the disanalogy in its sharpest form: there is *neutrality* (Adiaphorie) in the value-sphere but none in the truth-sphere. These are essential differences between the domain of theoretical truth and that of, so to speak, axiological truth or axiological validity. In the latter the analogue of excluded middle, according to which between yes and no there is no third, is lacking; or, conversely, in the domain of theoretical truth there is no neutrality, while in the axiological there is; or again, every conceivable situation is, with respect to being and non-being, objectively decided, while not every valuable situation is decided with respect to positive value-being and disvalue-being. The savage who in superstitious fashion reveres a fetish may value something that is not only not value in that respect, and not value in the sense he is conscious of, but is altogether "valueless"; and although there may be empirical possibilities through which the worthless thing serving him as fetish takes on some relative value, it is on the one hand questionable whether it can be said apriori of every thing that there must be relations in which it takes on derived, transferred value, and on the other hand it is not excluded that the same thing is, according to the relation, positively valuable, negatively valuable, or wholly valueless. The third possibility — value-indifference — is irreducibly built into the value-sphere.

Because of this, Husserl insists, the value-sphere needs its own axioms governing the *Adiaphora*. Value-indifference is an occurrence that valuing reason as such has to establish: whether a given situation has specific value-predicates or is value-free is to be established only by rational value-considerations, and in every axiological critique the case of possible value-freedom must necessarily be reckoned with — critique of performed valuings can apriori yield two results: either the situation is no value-situation at all, or it is one, and then it is first a question whether the relevant positive or negative predicate is the right one. This holds for every axiological region, within every category of valuation. To formulate the appertaining axioms, difficult concept-formations are presupposed, above all the concept of *matter*. In the intellective domain we distinguished, noetically and ontically, matter and quality; in the specifically logical domain of explicit logical judgments this gives the difference between the logical proposition (the logical judgment itself) and the proposition-matter or content, the abstractly identical that is conscious as content of the position "S is P\!" and of the negation, the no. In the intellective sphere the content is necessarily content of a positive or negative truth-proposition, of a valid positive or negative judgment, a valid yes or no.

Husserl states the resulting axioms of value-freedom. The same thing that functions as content of a valid yes or no, a truth or falsity, can also function as content of an axiological yes or no, can be content of an axiological proposition and possibly of an axiological validity or "truth." We then have the law that every axiological matter is either the matter of an axiological truth, of a valid axiological proposition, or of an axiological indifference — equivalently: the object or state of affairs presented by the content is, as so presented, either value at all (in the sense of a value-category laid down) or value-free, an *Adiaphoron*. The content comes into question, say, as wish-content, or is in this respect indifferent; it comes into question for beauty-valuation or not, it concerns the idea of beauty or, as it were, not at all. And the earlier law adds: if the content is valuable in the narrower sense, if it concerns valuing in this category, then it is content of a positive or a negative value-content, and there is no third. So far, then, we do have a kind of analogue of excluded middle. We need in the axiological domain an own concept "value" — the general of positive and negative value — and an own concept for axiological behavior and axiological validity, which stands over against the behavior of indifference, the privation of axiological validity, even though in a wider sense indifference too is something objectively decided by valuing behavior and value-consideration. For a complete axiomatics of the valuing-sphere one would naturally also have to state the axiom that, the same motivation-situation presupposed, valuableness in general and indifference exclude each other: if A is a value, it is not not-a-value, that is, it is no Adiaphoron — an identity-proposition.

Husserl closes the subsection by guarding the value-laws against being misread as logical trivialities, and this guard is itself a small theory of value-objectivity. One must beware of taking these laws for analytic-logical self-evidences. One might suppose that "an A is under certain presuppositions good and under the same presuppositions not good" is analytically-logically impossible. To this two things are to be replied. First, when our axioms speak of positive and negative value, of good and bad, this is by no means the same as speaking of good and not-good. In ordinary speech "un-good" and "not-good" are used also for "evil," for "bad," but that is an equivocal distinction. Analytically-logically the predicates "good" and "not-good" exclude each other in the same sense as a and non-a as arbitrary predicates — as the attributing of the a ("good") and the denying of "good"; but analytically-logically "evil" can no more be slipped under "not-good" than "black" under "not-white" (which covers anything having some other visual quality). Second, the transfer of any purely logical propositions onto value-predicates — first onto the general predicate "value" — has axiological laws as its presupposition, namely those that establish that value can be treated as a predicate at all. Only axioms of the kind just established make it possible for something like "value" to be objectively defined as a predicate. The fact of valuing, of liking, disliking, wishing, says at first nothing more than that we feel ourselves moved by objects thus and so; we objectivate so far as the presented appears, as it were, feeling-colored — the pleasing in rosy, the displeasing in gloomy light. But that we can go further and ascribe to the valued matters a predicate that belongs to them *apart from* the contingent valuing — that is something new, and it is what valuing reason contributes with its lawfulnesses. It is, for example, a fact that Gemütsakte can ground themselves on general presentations: we have disliking not only at the individual but in the consciousness of generality, as meanness in general displeases; yet the disliking we feel is a single phenomenon, as is the consciousness of generality founding it. That the emotive consciousness, having the intellective consciousness of generality underlying it, itself has its emotive generality, and that this act of generally-valuing consciousness, intellectively, that is, objectivatingly grasped, has the character of the givenness — possibly the evident givenness — of a general value or disvalue, is something wholly new, and it is presupposed if general value-laws are to be expressible at all.

#### **(b) The Law of Excluded Fourth for the Basic-Values. The Objectivity of Values**

Husserl recapitulates the result so far and then supplies the principle that secures value-objectivity. The analogues of contradiction and excluded middle in the axiological sphere consist in this: lawfully, with the same valuing-matter and with reference to one and the same axiological category, under the same value-presuppositions, positive and negative value-quality, and both of these together with the case of Adiaphorie, exclude one another in validity; but one cannot say that with the same matter either positive or negative quality holds, but only that one does *when a quality holds at all*. Here there are three apriori possibilities: positive quality, negative quality, or value-freedom.

He now isolates the basic-values to win a sharper principle. Instead of relating value-positings to value-presuppositions — instead of moving in the sphere of value-derivations and including the non-derived values only as limit-cases — let us consider these limit-cases themselves, excluding value-derivations. Then we can say: if M is in itself value, it is not in itself a disvalue and not in itself an Adiaphoron, and conversely, according to all disjunctive combinations. "Being-in-itself-value" is understood, after what has been said, in the sense that the value-being is not derived from another value-being, so that no values are presupposed in order first to posit the relevant value — which does not exclude that the value-being hangs on presuppositions, only that these presuppositions are not themselves axiological but consist, say, merely in a being or non-being.

Husserl draws the decisive step that converts mere consequence into objectivity. We said earlier that all value-derivation leads to last basic-values that are not themselves derived. But it is not merely the case that, once the valuing of an A is factually performed, it is in consequence rational to value B, so that B is relatively value if one presupposes and lets stand the A. Rather it is to be claimed as an *evidence* that one can express this objectively: "If A is a value, then B is a value"; and further that one can say: "Every value-presupposition is objectively to be decided as to validity and non-validity." To this belongs, first, that if M is the matter of the value-positing A, it can be said apriori: if M is the matter of a positive value-positing, then, if this positing is valid, the corresponding negative or adiaphoric positing is invalid, and conversely, according to all disjunctive combinations — just what we stated lawfully as valid for any matter whatever. Parallel to this we set up the axiom: if M is any matter, then — always within any axiological region — one of three cases is true: M is the matter of a positive in-itself value, or of a negative, or is in itself value-free. So for the sphere of in-itself-values and value-freedoms we have here an analogue of the law of contradiction and of the law of excluded middle — only that the latter is here a *law of excluded fourth* (*Gesetz vom ausgeschlossenen Vierten*), which then, as we shall presently hear, transfers to the derived values, which thereby become objective values. Because the basic possibilities are three (positive, negative, indifferent), the disjunctive principle excludes a fourth rather than a third.

He spells out what this secures. With these propositions the strictness and proper objectivity of validity for the axiological sphere is stated. It is said that if M is any matter, it cannot merely be the content of a valuing behavior — a behavior of positive, negative, or indifferent valuing — but that to the being-held-for-value there corresponds an *objective* value-being, an objective positive or negative value-being in the narrower sense, or an objective valuelessness. For with reference to this, every derived value and every derived value-freedom gains its objectivity and its not-merely-hypothetical validity: every basic-value in itself then has, with co-reference to value-deriving relations such as those between whole and part, its value-consequences, and these hold as consequences — objectively, in itself, independently of subjectivity — actually, when the basic-value is actually valid, that is, when it is in itself actually value. What we said about the possibility of value-conflicts remains standing, since it is not excluded that something be at once value in itself and relatively disvalue with respect to a value-derivation in relation to other positive or negative values. But what stands fast, if the axioms are right, is that "value" does not dissolve into subjectivity and in this sense into the relativity of valuing, as if what is value for the one were not value for the other and indifferent for a third. As little as there is a true and false *for someone* — though there is well a being-held-true-and-false-by-someone — so little is there, and in the same sense, a beautiful and ugly, a good and bad, a wish-worthy or flee-worthy *for someone*.

Husserl forestalls the obvious objection from relativity of circumstance, distinguishing two senses of "for someone." One must always ask what the complete matter is and what the valuing-category, and again whether the matter is valued in itself or in what value-derivation and under what presuppositions it is considered; what under certain presuppositions is a wish-value, wish-worthy, can under others be indifferent or flee-worthy — but, the presuppositions held fast, all is objectively determined. One must here attend to the sense of "value for the one, disvalue for the other," "good for the one, bad for the other." This concerns the relation of the *meant* value to the act of meaning and to the person who means. Something wholly other is the relation between a person and a person-value, or between persons and the utilities related to them: the valuing can of course relate to the person, to their deeds, to what is good and bad for them under the presuppositions specially obtaining for them — but then it is precisely determined in itself what is useful and good for this person in their situation, the person here belonging to the valuable situation, the valuing-matter relating to it. One would not, after all, derive a relativity in the sense of a subjectivity of truth from the fact that what is true for the one person is false for the other because the one can be blind and the other not: as the person is here the "object about which" the judgment-matter, the truth-matter, relates, so similarly may it stand with the person in relation to the axiological matter. Husserl notes that the standpoint of objectivism — which says at once idealism — must here, as in the intellective sphere, be fought through against the attacks of relativistic, now psychologistic, now anthropologistic and biologistic skepticism; that is a chapter of its own, into which he will not enter, treating the battle as a decided one. He flags in a footnote the terminological complication that, unlike "true" and "false," the word "value" is multiply ambiguous — shifting its sense according as objects, or matters, are called value — so that on the axiological side one must carefully distinguish the objects that have value, the matters as axiological contents, their axiological quality, the resulting axiological proposition, and the axiological state of affairs or value (*Wertverhalt*), exactly as on the intellective side one distinguishes proposition and state of affairs.

### **§ 12\. The Laws of Value-Comparison**

#### **(a) Laws for Value-Increase-Relations and for Value-Collections**

Husserl turns to a body of formal laws that have no analogue in the narrower apophantic-logical sphere — at most in the formal logic of probabilities — namely the laws bearing on *rank-relations* of values. He ties them to Brentano's brilliant *Vom Ursprung sittlicher Erkenntnis* (1889), who first formulated such laws and whose book in fact gave the impulse for all of Husserl's own attempts at a formal axiology. Brentano, to be sure, stands on psychological ground and recognized neither here the possibility and necessity of an ideal and formal ethics and axiology, nor in logic that of a formal logic of meaning; but this does not prevent his containing the fruitful germs called to further development. The acknowledgment is exact about the debt and about the divide: Husserl takes Brentano's value-laws but lifts them out of psychology onto the ideal, apriori plane.

He lays down the general framework of comparison. Between values there can obtain *Steigerungsverhältnisse* (increase- or gradation-relations), and to these, as to all such relations, belong three exclusive possibilities, the relation-types "equal," "more," "less." From the outset it must be said that we cannot demand that values in the widest sense be comparable in these gradation-relations in every respect; we must restrict ourselves to values of one value-region, or value-category. It seems to make no proper sense to compare a wish-value with a liking-value, or an existential value with a non-existential one — a good-value with a beauty-value — and to say the one is more wish-worthy than the other is liking-worthy. Holding to the bounds of one category, general gradation-relations obtain, and so far as they obtain the axioms belonging to the essence of all gradation-forms hold: (a \= b) → (b \= a); likewise for \> and \<; a ≥ b and b ≥ c → a ≥ c; equal substituted for equal gives equal, but only in the relations of summation. Hence "equal" in general cannot really be regarded as an equality but only as neither greater nor smaller — a reservation that will matter when substitutability is at stake.

Husserl now states the value-specific laws, beginning with Brentano's preference-law and at once correcting it with his noetic/ontic distinction. In Brentano we find the proposition: "A good and recognized as good is to be preferred to a bad and recognized as bad." Husserl would here, as everywhere, distinguish noetic and ontic laws. Noetically one can say, more generally than Brentano: "It is rational to prefer a held-for-good to a held-for-bad." But this needs limitation: if the held-for-good is in truth not good, the preference is objectively not right. How is the antinomy — which obtains for every kind of value — to be resolved? If I have already held something for good and something for bad, then it is the demand of rational consequence to prefer the held-for-good to the held-for-bad; a reason of preference is motivated by the good-holding and bad-holding acts as such, and this reason remains in force even if the good-holdings and bad-holdings are themselves irrational — which does not conflict with the value of the preference being conditioned by the values of these good-holdings. We can also say: the preference of the held-for-better is, considered in itself, a value, but this value is sublated if the value-holdings of the motivating acts are false; only when we abstract from the value-question on the side of the *Motivate* do we accord the preference a value simpliciter. His example, in the practical sphere: the purchase of a forged Raphael Madonna over against the genuine picture of a third-rank painter — we say the man chose and bought irrationally. It is as in logical inference: since the judger believed in the truth of the premisses, it was quite rational that he believed in the correctly derived conclusion; the "inferring was rational," the judging of the conclusion was rationally motivated, but this reason undergoes a devaluation through the unreason in the premiss-appraisals. So too in the axiological sphere the reason in consequence is indeed a reason and the value indeed a value, but one that can undergo either corroboration or invalidation, and in the latter case an annihilation through the demonstration of the disvalue of the presupposed values, the reversal of the values that came to positing in the premisses. Hence the law: the rationality of a consequence undergoes a value-increase through insight into the rationality of the premisses, and undergoes not merely a diminution but a devaluation through the insight that the premisses must reverse their value-predicates.

He specializes to practical preferring and then generalizes to value-rank as such. It is rational, in the sense of consequence, to prefer a held-for-practically-good to a held-for-practically-bad; and it is not merely consequent but unconditionally rational to prefer, in willing and acting, a *rationally* held-for-good to a *rationally* held-for-bad. To this corresponds, correlatively, the ontic law that every practically good considered in and for itself is practically better than every in-itself practically bad. And we can stay in the more general sphere and state the law for values in general in relations of value-comparison and preference: every in-itself value is, within its category, more valuable than every in-itself disvalue — every in-itself beautiful more valuable than every in-itself unbeautiful. With regard to a determinate value-derivation, referred to an identically-held basic-value, we can likewise say that the derived beautiful is better than the derived ugly; everywhere the beautiful as such, with its presuppositions held fast, is more valuable than the ugly.

Husserl reports and refines a series of Brentano's quantitative laws, recasting them in symbolic form. Brentano's law that the existence of a good is better than the existence of an evil, and conversely with non-existence, Husserl somewhat generalizes: if a beauty-value is more valuable than another such value, regardless of the signs, then the existence of the former is more valuable than that of the latter — S1 \> S2 → (E(S1) \> E(S2)), and conversely the non-existence of S2 \> the non-existence of S1. Brentano's law that a good alone is better than the same good in admixture with a bad, G \> G \+ S, Husserl prefers to render more exactly, first excluding the proper mixture, the composition: the existence of a good alone is better than at once the existence of this good together with that of an evil; the existence of two arbitrary goods together is better than the existence of one of them alone; the existence of a good and an evil together is better than that of the evil alone. In symbols: E(G) \> E(G \+ U); E(G \+ U) \> E(U); E(G \+ G1) \> E(G); E(G \+ G1) \> E(G1). From these the consequence for any number of members follows deductively: their sum, so to speak, is better than any partial sum, and the sum-value decreases when an evil enters the sum. Husserl adds the zero-law: if α is an Adiaphoron, then G \= G \+ α, so E(G) \= E(G \+ α), and any sum keeps its value unchanged when members are "increased" by Adiaphora — the Adiaphoron plays the role of zero. And he notes the proportionality of equivalent goods: if wholly equivalent goods enter a sum, the sums formed behave like magnitudes, so that the existence-sum of two such goods is twice as much worth as the existence of one alone, the collective existence of four equivalent goods twice as good as that of two. Finally he qualifies substitutability: in existence-sums "equal" values may be substituted for one another, so that goods belonging to a group with possible gradation-relations function such that the case where two goods are neither greater nor smaller relative to each other is to be treated as an equality — for we cannot without further ado assert that the "gradation-equal" possesses the full properties of equality, substitutability in every respect.

He distinguishes coexistence from consequence as two sources of summatory value-law. The summation-laws were grasped as laws for the summation of goods and evils in coexistence — we considered the together, the simultaneous existing; coexistence is rather to be understood simply collectively and may refer to a being-together at the same or at different times. But there is yet another form of connection of existence with existence that grounds analytic value-laws: to a good or evil there can in turn attach good and evil consequences, and one can designate this connection analogically as summatory, for then the summation-laws hold again in respect of form, now of course to be stated as new laws. Namely: if G is an existential value and Fg a good existential consequence of it, this raises the value of G — G is better with such a consequence than without it, and every new such consequence creates a new and higher value for G. Likewise an evil consequence lowers the value of G, and each further evil consequence anew; on both sides immediate and mediate value-consequences come equally into account, and good and evil consequences, which can arise from one and the same good, behave as in the value-sums generally, like positive and negative magnitudes. One may also take, instead of a positive existential value G, an existential disvalue as starting point, which is diminished by a good consequence and raised, in the negative sense, by a bad one. So there obtain lawful relativities in the value-domain: if a matter is value, the value-being can be manifoldly determined with regard to its own value-grounding moments and again with regard to its connection with other things, with value-consequences; and so far as things have such value-determining connections, we already have cases of value-compositions, of axiological wholes — which Husserl now proposes to consider in all their kinds.

#### **(b) The Different Relations between the Value-Whole and the Value-Components: Value-Summation and Value-Production. The Significance of Time-Extension and Intensity for the Determination of Value**

This subsection contains the doctrine Melle singles out as genuinely new in the 1911/1914 redaction — the distinction between *Wertsummation* (value-summation) and *Wertproduktion* (value-production) — and Husserl builds it on a refined theory of whole and part. The relations of wholes and parts give occasion, axiologically, to the following distinctions. If the whole is a value, value can be spoken of among the parts in different senses, and here the earlier accounts need essential supplements. If a part has value in and for itself — a value not bound precisely to its being-part in this whole and yet effective in this being-part — it is apriori "relevant" for the value of the whole: every modification that alters it in its value-character also influences the value of the whole, where "modification" includes the possible separation and removal of the part. Such parts Husserl calls *axiological components* of the value-whole; and going conversely from the whole, its value can depend on the own value of any of its parts, these own values, so far as they determine the whole's value, forming its axiological or value-components. On the other hand the whole can, and in general will, have parts that do not themselves have value but are preconditioning for its constitution or for the constitution of own-valued parts; so far as they are preconditioning for values they have a value too, a consequence-value, but they are no value-components — they have "mere precondition-value." Value-components could themselves be wholes whose value depends on value-components and could contain alongside them other components that have properly no value in themselves; and in the sphere of in-itself-values we evidently come down to *pure* value-components, to properly and purely value-grounding properties.

Husserl now draws the central distinction. Considering the relations between value-wholes and their value-components, two things are to be distinguished. It can be that the whole has value only with axiological regard to its possessing those value-componenting parts, and so that the merely general possessing of these parts determines the value of the whole exclusively in the manner of value-transfer: the connection of the value-founding parts is to have no axiological significance beyond their being connected, and through the connection as a connection of just this kind no peculiar axiological product is to arise. The second case is that precisely this excluded thing takes place: through the peculiar connection of the parts into a whole of this kind, and through the way the own values of the parts axiologically cooperate, a value-unity is to arise that is *more* than the collective unity of the value-components — a unity that does not, as in the former case, simply contain the values of the parts and merely connect them, but that creates, on the basis of the mutual influencing of these values, a new thing, dependent on them indeed and so not composed by them in the proper sense, but composed rather in the way we speak of composition in music. Every tone-harmony, and likewise a harmony of colors, is a fitting example: the sensible and feeling-values of the single elements contribute to the total value in a certain sense, but they do not compose it; they only found it as something essentially new over against them. Imagine the elements arbitrarily shuffled together, and each still has its content and, considered in itself, its value-character; each such whole has, with regard to the having of these value-elements, a relative derivation-value. But over against this, every combination grounds an axiological whole with an axiological unity-character founded in the elements yet by no means built up summatorily from them: a certain combination yields the beauty of harmony, which on alteration of the combination turns into disharmony or confused chaos. Here in-itself-beautiful and \-ugly, good and bad, can ground an axiological unity, indeed a positively valuable one, that is not damaged but heightened by the co-givenness of the bad; the omission of an in-itself displeasing element or complex of elements can lower rather than raise the positive value of the harmony, and likewise the omission of an in-itself beautiful, or its replacement by something in itself less beautiful, can raise rather than lower the value of the whole. Here, then, G \+ G′ \< G and G \+ U \> G can hold, instead of the summatory inequalities. The value-producing whole thus violates the summation-laws precisely because the parts' values interact rather than merely add.

Husserl fixes the consequence for the scope of the summation-laws and corrects Brentano accordingly. There are thus essential differences of axiological wholes, according as they have the character of summatory value-wholes or not — equivalently, according as they are wholes of value-components that are values in the whole just as they are for themselves alone, not influencing one another in the unity of the whole and founding no new unity-values, or according as this is just not the case. In the latter case the value-whole is, as to its value, a mere derivation-value of the value-grounding components, and for this kind of whole the summation-laws plainly hold — formally the same laws stated earlier for value-collections, for the values of collective existence of values, since the coexistence of a plurality of values and the existence of a whole of many mutually non-influencing values stand on a par. So, for a summatory value-connection, the law holds that a good-sum is better than a single good of this sum and than every summatory diminution of this sum; the summatory part-good is less worth than the summatory whole of mere goods; the summatory weaving-in of bad is always value-diminishing. In all value-appraisals of wholes — including in the question of derivation-values — these fundamental differences between value-summation and value-production must be heeded; and accordingly Brentano's laws are essentially improved by Husserl's distinction, since only under restriction to summatory values can and must they be upheld.

Within this restriction Husserl adds the laws of time-extension. The *Zeiterstreckung* (time-extension) of a good or evil, with regard to the possibility of time-division, is to be treated like a sum of part-values corresponding to the time-parts — Brentano too had listed time-extension among the summation-laws. So, symbolizing two time-durations by t1 and t2, if t1 \> t2 then the good extended over the duration t1, written Gt1, is greater than the good Gt2 referred to the duration t2; and we can say Gnt \= n·Gt: considered in itself, when nothing else enters into value-consideration, a good extended over n-fold time is in existence n times as worth as the one extended over single time, and equivalent too to n equal goods coexisting in a summatory unity, all extended over the single duration. He illustrates with a fiction: suppose there existed in the world a noble personality whose value preserves itself unchanged through time; in general it will be a radiation-point of significant effects for its environment, and the value-streams issuing from it will in equal times be very unequal, so that one cannot in general say the personality's good-value in the world simply sums itself according to the times. But fictioning a time-stretch in which such inequalities fall away, and taking the pure existential value of the personality for itself under the presupposition that it stays equal in equal times, we can indeed say, and with evidence, that the time-extension works like a summation — that twice-as-long being yields a doubling of the value — and equally that, under corresponding equality-fictions, the doubled time of one personality is equivalent to the existence of two personalities of equal value referred to the single duration, abstracting from all other value-determinations. Of course the ideal limit-case is in general not realized: it generally means much more for the world if several personalities work side by side than if one works correspondingly longer; nor do the times come into account simply as mathematical magnitudes, since below a certain duration a personality can achieve nothing significant and beyond a certain time the possibility of full and rich activity does not reach. But taking the pure existential values in themselves, abstracting from the consequences, or taking the consequences in as ideally equal, together with the time-division that the essence of the lasting good permits, one must indeed say that duration-extension grounds the character of a summatory value-magnitude, so that the summation-laws hold.

Husserl treats intensity analogously, with a sharper delimitation than Brentano's, and then marks the boundary of the analytic sphere. An analogous significance to that of time-extension Brentano attributes to *intensity*; here we must delimit more exactly than he did. We presuppose a property value-grounding in the proper and pure sense and assume further that something like intensity dwells in it, that it is, most generally expressed, capable of increase — and by restricting ourselves to the purely value-grounding, we have said that the increase-capability does not refer to moments merely interwoven with that in which the value properly grounds. Under these circumstances we can say that with the increase of the value-grounding the value itself increases — first the value primarily belonging to the property, and consequently the value of the whole that has the value-grounding moment, exclusively so far as it has it and so far as no further value-determining and value-modifying complications enter. Intensities are not like time-magnitudes, but there are intensity-distances, and these then play the analogous role to the time-stretches. Brentano stresses — what, given Husserl's whole orientation, scarcely needs stressing — that one must not confuse the *intensity of the value*, the difference of more-or-less worth belonging to the value itself, with the *intensity-property of the valuing*, as if, when a gradation-relation between values is established, this meant a livelier or less lively liking, wishing, valuing: "something is more wish-worthy than another" does not say that the wishing is or should be livelier — though of course there are also norms for the liveliness of valuings, for instance that it is incorrect to rejoice less lively over a higher value than over a lower. Husserl then registers a boundary: with the drawing-in of time and intensity the properly analytic law-sphere is exceeded, for in that sphere the talk is of values in general, and whether temporal and intensive things exist and are valuable does not concern, but rather restricts, the sphere of value in general. The strictly formal axiology abstracts even from time and intensity.

Husserl closes the section with a main law of derived comparison, an objection to it, and the noetic counterpart of the whole apparatus. A further main law is this: if A1 is a derived value with respect to W1 and A2 a derived value with respect to W2, then, if W1 \> W2, "with regard to that" A1 \> A2; and under this falls the will-sphere law that if M1 is value as means for W1 and M2 as means for W2, then, if the will-goal W1 \> W2, with relation to that the means M1 \> M2. Against this law an objection was once actually made to Husserl, that if it held all derivations of the same value would have to be equivalent, which is plainly not so; but on close consideration the consequence does not follow. Different means of the same end are indeed not equal, but whatever the rank of the values of the means of one and the same end may be, this holds: when they are considered purely as derivation-values of their end, they can all stand equal in this, that they are all inferior to all the means of a more valuable end — and that they are outright practically reprehensible follows from a further law that will occupy us presently. Finally Husserl flags that the laws stated have been *ontic*, relating as formal laws to "higher worth" and "lower worth"; but value-comparison itself is carried out on the basis of peculiar acts of *preference* and *subordination* (Bevorzugung and Hintansetzung), which are themselves Gemütsakte. All logical assertions and judgments about them require, for their demonstration, the performance of these Gemütsakte and must not be confused with them; value-comparison, as the common title for preference and subordination, we therefore count in the emotive sphere, and instead of comparison we also say value-weighing (*Wertabwägung*). When we attend to and evaluate these value-weighing acts themselves, they stand, like simple acts, under reason-appraisal, so that parallel to the ontic laws there are noetic ones — laws of rational preference and subordination. Thus it is a law of consequence in the peculiar sense that if one has, in valuing weighing, preferred W1 to W2, one must rationally value every derivation-value of W1 higher than every derivation-value of W2. Preferring and subordinating, moreover, relate to each other as positive and negative Gemütsakte, for which, as for every positivity and negativity, the law holds that if the preferring is rational the subordinating of the same matter is irrational, and conversely — correlatively, that if the preference obtains, the subordination does not, and conversely. All this belongs to a worked-out noetics of the Gemütsakte, which, just like the noetics of the logical acts, is by no means settled by the corresponding ontic and noematic laws through mere conversion of them. With this the formal axiology stands, and Husserl turns to the will, whose own peculiar laws the analytic of value has not exhausted.

## **III. Section. Toward the Phenomenology of the Will**

### **§ 13\. The Will-Sphere in the Narrower and Wider Sense**

The third Abschnitt is the willensphänomenologische expansion that Melle identifies as the 1914 course's distinctive addition, and it shifts register from formal law to descriptive analysis of the act of will. Husserl announces the need: we have learned many laws claiming axiological significance in the widest, most general sense, but the different act- and value-spheres comprised under the title *Gemüt* do not stand throughout under the same lawfulnesses; the different kinds of reason have only in part formally identical laws, and in the sphere of desiring acts, and still more in the will-sphere, quite peculiar laws appear. For a sure working-out of the "will-logic," he says, it would be best to state all the will-laws systematically and separately, rather than rest content with more general axiological laws that include some will-laws and then seek special supplementary laws holding only for the will-sphere; only so would one gain a complete and clear overview. But here he cannot proceed so, since it would bring great cumbersomeness — so the section proceeds descriptively rather than by a closed axiomatics.

He lays down a first general point: the will-domain is to be grasped in a fitting breadth, just as the domain of belief, of *Doxa*, is grasped correspondingly broadly. On the doxic side we have in view not only certain belief but its manifold modalizations — on the one hand unbelief, belief-rejection, and on the other the holding-for-possible in the sense in which something solicits us as being, in which we are in a certain way inclined to believe, in the consciousness that "something speaks for the being," without yet actually believing; the objective then stands not simply as being but as "well possible." This is possibility in a definite sense, the sense in which one weighs possibilities against possibilities, as in the doctrine of probabilities; and here too belongs the believingly preferential "inclining" toward one possible among several offered, the surmising of it, or instead the being in a disjunctive, questioning, or doubting attitude. This deliberating and weighing is itself to be reckoned to the belief-domain, provided we exclude all intentional, willful doing, however deeply the two are interwoven.

Correspondingly, by a will-act in the widest sense Husserl understands not only willing in the narrowest sense but manifold modalities, among them ones peculiar to the will. Thus a distinction is to be made between a willing that is not itself already acting-willing — every willing in the sense of resolving oneself to something — and a willing in the sense of an acting-willing, for example in the execution of a resolve formed earlier. Other modi of the will-sphere also confront us, mostly such as have their analogue in all other act-spheres. Willing in the ordinary narrower sense is positive and a willing in will-certainty: I am simply resolved, or I even act — the willing enclosed therein is always positive and will-certainty. But there are also other occurrences of the specific will-sphere, which already contrast with these emphatic expressions: clearly we have a negative willing as well, and likewise modi of will-uncertainty.

In this last respect, Husserl notes, the division between willing and wishing, striving, desiring becomes unclear, and he draws it sharply. Mere wishing contains nothing of willing, contains nothing of practical modalities, and is not itself a practical act, a will-act in the widest sense. A mere wishing is present where the wished is not in the least conscious as practically realizable — where it is conscious neither in certainty nor in a wholly problematic way as such, not even in the mode of the perhaps-realizable, or where it is even conscious as unrealizable. What can be wished is "anything possible," not merely anything practically possible. A merchant strives for riches; one cannot say that he wills them in the narrowest sense, for he can will only what is conscious as the practical end of a will-way thither, in certainty or probability — and in each deal to which he resolves himself, or which he carries out, a will in this determinate narrow sense lives. Yet his striving for wealth is not a mere wish, though it is also a wish: he does hold the goal of the striving to be "possible," and although he cannot tell himself that his powers suffice, that the chance-subjected circumstances of fortune will be sufficiently favorable, still it could be, perhaps it will succeed. However uncertain and indeterminate the way to this goal, and however indeterminately the goal itself is set, it is set as a goal and could be set — and this wealth-striving is already a will-modus going beyond the wish, and indeed a positive one.

### **§ 14\. Wish and Will**

Husserl deepens the wish/will distinction begun at the close of the last lecture. Both often appear in interweaving, but the mere wishing, however lively, is still no willing. This shows too in that the *graduality* of willing — the differences of will-tension peculiar to it — need not at all run parallel to the graduality of wishing: something passionately wished can be willed without energetic will-tension, in a slack willing, as it were. Here, he cautions, we speak of gradualities in the act itself and not of the habitual dispositions, which likewise have their gradualities — one personality can have firm, steadfast will-directions, another less firm ones, changing its will-directions more easily and standing more or less firmly directed with respect to the same will-goals. This habitual firmness, and likewise the habitus of pursuing goals with energy, are of course to be separated from the modifications of greater or lesser tension that the single will-act admits.

It is an open question, Husserl says, whether every willing includes a desiring, a wishing; at any rate the two are often interwoven. The will to set out on a journey does not sublate the desire for it, and even during the realizing execution the desire for what is further, or for the goal, may keep recurring or may lie as a constant under-layer in consciousness. One says that willing without desiring is unthinkable, that I can will nothing that is unwished by me, and that if it is in itself unwelcome it must be wished for the sake of something else. But that is not without further ado a *real* implication of a wishing in the willing: it would indeed be irrational to will something that were not, either in itself or for the sake of another, wish-worthy, so that an implication in the sense of reason is present — but for that an *actual* wishing need not be contained in the willing as substrate. "It is wished" does not always mean it is actually wished, but often has the sense of: it is wish-worthy. The distinction between actual wish and rational wish-worthiness, already familiar from § 6's treatment of means-willing, is thus carried into the heart of the will-analysis.

Husserl draws a structural consequence from the compatibility of the two. The very fact that one can wish a goal and at the same time will the same goal proves with certainty that the qualitative peculiarities of the act-characters wishing and willing are *not* coordinate differences of one and the same genus — for such differences exclude one another essentially, as two color-species exclude each other as covering the same patch of surface in the same duration. Wishing and willing are therefore distinct genera of act, not two species of one genus.

He places both within the general doctrine that all acts are, in their way, directed upon being. Wish and will both go in a certain way upon a being, as in a certain way all acts go upon being. Belief is the meaning of being; the modalizations of belief are meanings of a modalized being, of a "being-possible," "being-probable," and so on. The Gemütsakte too are to be grasped as being-modalizations, though of a new dimension: the wishing means an "it may be," the willing an "it shall be" — where the "it shall" is, to be sure, to be taken in a definite sense. The will, one says, goes upon *realization*; only in certain cases, on a certain detour, does the will go upon something already being in advance and conscious as being — namely as the will that something stay as it is. Excluding these cases provisionally and holding to the will-direction upon something not conscious in advance as actually being, the will, one then says, cannot go upon the ideal but only upon the real, and not upon the past but upon the future — in contrast to joy and to wish. Someone can rejoice that an ideal mathematical relation, the validity of a proposition and proof, turns out so, or can wish it; and here the joy and wish can, instead of upon the real turning-out, also go upon the ideal being itself, which for its part lies beyond all willing and making, the interest in the bringing-out of a truth transferring into an interest in the being of the pretended truth itself. But the will is here bound: the ideal sphere is closed to it, and likewise that of the past. This restriction of the will is, Husserl suggests, again a restriction of reason — though whether a madman might finally muster a will-act referred to the past or the ideal, we shall not be able to assert. The will's confinement to the real and the future is thus a rational, essential law of willing, not a mere empirical limit.

### **§ 15\. Action-Will and the Resolve-Will Directed at the Future**

This section contains the course's most striking descriptive thesis — that the will is *creative*, that its certainty about the future is not a prediction but a making. The will goes upon the future, Husserl observes, in a peculiar way distinct from all other acts concerning the future. Joy over a future presupposes the belief in the future being, the expectation of the future underlying it as substrate, and the joy-valuation goes upon what is already set as future-actual in the underlying belief. The will directed upon the future also implies, in a certain way, the belief in the future, but it does not *presuppose* this belief, does not imply it as substrate. Whoever wills — and, more exactly, positively wills, in the mode of will-certainty — that something shall happen, thereby also believes that it will happen. In the resolve to travel to Paris there of course lies, in a certain way, that the journey to Paris will be reality, and still more in the action-will that lives in the acting execution of the journey. But the future journey, the future stay in Paris, is not first certain-being and then, in addition, willed as something certain in advance; on the contrary, were it already certain-being in advance, it could not be willed at all. Instead of being certain in advance, it is certain only by virtue of the will-certainty: the will as will-certainty sets the future in a manner that first confers on it, for consciousness, the certainty of being. Consciousness does not say, as it were, "it will be, and accordingly I will it," but "because I will it, it will be." In other words, the will speaks its creative "Let it be\!" The will-positing is the positing of realization — but realization here means not merely a becoming-actual but a making-actual, the accomplishment of realization, and this is something wholly its own, having its source in the very peculiarity of the will-consciousness and letting itself be understood only there.

On the act-side, accordingly, the founding-relation is reversed. Instead of the willing being founded upon the belief of the future being, it is rather the belief of the future that springs forth from the willing; and if the willing is an action-willing, then in every phase in which the realization is performed — in which the becoming-real has the mode of now-being-real — this now-real is characterized as originarily created, as made, while correlatively the perception-appearance and the perception-certainty have the character of one born out of the willing. Whereas an ordinary perception has the character of a passivity, in which we take in something that is just there, turn toward something existing, the perception that appears here — that of all actual action-phases — has the character of one welling up out of the creative subjectivity, an object that is in consequence of the creative "fiat." That is the incomparable peculiarity of the will-positing as a creating positing.

Husserl then draws the essential distinction between executing and resolving will, and untangles the regress it threatens. The creative "Let it be\!" that makes up the essence of the will-positing can be an actually creating one — the will is action-will, executing will, making-actual — or it can be only directed upon creating, upon a future creating, as in every resolve directed at a future action. Sharply to be distinguished here are the will's relation to the future event and the "Become\!" referred to it, and the will's relation to the future willing, the future acting. The confusing thing is that a willing of the future willing and acting is also possible, and that the "Let it be\!" which sets the future acting thereby — but only by virtue of an apriori connection — also sets the future event, implicitly conferring the "Become\!" on it too. If the will indeed goes upon the future acting, then it is just the acting itself, the future actuality, that receives its practical positing in the present resolve; and if every willing directed upon a future were a willing of a future acting, we should evidently come upon an infinite regress. There obtain here, rather, reason-implications: whoever wills something future, more closely something that does not have the beginning of its becoming in the present, could not will it unless he willed the future action — an ambiguous saying that means, however, that the willing of the future *excludes* the not-willing, the rejecting, of the future action.

He gives the careful phenomenological resolution. Certainly the willing directed upon a future event not beginning at the now-point also includes, consciousness-wise, the future realizing acting, and indeed, really understood, a certainty-consciousness of the future acting: the future journey I have in view and for which I am now resolved is conscious to me not merely as a process but as a journey, that is, an action, and this is thoroughly necessary. But the present will, as the peculiar reality-positing of the "Let it be\!", does not set the future willing or action; rather it *sends the thesis "Let it be\!" through* it. Or, as we can also say, the will-thesis stretches into the future time-stretch and demands there a filled will-stretch through whose phases it extends; and now what the future will brings to will-positing in each phase, to future creation, is also will-posited by the present resolve-will — so that the process to be realized is what is set, and not the will or the action. Yet we have the peculiarity that the future will too, in all its continuous progress and corresponding to the future action, is characterized as future-actual and, in its future actuality, as welling up out of the present resolve-will; it too is therefore only through the will a being-becoming and has through it the certainty-character of a future being. But for all that it remains: the will-thesis is not directed upon it, but precisely upon the willed process, and only reflection teaches that the creative power of the will directed upon a future time-stretch necessarily reaches further than the proper will-theme. These are, Husserl concedes, very difficult relations that one must think through again and again; the will directed upon the future is, in a certain sense, a creative intention, and this "fulfills" itself in the executing action.

### **§ 16\. The Structure of the Action-Will**

Husserl inserts first a point about the will's substrate, then gives a temporal-phenomenological analysis of the action-will modeled on the analysis of time-consciousness. The will goes upon actuality — not ideal but individual, real actuality, where one need not think precisely of reality in the specifically substantial-causal sense, though the usual cases belong to that sphere. Each will presupposes not merely a presentation of the willed in general but necessarily a comprehensive presentation-substrate, and, despite its presuppositionlessness with respect to a being-belief in the willed, also a belief-substrate referring to real being: where reality is not already set, not somehow doxically conscious, creative realization cannot begin. Within this thingly environment I will something — something that is not yet in it shall come to be — and the will goes upon a determinate now as the beginning of a filled time-series, this now in its determinateness already pointing us to a field of actuality.

The acting is then either originary acting or acting that executes a resolve. The executing will follows, consciousness-wise, upon the resolve-will, and to its time-stretch in the elapse of time and within time-consciousness belongs the determinate now upon which it was referred as the starting-point of the process to be realized. If, in the time-stretch since the resolve, the will was not kept livingly through, it can nonetheless appear as a repeated one, setting itself in one with the earlier will re-presented in memory, and so appear as it must here, as the fulfilling action-will. Its first phase is at once actually creative: what is given in it as now-being and perceptively constituted appears as having become out of the fiat, as created. At this time-point, however, there is conscious in one a future-horizon of the still-to-be-realized — a horizon already conscious as a will-horizon, conscious in an anticipated will-continuity. The will-thesis goes not only upon the now with its creative beginning but upon the further time-stretch and its content; one with the creative present is a creative future, here constituted in the action with a peculiar originarity as such. But now the now passes over into an ever-new now, the pre-set creative future steadily transforms itself into creative present and so becomes really created; the just-created receives the character of the creative past, while on the other side the future-horizon persists but, by virtue of the bounding of the time-stretch of the envisaged accomplishment, shortens ever more. Finally the action is finished, it is at its end, and as a whole it is itself only creative past, possibly leaving over a lasting result as a work, something become through such a creative process and characterized as such.

Husserl describes the will-structure articulated in this process. During the action we have a peculiar structure of the willing: belonging to each action-phase a point of actual creating, the respective now-point with its actual now-accomplishment, and belonging to it a distinguished will-phase in which the will shows its creative originality; but belonging to each point also a generally double horizon of peculiar will-transformations in which the creative-past and the creative-future, the dispatched and the still-to-be-dispatched, constitute themselves consciousness-wise. Plus the two distinguished points: the starting-point with the first fiat, conferring as it were the creative primal-impulse, and the end-point with the character "It is accomplished" — both points distinguished by correlative one-sidednesses in their horizons. During the process, willing constantly wells up out of willing, which then passes over into the reproductions, so that in general, in the will-continuities belonging to each time-point, the willing-moments do not lie side by side but stand in continuous relations of welling-up-out-of-one-another; and the same holds, in a somewhat different way, no less for the whole will-continua in the sequence of time-points, since each new will-continuum, in the passage from the now to the new now, flows out of the previous one not merely as in original time-consciousness the now flows out of the past now, but wells up out of it by virtue of its own will-creation. In each now the will-direction and the creative "Let it be\!" goes through the continuity of the will-moments, and with each new actual creation-point a prior will-intention directed upon its content fulfills itself. The willings do not direct themselves upon the further willings; rather each will directs itself upon the things — creatively fulfilled upon the respective now-phase of the process and "intending" the whole rest of the process as to be realized. This confirms the analysis given for the will-directions of the forward-looking resolve: in the doing we do not the future will but precisely the done, although it belongs to this that will-continuities well up out of the initiating will, out of the fiat, and each new will-phase out of the previous.

Husserl closes the section by distinguishing the kinds of will-act that this structure can take. An action-will can be executing, fulfilling an earlier resolve-will — though, since every action-will in a certain way also has resolve-stretches, we do not call it a resolve, by which we understand precisely the case of a will-intention referred merely to future stretches. But an action-will need not be the execution of a resolve; it can, as a simple acting, begin straightaway. There remain in the will-sphere many further differences to discuss, and here Husserl mentions the difference between a resolve-will (*Vorsatzwille*) and the *Entschluss* (decisive resolve). The *Entschluss* in the pregnant sense points back to a will-awakening, a passage through prior will-solicitation, will-questions, and the like; the conclusion of a will-weighing is a will-position-taking as a willing in the narrower sense, and there we speak of *Entschluss*, and possibly of *Sich-Entscheiden* (deciding), a word that points specially to counter-possibilities — one decides for the one, against the other. Here, then, the will appears, the decided will, after other acts that we must address as modalities of the will have preceded. But not every will is an *Entschluss*, has the character of a decision: here belongs every will-act that in simple fashion follows a stimulus, without wavering and doubting, without deliberation and taking of sides — as when I look up, there stands my breakfast, and immediately say, I will now breakfast. Here arises, to be sure, the question of the relation of these willings that follow a "stimulus" without further ado to the drive-like activities we designate as involuntary — a question at which Husserl declines, for now, to stop.

### **§ 17\. The Parallels between Judgment-Modalities and Will-Modalities**

#### **(a) Willing in the Original Sense as Analogue of Certain Belief. The Problematic, Hypothetical, and Disjunctive Modification of Willing**

Husserl now systematizes the parallel between the modalities of the will and the modalities of belief, and he begins by delimiting what "modality" means here. Under the title "modalities" he does not mean just any modifications acts can undergo. If we take a certain belief of content A — say a predicative judgment "S is P" — it is no modality of the judgment to set against it the act of thinking-oneself-into the content "S is P" without oneself believing; that is a kind of modification found in all act-domains, just as one can think oneself into a willing without actually willing or performing any practical position-taking. Nor are the modalities the many phenomenological differences in how "the same" judgment is performed: now in living spontaneity, setting subject, then predicate, step by step; now arising unitarily as a flash, passively taken in, yet standing there in the manner of certain belief; or the differences between having, in spontaneous judging, a consciousness of the being of the state of affairs, and, after the judging-process is over, still holding the state of affairs in view; or the differences of clarity and unclarity, of judging on clear given grounds and judging that lacks all grounding, possibly determined only by a dark memory of an earlier grounding. All these are very different phenomena with analogues in every act-class — there is a willing wholly without grounding-substrate and one motivated by grounds, a willing in clear practical reason and one that includes unclear reason-motives. None of these, however important, concern us under the title "modalities," for they can vary manifoldly while we still say, in a good sense, that it is the same judgment "S is P" that is performed, determined by the same judgment-quality (the judgment-thesis, certain belief) and the same matter; and likewise we speak of the same wish "S may be P" and the same will "S be P" through manifold phenomenological transformations. We thus have now the same line of regard the logician has when he designs a *Formenlehre* of possible judgments while grasping the concept of judgment generously, so that the whole series of modifications of certain belief — holding-for-possible, holding-for-probable, doubting, questioning, deliberating, deciding — also counts as judging; and for these we have parallels in the will-sphere, where too there is a pure *Formenlehre* of will-formations taken in a wider sense, with respect to the will-acts and, parallel, to their correlates, the will-propositions and will-contents.

He runs the parallel through the basic modalities.
