Instead of judging simply, that is, being certain of a thing, I can perform an *uncertainty* — yet not as a mere privation of certainty: the thing solicits itself as being, possibly from definite conscious grounds, something speaks for the being without my having certainty, or possibly it solicits itself without my having definite grounds; the thing stands there as possible, and this possibility-consciousness is a modalization of belief, a modification of the belief-thesis that itself has thesis-character and belongs essentially to the doxic domain. Likewise in the practical sphere: instead of willing in the normal sense, I can perform a practical consciousness akin to willing and essentially characterized as a modification of a willing — the will-matter solicits itself as practically ought-to-be, possibly from definite will-grounds, possibly indefinitely. This is not a mere judgment "that I should perhaps" (where, moreover, duty, a norm, an ought-to-be in a special sense is not in question); I need not judge at all, but the relevant state of affairs stands, in a will-consciousness, peculiarly characterized, soliciting itself precisely as to-be-willed. If the possibility-consciousness in the belief-sphere is called *problematic* — not, Husserl grants, a wholly good expression — then this will-consciousness would analogically be called problematic: willing in the original sense is the analogue of certainty, where I set simply and practically as ought-to-be the "Let it be\!", whereas in the problematic behavior I have the mere solicitation of the "Let it be\!" and am only *inclined* to will, the concept of inclination to be oriented exactly by these descriptions. Further modalities are the hypothetical positing and the performing of a consequent-thesis upon a hypothetical positing — "if A is b, then C is d"; so too practically, instead of willing simply, I can perform a hypothetical willing and then upon it a consequent-willing, as in "suppose I went to Switzerland this summer, then I would go to the Engadine," which expresses itself as a hypothetical judgment, but where the hypothetical and the on-hypothesis-posited is performed, *before all judgment, in the willing*. And likewise with the disjunctive modifications of willing: "I will A or B," "I will either the sea or Switzerland" — which does not say I will the one and I will the other, and yet a will is there, and for each member a will-modality, after which the expressed disjunctive judgment is first oriented; that a willing in the narrower sense is also implied in this disjunctive-willful behavior is a matter of its own, but each disjunction-member is here not merely presented but willed, not simply but in a modalized way.

#### **(b) The Will-Question as Analogue of the Being-Question. Toward the Form-Theory of Questions**

For all modifications of judgment-consciousness, Husserl says, we find apriori parallel modifications of will-consciousness; to all the occurrences in the form-theory of propositions and the modal modifications of propositions correspond parallels for the will-propositions. He singles out the series holding-for-possible, doubting, questioning, deliberating, deciding: there is a practical doubting, practical questioning, practical deliberating and deciding that is purely the affair of the will, however much, in the founding of the will upon occurrences of the presentation- and judgment-sphere, a doxic questioning and deliberating may also play its role in the lower level. As I can theoretically doubt whether A is or B is, where each member of the doubt is presupposed as a soliciting-itself-as-being, so I can doubt in the will-sphere: in a will-solicitation A is conscious, and in just such a one B; each solicits itself as "owed," each as it were demands the willing; I waver in willing between A and B, and in the will itself the undecided being-referred-to-the-one-or-the-other is performed, the dividedness lying in the will itself characterizing will-doubt as the analogue of theoretical doubt. And as the parallel to the closely related phenomenon of the *Seinsfrage* (being-question) we have the practical question, the *Willensfrage* (will-question) — where the expression is, to be sure, ambiguous, since here as everywhere doxic occurrences can and very often do build upon the will-occurrences and express themselves in doxic question-sentences referred to the practical. To the essence of the question belongs the aiming at an answer, and this aiming fulfills itself in the actual answer; so the will-question harbors in itself the intention upon a corresponding will-answer.

Husserl pauses to develop a form-theory of questions, because the will-question can be understood only against it. Question and doubt, or question and mere holding-for-possible, are closely related but not the same. Questions can be one-membered and many-membered. There is a temptation to deny the one-membered question and regard it only as a linguistic abbreviation of a many-membered one — for if we ask "Is A?" we can indeed add "or is A not?", and there is here a relation of rational consequence, since the posing of the one question rationally justifies the supplementation to the disjunctive question — but it certainly does not need an actual transition to the counter-question: we can be simply questioningly directed upon the A-being, upon whether it is. If the questioning is genuine and not a sham questioning, a being-solicitation underlies it, and necessarily so; what consciousness-wise nothing speaks for, what does not already solicit itself as being, cannot be questionable as to its being. On the other hand the being-solicitation is plainly not yet itself a question; it does not yet contain the intention upon an answer peculiar to the question. To be more exact about the many-membered questions, usually understood as disjunctive, Husserl glances into the form-theory. As with judgments, we distinguish simple and composite questions, a question being composite (similarly to a composite judgment) when in it a plurality of questions are distinguished as ordered members, so that the question is really composed of questions — taking questions here as actual questions simpliciter and not as modalizations. It is not a matter of whether the question-*matter* is composite or simple, for simple questions can have composite matters: a question with hypothetical, disjunctive, or conjunctive matter is simple, of the forms "Is, if A is b, C d?", "Is one of A, B, or C?" — as when one asks whether one of the text-corrections proposed in a treatise is to be accepted, or, with conjunctive matter, "Have both events A and B occurred?" To be distinguished from these are *really* composite questions, composite in the form of conjunctive and of disjunctive questions: in the conjunctive question "Is A and is B?" two independently detachable questions are contained, one to each member, and it is at once a single question harboring the several in itself — conjunctive questions corresponding exactly to conjunctively composed judgments; and disjunctive questions of the form "Is A or is B or is C?" are likewise composite, each member corresponding to its own question. Here it is not asked whether one of all the matters holds — of that I may be convinced in advance, so that it is not questionable at all — and yet I ask "Is A or is B?", or indeterminately "Which of them is?" Husserl notes in passing a difference from the disjunctive judgment "Either A is or B is," insofar as that judgment is not composite, whereas the disjunctive question is always composite in the defined sense; whereas it would again not be a composite question if we took the modalization of hypothetical form, "If A is, would B then be?" He adds that the concept of the composite question, like that of the composite judgment, can be grasped more generously, by not referring composition to the unmodalized belief, the being-certain, but including the modalizations throughout — so that a disjunctive judgment would be a (doxically) composite judgment, and the phenomenon of questioning under a hypothesis composite of an assumption and a modalized question.

Husserl returns to the disjunctive question to isolate the proper essence of questioning. Disjunctive questions "Is A or is B?" plainly presuppose as their basis the doubt between A and B, but they go beyond the doubt and bring in something new in the form of the intention upon an answer — the specific questioning comes in as new. This intention does not mean an expectation of a certainty that decides for one member of the doubt, for such an expectation need by no means be present; nor is it right to seek the specific questioning in a wish directed upon such certainty, though very often a wish for an answer is given in one with the questioning. Both together, expectation and wish, and even command, we find in the ordinary case of inquiries directed at other persons — but those are complications, and above all the wish, however great the temptation, we need not draw into the concept of the question (though that is usually done). Doubting plus the wish for a certainty referred to one of the disjunctive possibilities do not make a question whether A or B is; there is a peculiar intentional tendency that we must separate out and designate as the peculiar essence of the question. With it alone the questionable constitutes itself for consciousness as such — something we must regard as a modalization of simple being, as the questioning is a modalization of belief; and once the peculiar essence of the question is given, further acts can interweave, so that the wish-question, for example, is a complication over against the pure question. Closely seen, the founding of questioning in doubting, explicitly and properly understood, obtains only in the case of explicitly performed questioning: there the doubting must first be performed, and then the new questioning intention appears, the doubting undergoing thereby a peculiar modification and not persisting in the form it had without the question. To a question can attach the process that relaxes and in a certain sense fulfills the questioning intention, terminating in the answer; and the belief that appears in this connection as answer is peculiarly characterized — what is present is not merely the transition from doubt or solicitation into certainty, but the fulfillment of the questioning intention in belief, and this characterizes it as answer. For disjunctive questions the answering process takes the form of disjunctive deliberation and possible consideration of the grounds, the most special case being the rational consideration illuminated by insight, by that clarity in which the grounds and grounding-connections come to perfect givenness and the answer thereby shows itself as the rational answer.

Husserl makes the transfer to the will explicit. This whole consideration was undertaken only to show that its essential content transfers to the will-domain. The analogue of the doxic doubt is the will-doubt, and to it the "will-question," if we use the analogical expression, relates just as the doxic question to the doxic doubt; the disjunctive will-question expresses itself in the words "Shall I this or that?", the one-membered in "Shall I this?" This question-sentence does indeed also and first express a doxic question, but no differently than assertions about valuings and values, willings and oughtnesses are first of all assertions and so doxic occurrences — all occurrences of the Gemüt- and will-sphere being possible objects of theoretical grasping, predication, the theoretical question. But the decisive point is that *before* all assertions, in the Gemüt- and specifically the will-sphere, analogues of the specifically doxic occurrences appear; and what the performance of the doxic acts presupposes in the way of structures and what it newly brings in is a theme for difficult analyses of its own.

#### **(c) The Analogies between Theoretical and Practical Deliberating, Deciding, and Surmising**

Husserl applies the form-theory of questions to choice. Our analysis has plainly prepared the analysis of the phenomena we are accustomed to call, in ordinary speech, phenomena of choice or of practical deliberation. A practical deliberation is nothing other than the performance of a will-question, and not only that but, following upon it, the performance of those modifications and supplementations of the questioning acts that lie on the way to the deciding of the question — processes of the fulfillment of the questioning intention that lead, in the favorable case, to the terminus ad quem of the fulfilling process, the performed will-answer. And this is just what we call the practical decision, the practical yes-saying, and, with disjunctive questions, the *choice*. The word is ambiguous: it means now the conclusion of the process, the practical answer to the disjunctive practical question, as when in buying one says "I choose this dress, my choice has fallen on it"; but "choice" also names the whole process of deliberation, possibly with its conclusion in the actual choice-decision — "the buyer chooses, but is not yet resolved" being equivalent to "he deliberates what he should choose." In the second locution "choosing" is the performance of the concluding decision, in the first it is the deliberating itself directed upon the conclusion; and, as noted, the deliberating-with-conclusion can also be called "choosing." The exact analogues are in theoretical deliberation or consideration, whose outcome is likewise a question; and the whole analysis holds for all act-kinds, for everywhere we find the analogues of questions, deliberations, answers, which everywhere, moreover, can be positive and negative. A supplementary point: the decision for the disjunction-member A gives a positive answer, but essentially belonging to it is the possibility of looking back at any of the other disjunction-members, which are then characterized as to-be-rejected — theoretically to-be-rejected in the theoretical question, practically to-be-rejected in the practical, and so everywhere.

Husserl traces the difficult complications, first in the doxic, then in the practical sphere, and so introduces the phenomenon of *surmise* as a mode of decision. In the doxic sphere the deliberation — possibly as a rational one passing over into continued deepening into the grounds that come to givenness — leads to various possible conclusions: the decision for the question "Is A or B or C?" can read "It is A\!", the A-being showing itself perhaps in an insightful proof or a well-motivated experiential grounding; or it may first show in the deliberation that C is not and B is not, and then, by virtue of the conviction "One of A, B, C is" that often implicitly governs the disjunctive question, it is decided that A is. But it can also go quite otherwise: in the deliberation I come to certainty on no member, and yet I come to a decision, because the "grounds" for A, B, C have different weight and the weight for A surpasses those for the rest, so that I take, as it were, its side. Deepening into the solicitations and into what speaks for A, for B, for C, laying out the motivations noetically, more or less clear differences of weight emerge, and the deliberation takes the form of a "consideration" (*Erwägung*): in the experience-sphere I weigh the possibilities, see what experiential grounds speak for one and another, and weigh — and coming to certainty on no side I yet in a certain way decide, the decision reading here not "A is simply," for I am not certain of that, but "A is probable." Therein lies a preferring that we call *surmising decision*: every surmising is a deciding in a consideration, and what is decided for has here the character "probable"; the consideration may be insightful or not, the surmise-grounds and the weights of the possibilities remaining in the dark and vague, some possibilities soliciting themselves vaguely as "better," as those for which more seems to speak, without clear laying-out. In certain domains where the weights of the so-called probability-grounds can be treated like magnitudes, insightful possibilities arise for quantitative measurement and for the determination of the magnitude of the probability, the measure of the relative weight of the probable against the improbable cases — and there lie the roots of the mathematical doctrine of probability. It grounds, Husserl notes, in the phenomenological essence of these situations that the deliberating and considering is performed in solicitation- and preference-consciousness, in the modalities of doxic consciousness, but that on the ground of these acts judgments in the pregnant sense are to be performed, judgments about probabilities and their magnitude-values.

Passing into the will-domain, Husserl shows the decision running correspondingly. First the consideration — which here too need by no means be a rational one, though it can be — leads to our practically rejecting B and C simpliciter in an exclusive disjunction between A, B, C: the will speaks, as it were, its practical No, perhaps from rational will-motives, and it shows itself insightfully that B and C are disvalues but A a value, and we decide for it — though no rational insight is needed, a holding-for-value or holding-for-disvalue sufficing; in any case the decision is a decided willing, positive or negative. But an analogue of the surmise can also appear here as decision. It may be that in the buying-consideration we have already performed all the negative decisions, in that certain plainly inferior wares were excluded from the choice, so that the choice remains for A or B; perhaps we are already decided in advance to buy something here and so to choose one of the two, and the will-question is now: Which? In the value-consideration each solicits itself not only as value, but some value-grounds give the one a higher value, some the other, and all in all the total value of the one object seems higher — only presumably higher than the other. Accordingly we sometimes give it a decided practical preference despite the imperfect grounding, just as in a theoretical being-consideration we sometimes decide for one side in judging belief despite the imperfection of the theoretical preference, judging "It is so" though not with good logical conscience; but often we are more conscientious and do not judge "It is so" but only surmisingly take this side, judging merely "That is the probable." So too in the practical sphere: sometimes we do not pass over to the assertoric decision, the deciding "That I buy"; instead a will-modality appears that expresses itself judgmentally in the words "This ware seems practically preferable, I shall probably have to choose it." The judgment to be uttered here, on the ground of the peculiar situation of choice, presupposes in the sphere of valuing and willing modalities of preference and modalized position-takings for the one side, which are analogues of the surmising position-takings and which, in judging behavior on their basis, themselves pass over into doxic surmises, into probability-assertions; how it then goes on, whether it finally comes to a real decision in which I choose, and above all whether objectively valid reason-laws hold here that demand a decision, is a matter of its own.

Husserl closes the subsection by clarifying when a question rationally survives its answer, with a sharp aside on genuine versus sham questions. Through this kind of preferential position-taking the theoretical or the will-question does in a certain sense receive its answer, but an imperfect one, and so can remain as a question and can *rationally* remain so. If I have decided for yes or no simpliciter, then the matter in question is for me no longer a questionable one, and I cannot rationally still ask where I have so decided — where I already know, I cannot ask. One must not let oneself be deceived here by the confusion of genuine questions and sham questions: the questions the teacher puts to the pupil, the examiner to the examinee, are sham questions and at any rate not real questions about the matters slipped under them — the genuine examination-question is not "What does Plato understand by Idea?" but "What do you know concerning what Plato understands by Idea?" A certain belief that A is excludes a questioning whether A is, as it excludes the other parallel modalities, the doubting and so on; in the unity of one consciousness the modality can indeed pass over into certain doxa, but it then undergoes through this a revaluation. Returning to the deliberation, then: it is clear that a mere surmising that A is can appear as answer to the question, but in such a way that it appears as an insufficient answer, which well allows the question to be renewed — I know that A probably is, but I now ask again, Is it really? — and just so in the will-sphere, so that if I would like to buy I am not finished with the surmising practical decision but pose anew the practical question, Shall I now buy this?, and the consideration can go on, which is not only really a possibility but a rational one. Through this investigation we have gained some insight into the essence of choice and practical deliberation, precisely through the parallelization with the theoretical question and deliberation, which itself thereby became clear; and in deepening into such relations we everywhere strike essence-connections, among them reason-laws that govern the practical questioning, deliberating, and choosing-deciding and their modalities just as the theoretical — all of which would of course have to be systematically sought out, formulated, and presented in the right order.

#### **(d) Affirmation and Negation in the Belief-Domain and in the Will-Domain**

Husserl takes up one further basic form: will-negativity. Besides the closely discussed modalizations of willing in the narrower sense — of the *Urwollen*, the act to which all other acts are essentially referred back — others would need a deeper analysis, but he will proceed in only one direction and take one basic form into closer view, namely the will-negativity that stands to the primal phenomenon of positive will-positing as, in the doxic domain, unbelief stands to positive belief. He adds at once a further parallel: besides the primal phenomenon of positive belief there is in the doxic sphere the modality of affirmative belief in the proper sense — besides the positive judgment "It is so," the affirming judgment "Yes, it is so" — so that three forms appear: the unmodalized ("It is so"), the affirming, and the negating ("No, it is not so"). The same holds for the will-domain: besides the simply positive "I will," the "Yes, I will" and the "No, I will not." Older logic, Husserl charges, did not recognize that negation and affirmation, though coordinate with each other, are phenomena to be set over against the primal belief, and are modalizations; it comprehends under the title "quality" usually only the two allegedly coordinate forms, affirmation and negation, and thereby conflates position with affirmation. But one must see that the consciousness "It is not" expresses, not only linguistically but phenomenologically, a rejection that points back in itself to an "It is," to a position — not actually performed — whose structure does not in turn contain a back-pointing; and the same holds of the proper affirmation, the proper yes-saying. As the negation, as it were, crosses out, so the affirmation underlines, and accordingly has the more complex intentional structure of just that kind characteristic of all modalizations. Of this same state of affairs we convince ourselves in the case of will-negation and will-affirmation too: the willing rejection of the "I will not" implies a positive will-solicitation; it is a willful crossing-out of a not-performed but soliciting "I will." With the expression "being unwilling" we sometimes designate this negativity, though the expression is ambiguous, since *Unwille* and "being unwilling" very often designate the phenomenon of indignation.

Husserl treats omission as a special case and marks its boundary from privation. A special case of will-rejection is *Unterlassung* (omission), usually understood as the opposite of doing and as a negation of doing — here of a preceding will, possibly of the completion of an action: we have, for instance, already formed the will for a journey and made preparations for it, but afterward the will, instead of being held through and translated into full deed, is crossed out, and we "omit" the journey. Of course, to omit and in general to will negatively — though this is often expressed in the words "I will not" — does not mean to perform no willing in the sense of a privation. In ethics the privation, the not-willing in the sense of "performing no will at all" where a will would be ethically demanded, often plays a role similar to that of the not-willing in the sense of omission; but Husserl would not count the relevant lawfulnesses in formal ethics and will not treat them. Connected with the will-negations is the following: a simple willing goes upon a realizing, upon a being-becoming, and precisely a willful being-becoming, hence not upon an already-being; but there is also a willing of an already-realized, which is, however, a willing mediated through negations. If I reject a will-solicitation that something may happen, this negative will is essentially to be converted into a positive will, into the will-affirmation that the thing stay as it is — only in this way is the will of an already-realized possible, namely in the passage through the rejection of a change. Finally, the iterated acts are to be noted: I can will an A, and can then will the willing of A, and so on; likewise I can omit an A, and can will the omission of A or also omit it, and so on. With these forms of negativity and iteration the descriptive phenomenology of the will has provided the material on which the formal Praktik of the next Abschnitt will erect its will-laws.

## **IV. Section. Formal Praktik**

### **§ 18\. The Reason-Laws Belonging to the Will-Modalities and Their Correlates**

#### **(a) The Laws Bearing on the Founding and Iteration of Acts**

The fourth Abschnitt builds the formal Praktik proper, the will-analogue of formal logic, and Husserl opens with the program. For all the modalities of acts and the correlates constituting themselves with them, reason-laws result; the task is, on the one hand, to set up systematically in a *Formenlehre* all the primitive modalities and those arising through complication and iteration, and, on the other, to set up, step by step following these form-formations, the appertaining reason-laws, the axiomatic and the derived. This is a laborious task requiring much care and patience; but since it has been clearly grasped as a task and exemplary executions in the purely logical sphere already lie ready, the solution of this task and the actual development of the formal Praktik can be only of subordinate difficulty. The will-laws are not invented but read off the will-forms, on the model already perfected in logic.

Husserl distinguishes the will-laws that are merely special cases of general position-taking laws from those peculiar to the will. Many of the laws to be set up systematically for the will-sphere are special cases of general laws extending over all genera of position-takings and their correlates — for instance the proposition that if the positive willing is right, the corresponding negative, the corresponding omission, is wrong; or that if the resolve or the deed have lawful standing, the non-resolve, the abstention from the deed, the "negate" of the deed, has no lawfulness. These are special cases of the general law, valid for all genera of theses, that if a thesis is right the corresponding counter-thesis is wrong, and conversely. Of general kind too is a law for the *founded* theses: as surmises tend to be founded by certainties, or valuings can be founded by certainties or probabilities, so willings are in turn founded by doxic theses and at the same time by theses of the sphere of valuings. Founded theses have not only rightness in general — in the sense that they have their doxic, axiological, practical truth or untruth — but they also "direct" themselves after their foundation-theses. For the joy over the obtaining of a state of affairs this means not only that, phenomenologically, the joy-quality cannot appear without the matter of the joy being doxically qualified in a certain way, but that the meaning of joyfulness, as a "meaning," presupposes a certain being-conviction — a peculiar relation of the qualities. The holding-for-joyful directs itself after the being-belief and in a certain way encloses it; and where this self-directing — a kind of modification — holds, the law holds that the total act is, so to speak, false if the substrate is false, that the rejoicing is wrong if the underlying conviction is wrong — not wrong in every respect, but precisely in this presupposition.

He brings out the privileged role of the axiological substrate of the will. For the will-sphere it comes especially into account that the willing — the will-proposition, the *Vorsatz* (resolve) — has on the one hand its founding in and a directing after doxic presuppositions, and on the other, in a still narrower and more distinguished measure, after axiological presuppositions. In a more distinguished measure, for a joy is by no means yet right when its doxic substrates have their right, and likewise a will; but the rightness of the will, of the resolve, is already prefigured by the rightness of the valuing presupposed in it — the right willing directs itself, as it were, after the right valuing. To this corresponds the law that the resolve which sets itself a good has, considered in and for itself, its rightness; and since every right act is, as right, itself an axiological good, by a general axiological law, one can also say that a resolving and doing that goes upon good is itself, axiologically considered, good — a willing of the good is good, a willing of the bad bad, a willing of the indifferent indifferent. Husserl notes, as needing a special terminological fixing, that the falsity arising for the resolve from its going upon something that is in truth not good but bad is a quite other and more radical falsity than that arising from its going astray with respect to its doxic presuppositions — for example willing something not realizable at all, though held to be realizable; and for omission the corresponding reversed propositions are to be specially formulated. The will can fail, then, in two wholly different dimensions: doxically, by misjudging realizability, and axiologically, by aiming at the bad — the latter the deeper failure.

Husserl examines the curious iteration-axioms, which have no clean doxic parallel. Instead of simply willing an A, one can also will the willing of A, and instead of omitting A, omit the omission of A; and here lie certain apriori implications in the sense of reason. One cannot will the willing of A and simply not will A — that would be a willful countersense; a positive resolve directed upon a positive resolve thus implies the resolve simpliciter. Evidently too the axiom holds that the omission of an omission of A implies the doing of A — equivalently, if the omission of an omission is right, the doing is right. These are propositions that have no proper parallel in the doxic domain: I can also believe in the belief, but if the belief that I believe A is right, the belief of A itself is not thereby right. To be sure, if the assent to a belief, an affirmation, is right, the simple belief is right, and conversely; but the analogy in the will-domain to this last proposition runs: if the willful assent is right, the simple will is right, and conversely — and to assent to a will does not mean to will the will, which rather means "I will that I will," where the talk of willing a willing is referred only to one and the same I. One would have to consider, correspondingly, how it stands with "I value that I value": if a valuing referred to a valuing is right, and indeed in itself right, then the valuing simpliciter is right too, and if this is in itself right, so is the valuing referred to it — and this holds quite generally and not merely within my I. These things, Husserl warns, have their snares and must be thought through well; he does not enter further. He notes finally that the complex modalities, the conjunctive and disjunctive, are then to be drawn in: if the will directed conjunctively upon (A and B) is right, each of the corresponding single wills is right; if the conjunctively directed omission is right, each single omission is right; but if it is wrong to omit both collectively, at least one of the two must be practically good; if it is right to do disjunctively A or B — more distinctly, to do some one of A and B — then one of them can be bad, or it is wrong to do neither; and if it is wrong to omit any one of A and B, it is right to do one of the two. Everywhere here the analogue of the "axiom of contradiction" plays its role for the derivation of propositions: it is impossible that the same thing be practically right as a positive to-be-willed and practically right as a to-be-omitted, and if the positively willed is a to-be-omitted, then the negatively willed is a positive to-be-willed. One would, of course, have to proceed here just as in the logical calculus, strictly and systematically and far more systematically still, distinguishing exactly all the will-modalities and making them fruitful for the laws, deducing in strict and ordered proofs from the established primitive axioms.

#### **(b) The Will-Laws of Choice. The Absorption-Law. The Idea of the Practical Realm and the Problem of the Categorical Imperative**

This subsection is the summit of the whole course: from the laws of choice Husserl derives the *Absorptionsgesetz* (absorption-law) and, on its basis, a formal-axiological reconstruction of the categorical imperative. He prefaces a more general law. Recalling the peculiar self-grounding of position-takings upon position-takings, the directing of axiological and practical position-takings after others — of willings after valuings — he states that, considering the differences of preferability, preferability on the side of the founding draws after it a preferability within the position-takings that "direct" themselves after it. Calling the more preferable the better: if W1 \> W2 in any sphere, and f(W1) and f(W2) are values that direct themselves after W1 and W2 respectively, then f(W1) \> f(W2). Thus the good-valuing follows the beauty-valuing: if something is beautiful, then with regard to that its existence is eo ipso good, so that if W1 is a better beauty-value than W2, the existence of W1 is a better good-value than that of W2; and likewise the wish or will directed upon the former good-value is better than that upon the latter — if G1 \> G2, then the will-goal of G1 is more preferable than that of G2. Generally, then, a good considered in and for itself is a better will-goal than a bad or an Adiaphoron; and since W1 \> W2 generalized does not mean that W1 and W2 are positive values — a less bad being "more preferable" than a worse — a less bad is a better will-goal than a worse, the willing of the bad being in itself bad but the willing of the less bad less bad than the willing of the worse, and an Adiaphoron better than a bad.

Husserl then states the basic will-law of choice and the devaluation it works. Let any will-subject stand in a choice, referred to practical possibilities among which the positive values W1 and W2 occur. If now W1 \< W2, then the practical decision for W1 alone is not only worse than the practical decision for W2 alone, but is itself *bad*; the subordination of the better and the preference of the worse, both taken in one, is wrong, and the choice is therefore to be valued as bad. While, then, the willing or doing of a good has, considered in and for itself, a positive value-character, the law shows that this value not merely diminishes but is wholly lost as soon as the good appears as a practical possibility in a choice beside a simultaneously subordinated better: in this connection the in-and-for-itself positive-valued becomes not merely lower-valued but negative-valued. The curious devaluation, Husserl observes, expresses itself nicely in the linguistic equivocation of the word *minderwertig*, "of lower value," which precisely with regard to such connections of choice has as a rule taken on a pejorative meaning.

He offers a construal of the devaluation through the summation-laws. One can lay out the situation thus: an in-itself good to which predominantly unfavorable consequences attach is thereby devalued, forfeits its value; considered in and for itself it is and remains a good, but by virtue of the consequence-connections it becomes the substrate of derived negative values, and these diminish the good-value by summation and can even, as in the supposed case, outweigh it, consuming its whole positivity. So here too: every will to the good is, considered in and for itself, good, positive-valued, right in its way; but as soon as this will appears in, or is thought in, the connection of a choice that indeed follows the good but at the same time subordinates a higher good, excluding it from realization, then with the positive will-value belonging to the less good there combines a lawfully outweighing disvalue of the subordination of the better — a kind of summation-effect. The peculiar thing, in any case, is that the subordination of a better so spoils, under all circumstances, the positive value of the simultaneous preference of a good that it ceases to be positive at all: it is, as it were, an annulled force, annulled by a counter-force acting at the same point of attack, in the same I and the same time. It is just because this law and, parallel to it, similar laws obtain that all the laws bearing on practical values and practical rightnesses of willings, resolves, and actions that orient themselves after values, but without taking the choice into account, constantly carry a restricting phrase with them — the peculiar talk of values "considered in and for themselves." It is similar to the mechanics of forces and the physics operating with forces, where the laws of resultant-formation bring it about that what holds for forces in abstraction from new forces straightway gains its modification as soon as new forces, and possibly new force-forms, appear. The analogy to a parallelogram of forces is exact: the in-itself value is a vector that the counter-vector of subordination can reverse.

Husserl develops the dependence of the devaluation-laws on the existence-relations of goods, and extends the basic law to many members. Closely seen, the law analytically encloses the presupposition that the realization of the one practical possibility and the non-realization of the other, better possibility are compatible; it could be otherwise, namely insofar as the realization of the one of itself draws the other after it — in which case to decide for W1 means implicitly to co-realize W2, and a higher value arises that summatorily unites both. Generally the laws of practical devaluation depend on the laws connected with the existence-dependence of goods: as where, in existence, a good has another good as consequence, the good-value-in-itself of the first undergoes an increase, gaining a derivation-good, so a corresponding value-diminution sets in where the existence of a good hinders the existence of another good, and devaluation sets in where its existence hinders a relatively higher good, the in-itself positive value sinking below the zero-point; whereas where goods neither draw one another in existence nor hinder one another in existing, they increase in their co-existence in the form of the grounding of a good-sum. Such laws have consequences for the practical sphere, since will goes upon realization: where the willful realization of a lower value would hinder that of a higher value which itself stands within the choice-range, will and realization of the lower-valued would be not merely something inferior but negative-valued; and if the two practical possibilities are incompatible in coexistence, the realization of the better would hinder the less good and so far yield a derived disvalue, but not only does a positive surplus remain in this respect through the realization of the better — the value of the choice of the better also adds itself as a value-increasing moment. The main law stated for two members extends to arbitrarily many: if n goods appear in a choice as practical possibilities such that each can be exclusively chosen and every other thereby practically rejected, and if among these n one is the best, then the choice of any other good of the series is not only of lower value but simply a disvalue — the choice is a false choice, or, since every choosing deliberation is a questioning, the will-question has found a false will-answer.

Husserl now guards the law against two misreadings and thereby introduces the crucial concept of the *practical realm*. It has not yet been said that the choice of the best of the n goods is already the *right* choice, any more than it has been said, as a consequence of the law, that in pairwise value-comparison every lower-valued member is to be struck out. How wrong that would be follows at once: the n goods were singly to be realized or rejected, and each could be chosen and all the others rejected — but nothing was said as to whether several or all goods of the series might be practically *compatible*, hence realizable and choosable together. If that is the case, they determine, by the summation-law, a good-sum that is itself again a good and a higher good than each of the single goods, and possibly even relations not merely of summation but of production are possible. So as soon as one of these higher values newly enters the field of view of the choice, the right will-answer shifts: what before was the best is afterward, as a single summand, a less good, whose choice would be bad. The law always says that the better, the best, goes first, but only in the sense that to choose a less good where a better lies in the choice-range would be perverse. Hence it is precisely *not* said that the best of the given disjunction is the practically good, for it is clear in advance that something better could be attained, both in the form of the possible drawing-in of good-sums to be produced and in the form of the possible drawing-in of further practical possibilities. So long as something new is to be drawn into the choice — new goods — we plainly have no positive practical *demand*, even though our law holds its validity for any arbitrarily set choice-range; and we see too that, just for this reason and so long as an expansion of the range is possible, none of the set-back goods may really be struck out: it remains constantly in consideration.

He completes the casuistry with equal goods, evils, and Adiaphora, and fixes the terminology of *Konvenienz*. If in a choice we had only equal goods, none objectively preferred to another, they are also practically equivalent, and the will-decision for one is not characterized as bad over against that for another; likewise a group of inferior goods may stand within a choice beside a group surpassing them but equivalent among themselves, in which case there is no unambiguously best of this choice, and the decision for each of the equivalently higher is equally right — a many-valued best. Every deciding or willing directed upon a disvalue is in itself wrong, so a choice whose members are exclusively disvalues yields the answer that every choice-decision running out positively is wrong: there it holds that to reject everything is right, without further raising the question of more or less bad — though, Husserl notes in a footnote, preference-laws hold here too, the decision for the less bad being itself less bad than that for the worse. Where goods appear beside evils, the evils are under all circumstances to be struck, not to be reckoned even as possible summands. The Adiaphora are to be seen within each choice like zeros, in the sense of the law, which always has in view only a will-question whether this or that thing standing in the choice is to be realized alone, without the competing members: to realize an Adiaphoron where a good would be realizable is perverse, but if no good is to be realized, only bad or some Adiaphora, then every will directed upon an Adiaphoron is indifferent. Husserl adds essential clarifications of vocabulary: a will directed upon a positive value, as directing itself after the positive-valued, is itself "right," and this expresses a special *Konvenienz* (befittingness) that is itself axiologically to be valued and is then, considered in and for itself, positive-valued; a will directing itself against a positive value, rejecting the value, or affirmingly upon a disvalue is wrong, "inconvenient," and this inconvenience, this will-contradiction against the positive value, is axiologically a disvalue — and similarly stand the convenience of a will-negation that directs itself against a disvalue and the inconvenience of a will-affirmation that directs itself upon a disvalue. With these we can use other expressions to define: an act under consideration we call "demanded," and a state of affairs under consideration, a practical possibility, a practical *Verhalt*, we call objectively demanded or objectively ought-to-be if the will directed upon it would be convenient, and not-ought-to-be, negatively demanded, in the case of inconvenience, the negative demand being equivalent to the demand of rejection. The talk of "ought" or "demand" is, Husserl stresses, a metaphor stemming from commands and imperatives, but of any such there is here in reality no question; we must hold strictly to the definition. In the case of the Adiaphora both convenience and inconvenience are lacking: there is neither a positive ought nor an ought of non-being, so that whoever chooses an indifferent, on the presupposition that no positive good is in question, conflicts with no ought and behaves not inconveniently, hence acts not wrongly — but also not rightly in the will-sense, not conveniently; he does nothing owed, but no ought is there either, and he exercises no will-reason, which goes only upon convenience.

Husserl draws the section to its climax: the absorption-law and the consequent problem of the categorical imperative. In the findings so far we feel ourselves very restricted. Standing in a choice and posing the will-question, shall I A or B, within the frame of its disjunction the objectively understood practical ought passes over from the lower values to the highest value and is completely "absorbed" by it; and the sense of the disjunctive will-question corresponds exactly to the presuppositions of the law, for "Shall I A or B or C?" means from the outset nothing other than "Is it right to choose A and at the same time not to choose B and C?", and likewise for every other member. Holding sharply to this sense, the law can be stated simply: *in every choice the better absorbs the good, and the best absorbs everything else to be esteemed, in and for itself, as practically good.* But this absorption creates no absolute but in general only a relative ought — better, no ought simpliciter but only an ought under reservation; and, drawing on Kantian expressions, we do not thereby get a "categorical imperative." What, then, defines a categorical imperative? Precisely the incapacity to be absorbed. But do we know already and in advance that for the willing I there is in every given case a non-absorbable ought, a best not to be surpassed? What alone we know is that in every choice that has a definitely bounded practical realm consisting of a finite number of members there is a best, provided a good is found in it at all — and the best may possibly be a many-valued one, which we could come to terms with, since it is an axiom that among several equal optima to do none is wrong and to choose any one of them, no matter which, is right, practically owed, though only relatively. The relativity, the validity under reservation, for any choice lies in the fact that, so long as nothing closer is settled about it, a certain finality of bounding is lacking: it is something contingent upon what the chooser directs his will-question and how far he spans the questioning disjunction, for he can draw ever-new practical possibilities into the range of the question, and with each expansion of the practical realm the optimum will, generally speaking, alter, while members absorbed at the earlier stage may possibly gain new significance. The best in a choice, and according to the measure of its practical realm, is the practically demanded only under reservation — namely, in case no better that absorbs it results upon expansion of the practical realm; and for this better the same can again hold. It is clear, then, that the possibility of a categorical imperative depends on our being able to find a determinate practical realm belonging to it, an objectively belonging practical field of efficacy that is objectively closed and so no longer capable of expansion, but that exists for every time-point at which any will-question whatever is to be put to the I. Is there such a realm, possibly, for individual I's, or is there one apriori for every I? With this Husserl's consideration — carried, as he may say, thoroughly by apriori and insightful necessities — has led to the most central problem of ethics, the problem of the categorical imperative, whose formal possibility now turns entirely on the idea of an all-encompassing, non-expandable practical realm.

### **§ 19\. The Idea of Perfect Will-Rightness. The Order of the Ethical Disciplines**

Husserl draws together the result of the formal Praktik and fixes its relation to Kant and to the further, material disciplines of ethics. The formal objective inquiry has reached its conclusion: it has yielded a purely formal determination of what is demanded for each subject and for each time-point of its action. To each subject we think coordinated its ideally complete practical field of efficacy, for some time-point to be envisaged; and we assume that each ideally possible subject having such a field can also will and do good in it. To do, among all attainable goods, the best — that is the absolutely right and so categorically demanded. *What* is good cannot be decided formaliter, any more than what is true can be decided by mere formal logic, and so neither can what is the objectively best and practically demanded; but it holds in advance that an objective good and best is prefigured. Like truth, goodness and preferability exclude every contingent relativity; there is no lawlessness that would bring the material content of the respective good and best into contingent connection with the contingent subject of the valuing, preferring, willing. As truth is objective truth from apriori law-grounds, so beauty, goodness, preferability, practical owedness are prefigured from apriori law-grounds — the objectivity pointing to apriori laws into which the respective matter, the determinate What valued in the valuing, is woven according to its general essential kind. So although the practical will-realm of each subject is a different one, although generally not goods of the same kind enter every such realm, and although the practically best is therefore a different one for each subject, every rational subject must nonetheless acknowledge that where one rightly good-values thus and so, everyone whatever who brings the same matter into consideration would have to value likewise; and likewise every rational subject must acknowledge that if a realm contains such and such goods as practical possibilities, the best is ideally prefigured for this realm, the subject of that realm being thus bound by idea and law.

Husserl uses this to extract the "alone valuable" kernel of Kant's doctrine while rejecting its formalism. So we do justice to the alone valuable content of the Kantian demand of a practical law and a categorical imperative. If someone acts, in the given case, so that the generalization to a universal law would be impossible, he acts surely falsely; but this says, and may say, nothing other than that perfect will-rightness is an idea that builds itself upon the idea of the practical realm and the idea of the best in this realm, and that all these founding ideas, and with them the founded one, stand under ideal laws, according to which it is certain that if a subject acts rightly, every other subject would have to act just so if we transformed its practical realm into that of the agent — and this grounds precisely in the fact that exclusively pure essence-laws prescribe the rightness and merely find application to the factually given individual case and the factual subject. So too we do justice to the fiction of the impartial spectator adduced by some historical ethics: we act rightly if an arbitrary impartial spectator, transposing himself into our situation, would have to acknowledge our acting, and we transpose ourselves into the role of the impartial spectator when we judge our own acting for rightness. The impartial spectator is here a rationally evaluating subject who convinces himself that the convictions founding the acting are right, that the good-valuings are rightly performed, that what we good-value would indeed, by its essence and so generally and according to apriori material value-laws, have to be valued as good, that likewise in the essence of the good-values just such and no other preferences have their lawful hold, so that the best is really the best. The difference in the (objectively right) decision of different acting subjects has, then, its grounds, and may only have its grounds, in the fact that the factual situation is a different one from case to case: the same essence-laws, material and formal, of course demand different things according to the application-case, but in each something determinate.

Husserl draws the sharp boundary against Kant's content-empty formalism. The formal Praktik, which alone he has had in view, speaks with laws like those of consequence and of the highest practical good eo ipso about nothing material; it contains nothing of determinate material values and goods, just as formal logic says nothing of determinate objects, their kinds and genera, of the determinate world with its empirical particularities, and so grounds nothing of materially determinate truths. But that there can be and are such truths, and that singular material truths are, with respect to their matter, bound by essence-laws into which the material content-kinds and content-genera enter, it presupposes in formal generality, and own axioms refer to it — and likewise for the formal axiology and Praktik. Were there no material apriori, were there no kinds and genera of objects distinguishable that by their generic essence would carry value-predicates apriori, then the concept of objective value would have no hold, and consequently neither would the idea of an objectively prefigured preferability and the idea of a best. A formal rule-giving of valuing and willing, on the presupposition that the matter of the valuing and willing — the content-particularity of the value- and will-objects — could be left out of account, is a countersense; and in this respect we must sharply separate our ways from those of the Kantian doctrine. The parallelistic considerations, whose feasibility has everywhere proved itself, show that the analogy of the formal and material in the practical sphere thoroughly holds with that in the logical sphere, so that we cannot fall into the aberration of wanting to prescribe, out of merely formal laws and with the mere help of the content-empty categorical imperative, what is the practically demanded, the absolutely owed, in the given materially determinate individual case. Formal logic with all its laws cannot put us in a position to derive the least fact-truth; it does not even encompass all apriori truth but precisely only formal truth. If logic tells us that every proposition of contradictory matter is false, it can yet, in no single case of propositions of materially determinate matter, tell us whether it is contradictory; if it tells us which inference-forms are right and which false, it tells us of no single inference laid before us with materially determinate termini whether it is right or false. Already the application to the materially determinate is a step that oversteps logic, and still more the fixing of the truths and laws belonging to particular matters by virtue of their special kind. Just so for the formal axiology and Praktik: within the circle of the practically attainable the better is the enemy of the good, to subordinate the better is unconditionally wrong, to choose the best possible is unconditionally demanded as the only and therefore absolutely right; to hold this formal principle before one's eyes and expressly state it can be useful, as it can be useful to formulate the formal-logical laws and let oneself be admonished by them — but the question what is good and better and best is not thereby answered, and even theoretically only a small, if the most fundamental, part of the task of a scientific and first of all apriori ethics is thereby settled.

Husserl sketches the material tasks that lie beyond the formal discipline and orders the ethical disciplines. Now the basic classes of values, of practical goods, would have to be fixed and the appertaining preference-laws theoretically investigated. How do sensible goods stand to the goods that lie in the reason-sphere itself? Is insight as such, are right and insightful valuing and willing as such, not objectively valuable, and do they not thereby designate classes of objective goods? How does it stand with the values of personality, with the valuing of personal qualities, such as those referred to the acts we call reason-acts — how, then, with the valuing of a personal being as a reason-being? And how does it stand if we think a rational subject in a social connection: what peculiar values and disvalues arise there, how far can a higher spiritual unity — the unity of a family, an association, a state, a people — constitute itself through sociality, and this higher unity itself again be ideally valuable, graspable as a kind of subject for achievements that can be good and bad? From there the lines lead into proper ethics, into individual and social ethics. The formal axiology and Praktik is thus a first, extraordinarily important stage, and obviously, in the order of the ethical disciplines, the first in itself; the higher stage is a systematic exhibition of the whole material apriori — where, as Husserl cannot here further work out, the oppositions of "formal" and "material" can still be variously oriented. To these apriori disciplines there correspond then, correlatively, noetic-apriori disciplines, analogously as to formal logic, which handles propositions, truths, possibilities, surmisabilities and probabilities, questionabilities, purely formally, there corresponds a doctrine of the corresponding reason-acts and their normative regulation, the research then going on into the whole phenomenology of the logical reason-consciousness. So too with ethics: from the objective, noematic stance we pass over into the stance upon the acts and their regulation, and thence we arrive, in systematically complete research, at a pure phenomenology of valuing and willing reason, which integrates itself into the general phenomenology of consciousness in general. All these investigations are philosophical; not properly philosophical are the applications then to be made to the building of practical disciplines — a practical logic, a pedagogy of right thinking and knowing, Husserl would not call a properly philosophical discipline, any more than a like pedagogy of right and insightful aesthetic valuing, of right and insightful willing and acting, all these disciplines referred to man in the given world. He notes finally that turns into the practical can of course also be performed apriori and would then belong to the stock of the indicated apriori disciplines, as geometry turns its theoretical laws often into a practical form that is for that reason still apriori — weighing construction-possibilities, posing construction-tasks, giving ways of proceeding in the solution, how this or that task can be solved with compass and ruler — such constructions and construction-rules being plainly thoroughly apriori and by no means seriously referred to man and the given actuality.

### **§ 20\. Insightful Willing as Will-Goal**

Husserl closes the course (before the Beilagen) with an antinomy that arises in passing from the formal, objective ethics to the noetic — the question of the value of *insight* in willing. The formal objective imperative runs: "Do the best among the attainable good within your respective practical total-sphere\!" This best formally circumscribes the objective will-rightness and designates, correlatively, what shall be willed and done. But how stands the value of a *right* will? Is the will that goes upon the absolutely right thereby already the *best* will? Does the categorical imperative not go upon the best will? Does not the highest formal ethical demand lie in this, that it directs itself at the will-subject and categorically demands of it the in each case conceivably best will? And in this respect one sees at once that the objective imperative we had arrived at is, as concerns the will, not to be valued very highly. In our derivation, which determines the sense of the objective imperative of perfect rightness, neither the reason of the willer nor even whether he engages in deliberation and consideration was presupposed; but if this is so, then the will can be a wholly *blind* one. Without any insight, yielding to a blind drive — for example a blind habituation stemming from a conventional upbringing — the willer may by chance hit upon what is in itself the best, the only "right," as a rational appraiser can recognize in full insight: the appraiser performs the reason-considerations, the valuings and groundings valid for the case, which the willer himself has not performed, and the result of his ethical reckoning agrees with the blind will-direction of the agent. It is plainly exactly as in the judgment-domain: the objective question is that of truth, and truth means, with respect to the judgment-act, rightness or validity; in the objective appraisal the judgment is right, the asserted proposition true and so far of value, although the judger has perhaps judged at random, following a blind inclination and so not at all praiseworthy. But do we not put to the judger the demand to judge rightly, do we not demand of every judgment that it ought to be right? On the one hand, then, we ascribe value to the right judgment and seem to demand almost nothing but rightness; on the other hand we say that to judge rightly but blindly is worthless — and something similar seems to result in the will-domain.

Husserl shows that the judgment-case already refers back to the will, and so resolves the antinomy by way of the will. In the judgment-domain, considered more closely, we are referred to the will-domain: a demand we direct at someone directs itself at his will, so a judgment-demand at a judging-willing; the judger shall therefore willfully direct his judging so that it becomes valid, so that it acknowledges the true as true. To be able to do that, the judger must be able to see rightness and wrongness in judgments — in other words, he must strive not for judging in general but for *rational* judging; for to judge rationally is nothing other than to judge so that the passed judgment not only hits the truth in general and by chance but hits it insightfully, so that the judger can convince himself therein of the validity. How then in the ethical sphere? Uttering ethical demands, we impute to the acting subject volitions that it shall perform, and these are to be right volitions; we thereby present it as one who willfully governs his will-life, makes this itself the field of praxis, the will-realm, and now strives for right willing — and that requires, of course, insight into the rightness, hence not blind but insightful willing.

Husserl confronts the resulting paradox and dissolves it phenomenologically. Are we then to say, on the ground of this consideration, that not right willing in general but insightful willing is the best — that not the best in the respective practical realm is the practically best, but an insightful will directed upon the best, and, following it, the doing of the insightfully best? But what odd talk is this: that the best of the practical realm is not the best, but the insightful will upon it, as if this will were not itself reckoned in here to the practical realm? How do we help ourselves out of these snares? Husserl's answer: thinking ourselves into will-acts, investigating their essence and the essence of their correlates, we find possibilities of the appertaining clarification of the will-goal, of the re-examination of the doxic presuppositions, of the appertaining valuings; it belongs to the essence of the will that such act-series can in principle be enclosed in it, and when we perform them in ourselves we behold differences of rightness and wrongness, and preferring the case of rightness and investigating what this idea encloses in the will-sphere, we come upon the law of the best. It is exactly as when, thinking ourselves into some judging — even a freely fantasied one — we are led into grounding-connections that are prefigured, by the essence of the judgment in question and more closely by its quality and matter, according to their typical essence; that they are so prefigured we recognize here, as in the judgment-domain, through a reflective phenomenological essence-cognition. In this way we perform insights in which we recognize that to every judgment whatever an idea of rightness or truth, or of its opposite falsity, is prefigured, and we recognize formal-logical laws as laws belonging generally to the idea of truth as such; and just so we grasp in insight, in act-connections that lead the will-acts over into connections in which they reach their reason-goal, by ideation, that will-acts as such stand under the idea of rightness and that for them formal will-laws hold, among them the law of the highest practical good — and this validity is an apriori and unconditioned one.

He closes by folding the value of insight back into the law of the best itself. Apriori too it holds, for every subject whatever, that the volitions themselves belong to the will-realm of the willer — namely that I can in the now direct my will upon a future willing, that I can equally at will suspend will-impulses, fend off inclinations, and direct my will upon rational deliberation and choice and thereby upon rational will-decisions. With regard to this they themselves belong to every will-realm, and the law of the best now says that special regard is to be taken of the goods that lie in these reason-acts, and that these reason-acts make up necessary conditions of the possibility of attaining the consequent goal of will-rightness. I must therefore not only otherwise ask what I can and consider what is the best, thereby taking up the reason-stance of itself, but I must intentionally strive for the goal of the greatest possible clarity and rationality, by which I not only secure the otherwise and only relatively best but add, in the insight itself, a new value. Everything else is a matter of supplementary considerations — for it is to be considered generally how the idea of the highest practical good can be practically satisfied, what is to be regarded for ideal grounds, and how the apparent principial difficulties that arise in the process can be removed. The antinomy is thus dissolved: insightful willing is not a rival best outside the practical realm but a good *within* it, demanded by the very law of the best, since the reason-acts are themselves attainable goods and conditions of attaining rightness at all.

### **§ 21\. The Objectivity of the Practical Possibilities and Their Relativity to the Subject. The Convenience or Inconvenience Grounded in the Essence of the Will-Act. The Components of Perfect Will-Rightness**

The final section of the course gathers the threads of the categorical-imperative problem and the noetic supplement, fixing precisely what the formal Praktik secures and what it leaves open. Husserl recalls that the absorption-law established only that if in a choice — more distinctly, in a will-question — positive values stand in question, the best of them absorbs all the others; but it was *not* thereby said that this best value is the objectively owed simpliciter, the absolutely right will-goal. Only if we think the realm of the practical goods existing for the I so widely spanned that it encompasses all such goods — all the goods attainable for the subject in the relevant present — and then refer the will-question to all these goods, is the best of this widest circle no longer absorbable, no longer merely a relative best: no expansion of the circle is then thinkable that would still contain a practical good that could be better still. Equivalently, thinking the whole practical realm of the I at the relevant time-point of acting as containing all its practical realizabilities, this realm contains in general, though not necessarily, goods, and partly goods, partly evils, partly Adiaphora; and if it contains goods at all, then with regard to the will-question to be posed for the whole realm according to all its members there is, unambiguously or many-ambiguously, an *absolute* best, and this is then owed simpliciter. In the case of many-ambiguity it stands free to the subject to choose any of the several optima, but no choosing would be wrong — any one, no matter which, is absolutely owed; and if the whole practical realm contains only a single good, this would eo ipso be the absolutely owed, the absolutely right will-goal. But the presupposition remains open that the respective will-subject really has a *closed* practical realm that would let itself be disjoined in a complete choice — so an essential problem here stays unsolved, and Husserl declines to orient the further consideration by it, since it is above all important first to clarify the sense of what has been gained.

Husserl establishes the full objectivity of the findings, their independence of any actual choosing. Seen more closely, all our findings are free of the actuality of a choice of the will-subject; it does not depend on the subject's posing will-questions to itself. Objectively it is to be said that the subject "has the choice" even when it does not choose — it has the choice meaning that it *could* choose and rationally choose, and this "could" again means mere ideal possibility. If we think any will-subject in purely apriori consideration, it is determined in itself what is a practical possibility for it and what is not; it is objectively determined what among these practical possibilities is a good or not, and objectively determined are the value-relations of the goods and the unambiguously or many-ambiguously — if not even infinitely many-ambiguously — best. Among all this it has the choice, even if it never occurs to it seriously to choose and to survey, articulate, and exercise value-comparisons over the whole practical realm; and if in the given case it really chooses, it may behave wholly falsely in the choice — overstepping the objective will-realm, regarding something as a practical possibility that is none at all, or, within the genuine or falsely posited possibilities, holding something for a good or best that is in truth a bad or inferior. But all this is now out of account; the truth that the relevant I has such and such an objective will-realm, and in it perhaps a *summum bonum*, must be a knowable one in an ideally possible subject, and so ideally a choice must be thinkable that in absolutely insightful manner encompasses the whole will-realm of that I, recognizes its completeness, insightfully values all its values, and determines out the absolutely practically best. We spectators, with sufficient intelligence, could perform this insightful appraisal — we choose, as it were, for the will-subject, which itself need not choose at all — and we then say that it has, objectively spoken, the choice precisely between these and no other practical possibilities, and that objectively just this and nothing else is for it the highest practically attainable good and so the categorically right, the objectively owed. But with all this we draw in only ideal possibilities that we need not draw in; omitting that, there remains an in-itself-existing realm of practical possibilities with its eventual best.

Husserl marks the kinship of will-rightness with the rightness of *surmise* rather than of objective truth. It stands here with objective will-rightness as with objective judgment-rightness, or still better surmise-rightness: whether the surmiser has rightly chosen among the favorable and unfavorable possibilities at his disposal, indeed whether he has chosen at all, his surmise is objectively right or wrong, and it is right if it conforms to the preponderant possibilities, wrong if it follows the side of the less weighty possibilities. As there are in the doctrine of probability objective and formal laws that in their objectivity do not ask whether the surmiser has reason-insight or not, so we have here a formal objective law of will-rightness; and there are also similarities with the judgment-domain in the narrower sense, since a judgment that sets a contradiction as obtaining is wrong, whether or not the judger knows that he contradicts himself. Looking more closely at the role the I and the will-act play in this objectively grasped situation, it is clear that by the own essence of the respective will-act it is wholly determined whether it is convenient or not — convenient purely insofar as it directs itself upon an objective value or not; if it does not, it directs itself upon an objective disvalue or upon an Adiaphoron, and then again inconvenience or indifference is objectively determined by the own essence of the will-act. Correlatively the same holds for the resolves belonging to the immanent essence of the respective will-acts; calling the resolves, according to the convenience and inconvenience of the will-acts, appropriate or inappropriate (*angemessen* or *unangemessen*), we can say that for convenience and inconvenience, for appropriateness and inappropriateness, only the essence of the respective will-act or resolve comes in question — similarly as in the domain of judgments only the essence of the judgment, the bare proposition, comes in question. "Only" means that whether this or that subject judges makes no difference: the judging is a right one, the proposition a true one in itself, no matter who so judges, who predicates the proposition; and likewise a willing is convenient and in this sense fittingly directed, and the relevant resolve, the analogue of the logical proposition, is appropriate, as whoever's willing we may think the relevant willing to be. Pure knowledge-joy, for example, is in itself valuable, a willing that directs itself after this joy is in itself convenient, a resolve following it in itself appropriate — just as a willing that directs itself after an ignoble joy, say an animal-tormenting joy, is in itself inconvenient and, in further consequence and axiologically considered, a disvalue, regardless of the subject that so wills.

Husserl now refines the terminology of the previous lecture, separating *convenience* from *attainability* and so from *owedness* — and here subjectivity enters in a way without analogue in the truth-domain. Convenience is not everything; closely seen, not even the realizability of the resolve, the fulfillability of the willing, comes into question for it. Highlighting it purely as the *one* component belonging to the possible objective validity of the will, we may not, as last time, already speak in the case of convenience of an objective ought; we do well to modify the earlier terminology suitably. That a resolve is objectively an owed one, an objectively demanded one, contains — if we deepen ourselves into the language-usual sense of such words — convenience, appropriateness, but yet more; so it is better, as now, to distill out the mere convenience and to speak, for the corresponding resolve, only of appropriateness and not yet of owedness. That last time we decided terminologically otherwise lay in our having tacitly included in convenience at least already practical attainability, which we now, for good reasons, do not. Convenience is wholly independent of the attainability of the willed, as indeed the former can be decided before all consideration of the latter; on the other hand, apriori nothing is objectively demanded of a subject that is unattainable for it — what someone cannot do, he also ought not. The resolve that has the predicate of ought has thereby a richer predicate than that of convenience; and now we see that already with this expansion subjectivity plays a role that has no analogy in the judgment- or truth-domain. An objective truth is truth in itself, and this "in itself" says that the judging subject does not relativize the truth: the truth in itself is not truth for this one or that one, every objective judgment is in itself decided, and whoever judges, if only he judges rightly, judges with respect to one and the same proposition-matter the same — he judges the one self-same proposition that is truth, and the truth is what it is whether or not anyone judges it. By contrast, what is a practical possibility for me need not be one for everyone else: practical possibilities are essentially referred to the will-capable subject. That a doing is a practical possibility for a subject, or whether it is, is in itself determined — the subject itself may judge wrongly about it, may hold something practically possible that in truth is not — and so far we have here something objective; but the objectivity of the practical possibility is essentially bound to the relevant subject, and the same holds for the whole practical realm of the subject for each moment of its possible acting, the realm of all the practical possibilities objectively existing for it in the relevant moment. If it has practical possibilities at all, hence a practical total-realm, it is at any rate *its own*, and one can at least say apriori the one thing, that two I-subjects cannot in principle have an identical practical total-realm, not the identically possible field of efficacy; and at the same time this realm in general alters from time-point to time-point.
