Where, in this respect, the analogy between the highest will-rightness and judgment-rightness, objective judgment-truth, is lacking, one re-emerges in the comparison with the cases of surmise-rightness; for the rightness of the surmise is referred to an objectively to-be-fixed holding of positive and negative solicitations, to a holding of knowledge and non-knowledge. Before the question of the right position-taking, the right preference among the better possibilities, lies here the question of the total-realm of the relevant knowledge and non-knowledge, and so of the total-realm of the real possibilities between which the surmising subject objectively has the choice — and this total-realm too is bound to the subjects, the holding of knowledge and non-knowledge being in general a different one for different subjects and for the same subject at different times. The appraisal of the objective rightness of the surmising decision bases itself here on the appraisal of the knowledge-realm, the realm of possibilities, objectively existing for the subject, whereby it is, as said, out of question whether and how the subject itself appraises its knowledge and its possibility-realm. Husserl's appended footnote draws the parallel out as a maxim: choose the best, do the best, the best is the only right; and just so in the surmise-sphere, surmise the most probable, decide surmisingly for the solicitation of greatest weight within the total-realm of your "problematic possibilities" — and since this total-realm is essentially shiftable, new problematic possibilities being able to enter it later, the surmising decision is something always again to be modified, the noetic demand arising to determine the weights and to assure oneself of the respective relative weight-relations, so as to have the "value," the weight-relation corresponding to each surmise, constantly in firm posit; surmises have their "values," surmises absorb one another according to their values, and where certainty is lacking, the surmises too have, axiologically, relative value according to their "probabilities," the values in the other sense. The surmise-analogy thus binds the formal Praktik to the formal logic of probability: both are objective formal disciplines of rational position-taking under an irreducibly subject- and situation-relative field.

Husserl continues the analysis of the components of perfect will-rightness, distinguishing *objectuality* from convenience and ordering them under the absorption-law. A willing, a resolve, that goes upon something practically possible for the relevant I in the given moment has, apart from convenience and inconvenience, already in that respect a kind of rightness — we could also say it has *Gegenständlichkeit* (objectuality) — while where it goes upon something "non-realizable," upon the practically impossible, it is "objectless." But combining the rightness of convenience with that in the sense of objectuality does not yet yield a will that has rightness in the highest sense. The will goes upon a "practical good," and the resolve we might accordingly be tempted to call a good one in the practical sense, but this good is still very imperfect; quite generally a will is of the higher value the higher the good it directs itself after. There are differences of preferability, at first apart from attainability and from the relation of any moment to one and the same subject and its practical realm; but if we compare willings and resolves that refer to this — if we fix the I-subject, the time-point, and the practical total-realm belonging to it — then the laws of absorption take effect, and with them the difference of objective owedness and of reprehensibility appears: to will the less preferable is practically reprehensible, to will the more preferable is relatively owed, to subordinate it reprehensible — all of this objectively understood, without the question how the subject itself rightly or wrongly appraises the preferability and whether it chooses at all. But the preferable too must yield to a still more preferable, and so in general, as long as objectively higher goods are to be found in the practical total-realm whose existence, and whose occupation with goods, we presuppose; and it results from this consideration that one and only the unsurpassable preferability, the best in the whole practical sphere of the given moment, is that which can no longer give up its objective ought-character — it alone is not merely a relatively owed but an owed simpliciter, always objectively understood. A relativity does remain, namely to subject and time-point; yet, although each subject in general owes something different, and likewise the same subject at different times, each owes at each time only one thing, and an objectively determinate one, if it owes anything at all — this "if" meaning, if the subject in the given moment has a practical field of efficacy at all, and further, if in this any goods are to be found at all, the extent of these restrictions being of course specially to be considered. As to the unity of the best, Husserl refers back to the distinction of unambiguity and many-ambiguity: the "best" or owed may have the form of a disjunction, one of A1, A2, and so on, no matter which, where the entire A are equally best.

Husserl closes the course's main text with the antinomy of insight, resolving it as in § 20 but now for the will. Are we then at an end, and is what we have gained satisfying in every respect? The objective consideration is concluded; it yielded a purely formal determination of what is at any time and for any subject absolutely owed. But is this categorically owed a real categorical imperative, and does the concept of ought-to-do not have yet another sense to which we have not done justice? In the parallel judgment- or surmise-domain, someone can judge wholly blindly, can "talk into the day" without any reason, and yet "by chance" hit the right; in the objective appraisal it holds that what he says is true, and we even speak of the value of right judging, and yet we do not credit the judger with this value at all — he has, we mean, no merit, we even call his judging worthless. A valuable judging, we say, is one performed on reason-motives, one that, led by reason-grounds, takes the right direction and in the favorable case is fully insightful; only in such cases does the value-giving lie originarily in the judging itself, the rightness lying originarily given in the insightfulness and then originarily determining the value-holding. In the original sense the insightful judgment, as giving the rightness and truth, has value, and if we nonetheless positively value an uninsightful judging, it lies only in the ideal possibility of transforming this judgment into an insightful one, of seeing in insight that it by its sense makes ideal insightfulness possible. Only in insightful judging does the judger himself experience the genuine ought — which in the judgment-domain means that he experiences the reason-motivation, performing his thesis of the "It is so" not straightaway but orienting it after genuine grounds, the things and the matter-connections themselves coming to givenness and, as given, speaking out as it were demands that the judgment follows. This originary consciousness of rightness, which lies in the reason-judgment itself and which a possible external spectator who, so to speak, recomputes the judgment-reckoning in place of the judger does *not* perform, represents a peculiar saturation of the judgment-intention, a saturation by the goal itself, an inner fulfilledness of the adequation of the meaning in the givenness of the meant. It stands plainly so in every act-domain, with every kind of meaning and position-taking, and so specially in the will-domain too: in the objective attitude we judged insightfully about will-rightness, performing an essence-analysis of the components that make up full rightness in the highest sense, and the will-goal lies fully and wholly in what we designated as objectively owed, the highest goal-rightness, a convenience in the extended sense of the word, belonging to the will of the best among the attainable. But in itself, originarily considered, the value does not lie in the goal-right willing; here too we have the difference between blind will and reason-will. A will too can be objectively considered, its rightness recomputed, as it were, by some rational subject; but only when the willer himself, in himself as it were, reckons — that is, when he lets himself be determined in his willing by genuine reason-motives, gives his will-vote not blindly but so that the goal-rightness constitutes itself originarily in his willing — is he in himself a consciousness of an ought to which the willing adequately corresponds, and only then has he originary value. Surely the objective imperative "Do the best among the attainable" is unimpeachable, for we see into its objective validity, and a will that wills otherwise is evidently wrong, "untrue" as it were, missing the goal prefigured in the general essence of the will — just as in the objective consideration of logic we establish that a contradiction may not be judged, which we can analogically express as an imperative, "commit no contradiction, hold to the logical laws as laws of possible judgment-rightness." But although the logical laws say nothing of insight, we nonetheless demand in noetics insightful judging, and so in will-noetics insightful willing; we therefore have also the imperative: *Will and act rationally\!* If your willing is right, it is not thereby yet valuable; only rational willing is valuable. With this twofold imperative — the objective "Do the best attainable" and the noetic "Will rationally and insightfully" — the 1914 lecture-course proper closes.

### **Beilage I (to pp. 47 f., 60 ff., 75, §§ 13–15): Obliquities in My Doctrine of Valuing in the Lecture on Formal Axiology and Praktik**

This research-note is Husserl's self-critical reckoning with the act-classification on which the whole course rests, and it should be read as the author flagging the soft point of his own architecture. He confesses that he had used the term *Werten* (valuing) so generally that it encompassed beauty-valuing and good-valuing, the existentially interested "valuing," yet not so far as to encompass also desiring and willing — and that in doing so the rejoicing (*Sich-Freuen*) was not clearly separated, indeed not separated at all, from the valuing of the existent as existent or of the non-existent as non-existent. He sets out to make the needed distinctions. First, there is a valuing, called in the lectures "beauty-valuing," that takes no regard to existence or non-existence: presenting to myself a "glorious" woman-figure, she stands before me as "quasi-being," fictioned, yet I do not posit her non-being, do not take position in that way; the aesthetic liking is a "position-taking," an actual valuing of the presented as such in the how of its presentedness — though one may ask whether this is actually *posited*, since one can say the presented is merely "given" and so doxically conscious. Second, the good-valuing: drawing being and non-being into the emotive position-taking, the behavior can be various. Taking a real, temporal being — present, past, or future — I can, convinced that it is, say that "A is, was, will be" is right, is good; in the simplest case A is by its own content subjected to a content-valuing, a beauty-valuing, and conscious as positive value regardless of its existence, pleasing by its presentation-content as this appearing thing, standing there as "value." Then a consequence-valuing supervenes: that the in-itself beautiful, pleasing in its appearance as beautiful, really be, according to the time-modes, is right, is good. More precisely, if the existential position-taking has certainty underlying it, the beauty-valuing transforms into the good-valuing, just as Husserl had always presented; if a modality of the existential position-taking underlies it, we have modalizations of the good-valuing — supposing that A be, then A would be good. Judged universally, that a beautiful be at all is good; but here a distinction is to be made between the aesthetic valuing as an *appearance-valuing*, where manifold appearances of one and the same thing and manifold modes of presentation are possible, and the *content-valuing*, which concerns not the appearances but the "content" of the object, its objective hold — as when, valuing a person, its excellence according to its various personal sides is in presentation a pleasing thing, and a valuing goes upon this content regardless of any mode of appearance, such an "ideal" being of course good when we take existence into account.

The note's core problem is the relation of valuing to the existence-interested acts, and it is here that Husserl breaks with the identification made in the lectures. How do these content- and existential-valuings stand in relation to the acts "interested" in the existence of objects — joy, grief, regret, desire and wish, and even willing? The question is whether "that A is, is good" (or "is un-good") is equivalent to "that A is, is joyful" (or "is unjoyful"); in the lectures Husserl identified the two, but it must be carefully weighed whether rightly. He inclines repeatedly — and again now — to the view that here a distinction is to be drawn. I value as beautiful; I value, taking existence into account, the being: that A is, is valuable, that A was, that A will be, is valuable, is good; and if I merely "think" the being, I value on the ground of this thinking that A be is valuable, right, though I here merely think and do not yet know that it is. That an A at all be, a so-constituted one, perhaps one in itself, by its content, valuable, is generally to be approved as valuable — but this, he notes, is ambiguous: something of that kind has a content that is to be esteemed as value-content, and that a valuable thing be is itself to be esteemed as valuable; the being of a value is itself valuable, science is something beautiful, and that science really be is itself "beautiful," a good, so that the being of science is not mere being but an "ought-to-be," and one can say that an A at all be is generally an "ought-to-be." Here, Husserl stresses, there is no talk of desire, nor of wish: the longing that lies in wishing, or even in willing, the practical "it shall be" that is the correlate of the "I will," is not in question; and likewise with joy. He had always tried to coordinate joy with desire and wish — "desire" in ordinary speech having a coloring that points back to the I in the special sense of wishing-for-me — joy being the act that satiates and fulfills the longing of desire, only that it is not essential to it to be the end of a transition from longing striving-toward to fulfillment; still, it is the end-mode of desire, and in "desire" lies the unfulfilledness, otherwise than with joy, which is the in-feeling being-satisfied, whether in the satisfaction of a desire that thereby stills itself or in a being-content, being-glad without desire. But valuing is no longing and no being-satisfied as a mere end-mode of longing in the sense of joy: the "being-interested" of joy and grief, hope and fear, wishing in all its forms and even willing is something quite other than valuing and its valuing reckoning-in of existence or non-existence — the former acts being, in the pregnant sense, aiming or terminating ones, but not valuing. How, though, is the difference to be clarified?

Husserl works toward the clarification through the contrast of "cool" valuing and "warm" feeling, and it is this contrast that yields the note's positive doctrine. The question is how connection and separation are radically to be understood: does there belong to every joy, every wishing, as a component or substrate, a "valuing," a holding-for-beautiful or holding-for-good — or only in the case of the reason-acts? But then the question recurs the more, for are the blind acts not "meanings," does a valuing not lie "hidden" in the wishes, and what does this hiddenness say? The mere valuing is "cool," whereas the acts of joy, of love, of fellow-feeling (joy-with, pity), of wishing in every form are "warm" — though the will can again be "cool" and makes its own difficulties. The value of the emotionally warm acts depends on the value of the goals upon which they direct themselves, since only they are aiming; if I value something as good, the joy at it is justified and the wish for it justified — but can I then say that every rejoicing, every wishing, *implies* a valuing? Here, Husserl says, the investigation is to be set. Especially with joy: are we to distinguish the warm feeling of joy, the rapture, and the value-holding founding it, a quite other feeling and really no feeling at all? Are we not to grasp the concept of joy more generally, so that we speak of aesthetic joy, enjoyment, as of the "joy" at a fact, at a being? The aesthetic valuing — the originary consciousness of the beautiful and the ugly — was the motive, the ground of the joy at being, of the aesthetic rapture, of the aesthetic being-repelled; the holding-for-disvalue is not itself a being-repelled, any more than the aesthetic holding-for-value is a being-attracted, but it grounds such. And valuings, Husserl observes, have no intensity: when I am tired I can no longer rejoice in the picture-gallery, or only very weakly, but the valuings do not change — they have only differences of "higher value" and "lower value." Wishing, by contrast, has intensity-differences; but the wishing "directs" itself after a valuing that "grounds" it, and this has no intensity-differences but value-differences, differences of value-height and preference. What does it mean that something is wished and another, in comparison, "more wished"? Partly differences of the founding values; but does a wish-value lie in the wishing itself, or is the matter not rather that the wish itself is to be evaluated — more wished equals more wish-worthy, the wishing being more valuable and, correlatively, the wish? Likewise "A is more joyful than B" means objectively that A is more valuable than B, after which the joy orients itself — but not only that, for the joy for A is *better* than the joy for B; the joys are evaluated, as against the intensities, since I can rejoice more strongly over the lesser.

Husserl extends the analysis to the will and reaches the note's most consequential proposal — a three-class scheme of position-takings. Willings, resolves, deeds too are evaluated. The will can show differences of "energy" and can be more or less passionate, but that then concerns the intensity of the underlying desires: I will something and desire it passionately, and will and desire do not cancel each other — I passionately wish the victory and will the victory — yet I need not will passionately, and the will itself probably has no intensity-differences of passionateness at all. The will does not belong in the series of the desiring, longing acts, joy and wish; but a word is lacking here, for the will is also a longing, only that it brings in a new thing that presupposes longing but is not longing, not desire and wish: the *fiat*, the practical "It shall be\!", of which it is a question whether it has phenomenological differences of "energy" in itself. Are we not, Husserl asks, to *parallelize* willing and valuing and to set them over against all the "Gemütsakte"? And are we not to say that the properly "position-taking" acts, the proper theses, are: judgments (being-positings), valuings (positings of the beauty- and good-being, the value-being), and willings (practical positings, positings of the willful ought-to-be)? Then the doctrine of the "acknowledgments" and "rejections," the approvals and disapprovals — another word for the "position-takings" — has its good sense: being-approvals and being-acknowledgments, valuing approvals, willful approvals; over against which stand the receptive intentional experiences of the presentations, perception and memory but without position-taking, and the receptive feelings, and then the feelings produced by the position-takings, founded in them — the desires with the joys, which confer on the objects of the joy the joyfulness, an objectivity that is objective if it withstands an objective valuing — while feelings as such are no position-takings, no theses, no "valuing" acts in the widest sense. Correlatively we would have a threefold: the being, the good (value), the ought-to-be (the practically good); and Husserl asks whether the latter two belong perhaps in one class, and answers: no. I value present, past, future, value being of every kind as good, and I speak my willful "It shall": for the present it already stands there as good, so I value it, but I can value it in addition as right — it is right so, if it is in my will-sphere, in the will-attitude; it is so, and as a good and best may it stay so, it is right so; while for the future it shall be so, through the will, practically. The note ends with the deferred question about preference itself: how does it stand with the choice of the best, as with all preference and subordination in the sphere of valuing — how do I recognize the better, and is the preferring act a judgment-act, or is it not rather an act belonging to valuing itself, just as the preferring act for the holding-probable is a doxic act? Husserl leaves the question open; but the Beilage has, in passing, recommended exactly the cleaner act-taxonomy — judging, valuing, willing as three irreducible thetic genera, distinct from the warm founded feelings — that the *Studien zur Struktur des Bewußtseins* would pursue, and it candidly marks the place where the course's working notion of "valuing" was too coarse.

### **Beilage II (to § 21): The Exclusion of Empty Possibilities from Practical Consideration**

This short note supplies the missing piece of § 21's open problem — how the practical realm is bounded so that a best can exist at all — by introducing a probability-threshold below which possibilities do not count. In the practical realm, Husserl states, the law holds that empty possibilities of goods and evils, those for which nothing speaks and against which equally nothing speaks, are to be set as zeros. Better still: for a really possible good, and in general a real possible, to come into question as a practical one for me, some preponderant *chance* must speak for the possibility of being able to realize it — positive and negative instances must not hold the balance; and if such a chance is not present, then it is admittedly not to be said that the thing is something unattainable, but it does not concern me. Whether this is precise enough Husserl himself questions with an example: I could resolve to become a general, a great reformer, a founder of a religion — are these practical possibilities? At any rate these goals depend on so many contingencies that I must say already on that account that it would be infinitely improbable to attain them, even if I possessed the positive endowments for it; and whether I have them I do not know, nothing speaking for it and perhaps nothing against it, so that it cannot be reckoned at all, the probability being equal to zero. I have, then, an empty horizon filled with mere possibilities, with arbitrary imaginabilities against which nothing speaks and for which nothing speaks, or with such possibilities for which as good as nothing and against which infinitely preponderant grounds speak; and this horizon I can treat as not present. I have only to restrict myself to the goods attainable in certainty or in a certain finite probability, and indeed only the positive ones, and I have to take the horizon only so far as these givennesses reach, so far as I find such goods — as, for instance, if I credit myself with eternal life, the here-and-now suffices, for of the beyond I know nothing, though for one who believes himself to know of it the matter already stands otherwise. The bounding of the practical realm is thus not arbitrary but fixed by the real chances of attainment, and the merely logical, zero-probability possibilities are struck out in advance.

Husserl then connects the note to the reflexive goods of reason — the very point § 20 had established — and to the temporal structure of practical deliberation. How, then, am I to order the psychic goods and the outer goods, with the back-relation that the valuing of a good is itself a value, that the willing of a good is itself good, that the will to will in future only good is itself again a good — how is such a thing itself to be set into the reckoning? I direct the will upon the future, upon the future best that I would realize; the present will directed upon the best and upon the attainment of the insight what the future best is does not belong to the future good but is a good of the present, a good of which subsequent reflection recognizes that it was a good — so it is itself a good to be referred to the future-sphere and practically possible, namely the resolve: in future I will, as often as new horizons open, seek to win the insight what is the best, and form the will to realize the best to the best of my powers. The question then is what role this resolve — to keep forming this resolve again and again, or to remember it and hold it through — plays. Husserl works the temporal predicament concretely: willing in the insight that A is the best among the attainable, the best among the attainable being meant for an I in the strictest generality, I must consider what the best among the attainable is, what I can do, what is attainable — but while I weigh this, the time passes, and perhaps something especially valuable escapes me; yet I could just as well choose something valuable as something bad, so it is better first to suspend the choice and consider. But how far should the consideration extend — what shall I do in a minute, what in the next hour, what for all the future? Ideally there is an I that has its future-horizon, surveyable and with the possibility of finding attainabilities in it, possibly infinitely many still unknown; I see, say, A1, B1, A2, B2 — these alone I see, of others I simply know nothing — and among the seen I see insightfully that G is the best among the attainable, falling into the time-span Z that I survey, which perhaps still contains much that I do not see, good or evil. What I do not know does not trouble me (*was ich nicht weiß, macht mich nicht heiß*): were I not to do G, a value would at any rate be left undone, and were A left undone, a value too, but "it" is no loss, since its value is contained absorbed in G. It is indeed thinkable that the future richly compensates me, that it brings who knows what beauty; but nothing speaks for that, otherwise I should have drawn it into consideration — and possibilities and probabilities do come into question, but empty possibilities are to be excluded. A sure good is to be preferred to a merely possible one, and even a possible, presumable good to a merely possible one; so for my practical consideration there come into question only the goods and evils for whose attainability something speaks, some chance having to be there that I could attain some good — and as soon as it is there, it comes into account for me. With this the bounding of the practical realm is secured against both the infinite regress of empty possibilities and the paralysis of unending deliberation, and Part A of Husserliana XXVIII closes.

# **Commentary on *Vorlesungen über Ethik und Wertlehre 1908–1914* (Husserliana XXVIII), Part B, pp. 163–232**

This continues the paragraph-by-paragraph commentary into Part B of Husserliana XXVIII — the *Einleitung* and *Schlußstück* of the 1911 lecture course *Grundprobleme der Ethik und Wertlehre*, together with the Beilagen (III–VII) appended to it. The conventions are those of Part A: Husserliana pagination, German technical terms retained with an English rendering on first use, translated headings, equal-depth coverage, prose only. Part B is, chronologically, the redaction between the 1908/09 original and the 1914 course of Part A; the *Einleitung* is the long programmatic introduction to "the idea of philosophy" that Husserl prefixed in 1911, and the *Schlußstück* is the 1911 formulation of the formal theory of rational choice that Part A presents, expanded, as §§ 18–21.

A few interpretive cautions specific to Part B. First, the *Einleitung* builds toward an idea of God, of a most perfect world, and a most perfect consciousness; these are deliberately *ideas* and *ideal possibilities* — apriori constructions and regulative ideals — not metaphysical assertions, and Husserl ends the relevant section by calling them "intimating questions, no more." The theology that results is itself an *apriori* discipline "handling an idea, not a reality." Second, the "theoretical virtuousness" of nature (its fittedness to the ideal of knowledge) is taken from Kant's *Critique of Judgment* and is expressly *not* to be read as a biological thought-economy useful for species-preservation. Third, the diagnosis of the "naivety" of the natural sciences and the "hidden countersense of every natural theory of knowledge" (§ 5\) is Husserl's epistemological route to the phenomenological reduction: a theory of knowledge built on natural-scientific (psychological) ground cancels itself, exactly as logical psychologism did in Part A. Fourth, the *Prinzipienlehren* — formal and regional ontologies, and their correlative noetic disciplines — are presented as the *Leitfaden* (clue) for phenomenology, and the whole apparatus of Part A's logic–ethics parallel reappears here as a step within the program of a phenomenology of reason. Fifth, "philosophy" is progressively widened from the science of absolute *knowledge* to the science of absolutely perfect *consciousness* and *personality* in general, so that ethics and the value-disciplines are not appendages but belong to philosophy's core.

## **Einleitung. The Idea of Philosophy**

### **§ 1\. The Purely Theoretical Interest and the Tendency toward Perfect Knowledge. The Course of Natural Knowing from the Individual to Explanatory Theory**

Husserl opens the 1911 introduction by problematizing the very classification under which the course stands. From of old, ethics has been ranked, alongside logic and aesthetics, among the philosophical disciplines; but that ranking does not yet establish that they are philosophical disciplines in the proper sense. Up to Kant's time the whole complex of scientific disciplines that we today separate, as natural sciences, quite self-evidently from philosophy was still reckoned to philosophy; and even now, in wide circles, the empirical-psychological disciplines count as philosophical *par excellence*, although it is beyond doubt that in a few generations their separation from philosophy will be so decided and clean that no scientifically educated person will any longer think, under the title "philosophy," of psychophysics or any other empirical psychology. The historical fluidity of the boundary is thus the first thing to be set aside.

It would, Husserl insists, misjudge fundamentally essential differences to explain the division between philosophical and non-philosophical disciplines from mere grounds of thought-practice, to trace it back to mere conveniences of the division of labor. It takes, to be sure, a relatively high development of philosophy to grasp more determinately the deeper sense of the distinction between the specifically philosophical and the non-philosophical. The question what philosophy is, what gives it an own right over against all other sciences, which irrecusable knowledge-interests it represents against them, and in what sense it does not coordinate itself with the other sciences but sets itself over against them all — that is itself a philosophical problem, and by no means one of the easiest. The self-referential character of the question is itself a mark of the philosophical.

To treat this problem in full thoroughness and detail cannot, Husserl says, be the task of these lectures, devoted as they are to the fundamental problems of ethics and value-theory — it would easily require the whole time of the semester. But much depends on our having brought to clarity, at least in the main points, the sense of philosophical science over against non-philosophical and pre-philosophical science; for only in connection with the idea of philosophy in general can we understand what philosophical ethics, philosophical praktik, philosophical aesthetics, and philosophical value-theory are, and what systematic significance the particular problem-groups he is to single out may claim within philosophy as a whole. The introduction is thus motivated as indispensable orientation, not as a digression.

Husserl makes a first attempt at a definition. Philosophy is the science that represents the highest knowledge-interests, or the one that is consciously moved by the idea of perfect, absolute knowledge. It falls to philosophy, then, to develop scientifically, to determine and clarify, this at-first-unclear and confused idea — the idea to which all knowledge orders or subordinates itself but which none fully satisfies — to develop the principles and principial disciplines belonging to it, and to apply these, as ideal norms, methodically to all particular knowledge. The particular knowledge is thereby to be stamped into philosophical knowledge: it is to be ordered into a systematic course of knowledge toward the ideal of absolute, in every respect most perfect knowledge. All isolated knowledge, whether individual or general, shall lose its isolation and particularity, shall order itself into upward-leading systematic connections of knowledge, and shall likewise lose its imperfection in other respects, its imperfect clarity and insight; it shall hold the rank of a mere transitional level in the ascending course to absolute knowledge. Husserl flags in a footnote that the idea of knowledge here itself needs clarification — whether the knowledge-act is meant, or rather knowledge as "produced" truth and as the "abiding" possession of the knowing I, and whether we are not led from the individual I to the *community* of knowing subjects and their common acquisition and possession of knowledge — a pointer toward the intersubjective and habitual dimensions that the marginal self-criticism of § 3 will press.

He then exhibits this guiding ideal as the bringing-to-consciousness of a tendency already latent in all theoretical interest. The philosophical interest has, in fully conscious fashion, as its guiding goal what announces itself in every purely theoretical interest as a hidden tendency: the tendency toward perfect knowledge lies in all purely theoretical striving. It feels itself ceaselessly driven toward the greatest possible distinctness, clarification, and fullest grounding; and it feels itself ceaselessly unsatisfied with the isolated fact, the isolated law, unable to remain fixed there — pressed to rise from the particular to the more general, from the fact to the law, from lower to higher generality, and, not content with that, to run through all possible theoretical doctrines, to build encompassing theories, to connect theories into comprehensive theories, and so ever further; whereby the narrow circle of objectivities first taken into view by the theoretical interest widens, the unity of theoretical knowledge reaching ever further from domain to domain without limit. Bringing these tendencies to clarity, we see that they serve, in hiddenness, the goal of the highest unification, that they are hostile to scattered, connectionless knowledge, that they will tolerate nothing untheoretical and ununified, and just as little an end, so long as any *plus ultra* is still thinkable. And to all this belongs the tendency to fully demonstrating clarity: not systematic progress and unification in general is the goal, but a *system* that is fully illumined, or at each step to be illumined, by the light of evidence. The goal is thus not mere coherence but evident coherence.

Husserl traces this telos through the course of natural knowing. At first there is naive experiencing and thinking; the objectivities of nature, physical and psychic, draw the interest to themselves, an interest at first practically determined and delivering knowledge adequate to the generally driving practical needs of individual and community life. But the knowledge-interest frees itself ever more from the contingent needs of life, and a purely "theoretical" knowledge-interest grows up, determined purely by the values lying in knowledge itself; this pure theoretical interest pursues the being of the experienced nature-objectivities, their inner and outer properties, their being-interweavings, their causal dependencies, and is led from particulars on into the being-interweaving with other particulars. The regular, the general, presses itself forward, its knowledge standing there as a higher value and awakening a correspondingly higher theoretical interest directed at greater generality; from generalities of lower stage the interest is driven to those of higher, and so the idea of theoretical knowledge — as knowledge that seeks law and theory and explains the particular and individual from laws — works itself through. Sciences form themselves, researching theoretically generally delimited object-domains; and in each such science a philosophical trait holds sway, lying in the science-tendency as such, in the striving-beyond the isolated to the general, the theoretical-systematic. Everywhere the ideal works itself through, to give systematic unity to all actual and possible knowledge falling within the conceptual frame of the domain, by relation to highest laws and to an all-spanning theory belonging to them; so that, in idea, to every natural science belongs a unity of explanatory theory, a fundamental lawfulness from which the unified theory deduces in systematic deduction all the laws belonging to the domain, by means of which every determinate being can then be grasped, as necessary under the given circumstances, and so explained. (Husserl notes in a footnote the stages: descriptive science, exact science, unambiguous determination, and exact conceptual necessity.) Natural knowledge comes from different starting points to different scientific domains and sciences; and the unity-drive that holds sway in each is satisfied only restrictedly in their relative unity, for nothing in all knowledge is wholly isolated, and systematic relations pass over from the knowledge-objectivities of one domain to those of another. Man, surrendering himself to the natural theoretical drive, is thus occasionally pointed beyond the particular science to problems that set different sciences in relation to one another — objectively, to problems that establish relations between object-domains. The telos of unity thus already breaches the boundaries of the special sciences from within.

### **§ 2\. The Question of Right concerning the Reason and Unreason of the Cognizing and Valuing Acts**

Husserl now opens the second, reflexive direction in which natural knowing is driven — the turn from things to the knowing of things, which will generate the formal and noetic disciplines and, decisively, the value-sciences. The natural knowledge-striving is, in the development of the sciences, pressed also to quite differently constituted problems. Knowledge goes upon truth; but what is posited judgmentally as being or being-thus — partly on the basis of direct experience, partly in the connection of general and indirect thinking — proves subsequently, in the measuring against experience (or against equally-ranking acts of givenness-consciousness), to be not-being, or not so, or not so quite surely. Dark motives of doubt become felt, the judgment lacks clear grounding, and the supposed knowledge appears discredited as sham-knowledge. In the development of knowledge into scientific knowledge, and from lower to higher stages of scientific rigor, man is forced, more and ever more frequently, to turn the gaze from the things to the *thoughts about the things* — more precisely, to the course of the knowing itself, of the intuiting and thinking itself, and to the thought-element itself of the thinking, the methodology of the procedure, and likewise to the general forms of the thoughts as supposednesses, of the judgments as supposed truths, of the supposed questionabilities, probabilities, and so on. The research now directs itself at whether a judging or surmising of such content, motivating itself thus and so through other judging or through memories, perceptions, and the like, is a lawful one; and again at whether the truths and probabilities supposed therein can, by virtue of their form, be truths and probabilities at all — genuine ones — or whether such forms do not rather exclude validity, the being-true. (Husserl adds in a footnote that norm-conformity belongs not only to science but is itself striven for within it, which is a motive for the corresponding general inquiries.) The turn to method is thus driven by the threat of error itself.

Such reflections on knowledge-acts — on thinking and thoughts, and on the meaning-content and validity-content of knowledge, in short on all that the ambiguous concept of "method" comprises — appear in all the sciences. Comparative consideration now shows that they are mostly of a kind that does not bind them to the particular occasions and particular sciences, so that a *more general* inquiry can establish itself, taking its direction upon the lawfulness of cognizing acts in general and upon the forms of possible validity of propositions in general, inferences in general, and that in a generality which takes into account no particularity of special knowledge-spheres or sciences. So there establishes itself a formal mathematics — for example an arithmetic that treats of number-relations and laws, where it is clear that in every knowledge-sphere something countable can occur, to which then number-formulas and more general propositions can find application — and likewise a formal logic as a discipline of the valid judgment-forms in general, inference-forms in general, then a formal logic of individuality, and so on. The formal disciplines arise, then, by abstraction from the matter of all the special spheres.

Husserl now extends the point past the cognition of being to the valuing and willing spheres — the move that founds the value-sciences. What has been carried out holds not only for the advance of human knowledge from sensible and psychophysical experience, social and humanistic experience, to the manifold of ever-newly-branching psychophysical natural sciences and human sciences, ever entering into more intimate systematic interweavings; and it holds not only in the sense that formal logic and mathematics would be related to these sciences alone. For we have not only experience in the narrower sense — experience of things and spirits, of thingly and psychic occurrences. We and others also perform aesthetic and other valuations, and on the ground of these we behold, "experience," values, and we perform value-judgments which, like all judgments, have their truth and falsity. Likewise we exercise practical behavior, and meaning ourselves into our own or another's desiring, willing, acting, we make practical experiences and judge about goods and deeds, dispositions and resolves, ends and means. And as everywhere in valuing esteem, in wishing, in practical behavior there is talk of reason and unreason, so in the judgments built upon these there is talk of truth and falsity. Here too particularity and generality hold sway: we value not only singular being in singular fashion but also generally, and accordingly we utter general value-judgments and draw axiological inferences from them. All this holds already in common life, which has its manifold formulated, if rather vague, rules according to which it judges classes of joys and sufferings, of evaluations and devaluations, of dispositions and actions as right, rational, or as irrational.

Husserl draws the institutional consequence: alongside the natural sciences there stand value-sciences, and the difference lies precisely in the *question of right*. But of these spheres too the theoretical interest can take possession, and can set out to determine scientifically what can be asserted about values and goods, about resolves and deeds, in generally delimited spheres and, on the other hand, in principial and possibly merely formal generality. We have, then, not merely the physical and psychophysical sciences that we call natural sciences, but also *value-sciences*. In the natural sciences, value-holding, wishing, willing, and the like do indeed occur, but only as a *nature-fact* and not under the viewpoint of the question of right — how far the value-holding is itself rational or irrational, how far the values supposed in it are real values, and the like. To these mere natural sciences there are added, then, value-sciences, in which not nature-objects but values are the objects; which does not say that nature-objects do not come into account for them at all, but that these are in question only as possible substrates of value-properties. Husserl notes in his footnotes the careful distinction of the mere fact that such volitions and supposed values occur in humans and communities and determine action, from the *biological* significance of theoretical and practical reason that Beilage III takes up. With the value-sciences thus secured as a proper class governed by the right-question, the parallel of Part A — value-reason as an autonomous reason-domain — is here installed at the level of the very classification of the sciences.

### **§ 3\. Absolute and Universal Knowledge as the Ideal of Philosophy. The Philosophical Disciplines. The Apriori Construction of the Idea of a Most Perfect World and a Most Perfect Consciousness**

Husserl recapitulates the previous lecture and then unfolds the full system of philosophical disciplines, culminating in the apriori construction of the most perfect world. In view of determining the idea of philosophy, he had tracked the teleology that holds sway in all knowledge, in its natural advance from lower to higher stages: the advance from individual experience-givennesses to systematic-theoretical knowledge of scientific domains, and from the systematic research of individual such domains to the research of the interweavings that reach beyond them to higher domain-unities; and further the tendency to pass over from the research of the thing to that of the thoughts. This last means, on the one side, the transition to the research of knowledge itself and of all that it presupposes in order to be genuine, valid knowledge — how it must be grounded on experience, on intuition, how this insight-making intuition must look according to the principally different object-regions, what structure the mediate knowledge must in each case possess. On the other side, given the double sense of the talk of "thoughts," something else can be meant: the research of the knowledge-meaning, of the meant-in-the-knowledge as such and the conditions of validity lying therein — for example the connection between possible truth and the logical forms prefigured apriori in the essence of the judgment-relation. The two directions are the noetic and the apophantic-ontological, exactly as in Part A.

He restates the diagnosis: in all such advance a philosophical trait holds sway in the form of the governing teleology, but, as it were, unconsciously — not grasped thinkingly as an idea, not valued as a knowledge-ideal, not set as the highest goal of the knowledge-will and carried over into realizing activity; in a word, not in the form of philosophy. This sets up the definition. *Philosophy is the science in which the tendency grounded in the essence of all knowledge toward absolute, conceivably most perfect knowledge has become the consciously guiding goal.* That means: philosophy is thematically directed upon the "ideal" of a systematic, theoretically as well as materially all-spanning knowledge, and of course a fully grounded, most perfect one. (Husserl asks in a footnote whether this is directed upon divine omniscience.) To philosophy belong, accordingly and first, all inquiries that serve specifically the interest of the unity of knowledge, and likewise those that aim to bring particular knowledge under the highest viewpoints and so to realize, step by step, the guiding idea of the highest unity of knowledge. Philosophical, then, will be those inquiries that are constitutive for the building of the at-first-formal idea of a highest knowledge-unity, and that with regard to their being so — which does not exclude that such inquiries can be pursued without any consciousness of the relation to the highest knowledge-idea. Philosophy is thus defined by a *function* and an *orientation*, not by a fixed subject-matter, and a discipline can be philosophical "in itself" though pursued in ignorance of its philosophical office.

Husserl enumerates the philosophical disciplines in ascending order. First, the systematic disciplines comprised under the title of an analytic or formal ontology, establishing truths and theories that are valid in unconditioned generality for all objectivities as such, to whatever object-domains, whatever of the most general object-regions, they may belong: this concerns formal logic in its full natural extension, pure cardinal-number theory, pure ordinal-number theory, combinatorics, the whole of formal mathematics. Philosophical, further, will be knowledge that relates to all objects in another way — knowledge concerning the principial division of the objectivities of possible knowledge in general into object-regions and \-domains, into cardinal categories of objectivities; and likewise all theories and laws that belong apriori to the idea of each object-region, and that further concern, in pure generality, the essence-connections of objectivities of different regions. Philosophical, then, is what is often, if very unclearly, called the doctrine of categories; philosophical, further, the apriori ontology of nature belonging to the region of nature — not only Kant's pure natural science but also the pure theory of time, the pure theory of space (the well-known science of geometry), the pure theory of motion, and so on. Philosophical in this sense would be, further, the ontologies relating to the remaining regions and domains — and so, what here especially interests us, every discipline that, if such a thing were exhibitable, systematically researches an apriori of the axiological and practical sphere, as a possible pure value-theory, pure praktik. The pure value-theory and pure praktik of Part A are thus located precisely as the regional ontologies of the value-sphere.

Husserl then introduces the highest, most distinctive disciplines: the constructive teleological ones. Finally, and in the highest sense, philosophical are the constructive teleological disciplines that he had tried to sketch at the end of the previous semester (1910/11) in his logic lectures. It is a matter of a construction to be carried out on the ground of all the regional ontologies — an *apriori* construction of the idea of a world most perfect in every respect, and of the correlates and norms belonging to this idea. That this must be no fantastic but a scientifically graspable matter he had then shown in detail. He had pointed, for instance, to a trait of this constructive teleology that repeatedly occupied Kant's thinking, already in the *Critique of Pure Reason* and especially in the *Critique of Judgment*: the remarkable adaptation of nature to the value-ideal of human knowledge — not, however, understood as adaptation in the sense of the modern biological doctrine of evolution, in the sense of a thought-economy useful for the preservation of the species. The given nature, the nature that is fact, shows, he had said, a remarkable *theoretical virtuousness*: it is so constituted that the knowledge researching it is able to realize high intellectual ideals. It is classifiable in comprehensive systems of unified classification that satisfy theoretical interests; it stands not merely under laws, and laws that order themselves into theories, but all theoretical knowledge, so it seems, orders itself into the unity of one knowledge-system, all theories seem to order themselves into a unity of theory with one unified all-spanning natural lawfulness. So the factual nature corresponds, be it perfectly, be it quite closely, to a knowledge-ideal; it is just so constituted that it is able to satisfy high, or even most highly strained, theoretical interests. The guard against the biologistic misreading is explicit: nature's fittedness to knowledge is an axiological fact about the value of knowledge, not a survival-mechanism.

Husserl insists that this can be made exact and that it concerns *grades* of the value of knowledge. We held, he says, that such reflections need not move, either in concreto or above all in abstracto, in vague generality, but that it is very well possible, in a scientifically exact way, starting from the idea of a nature in general, to sketch the various systematic possibilities of an actual nature, and on the other side to delimit more exactly the ideal of theoretical knowledge. In these considerations it is not a matter of the consideration of knowledge insofar as it is truth at all, nor of the value of truth or of correct knowledge as such, but of the consideration of the various and very graduated values that correct knowledge, ideally taken, can have according to the *theoretical nature of the known* as such. That 1 \+ 1 \= 2 is a rock-solid truth, but it will not greatly excite us; it appears to us no high value, it vanishes like a nothing over against the high value of the knowledge, say, of the theory of number-fields. The value of knowledge is thus differentiated by the theoretical richness of its object, not exhausted by mere correctness.

He sketches how the construction would proceed, on the model of mathematical manifold-theory. In the ontologies the essence-laws and theories belonging to the essence — be it of being in the most general sense, be it of being in the sense of the cardinal being-regions, for example nature and spirit — are developed. Assuming these theories already brought to a corresponding development, one could, on their ground, research apriori in systematic construction the particular being-possibilities, the possible worlds, enclosed in their lawfulnesses. The model and at once a piece of this achievement is offered by the formal manifold-theory of mathematics. Husserl recalls how there, on the ground of the apriori laws of pure logic, pure cardinal- and ordinal-number theory, and so on, the formal ideas of possible "manifolds" — the Euclidean and non-Euclidean — are systematically constructed in rising generality, in the constructive procedure that the mathematicians, very misleadingly, are accustomed to call "axiomatic." With regard to such constructions one can now ask which particular shapes of physical or psychophysical nature, apriori constructible within a formal-general idea of nature in general, would be those that, from the standpoint of the valuation of knowledge, could claim the highest perfection; and one can ask further which among these possible worlds would be preferable under the viewpoint of the other possible cardinal valuation-directions, or rather, beforehand, which constructive possibilities lie in these other directions and how the choice between them is to be carried out. The construction of an optimal world is thus a problem of *choice* among apriori possibilities under several valuation-directions — the very form of problem the Schlußstück and Part A's absorption-law address.

Husserl now develops the three valuation-directions that the knowing-valuing-willing consciousness opens. Knowing consciousness is at once valuing and willing. We first thought the knowing valued; it showed that the value of the knowing — its correctness always presupposed — shows itself dependent on the kind of theoretizability of the known being, and so there grows the first great valuation-direction and the selection of the theoretically most perfect nature, or possibly natures in the plural. But the valuing can also direct itself upon nature in another respect — upon its material content without regard to the advantages of theory, upon reality itself — and can at the same time direct itself upon the valuing itself and its greater or lesser perfection. Presupposing again correct valuing, so that I grasp real values: now reality can be richer or less rich in the rightly valuable, more beautiful and less beautiful; the more manifold and the more values it would realize, the higher it perfectingly increases in the connection, the more valuable it would be. At the same time it is clear that a valuing consciousness, a life on the side of correct valuing, would be the more perfect the more perfect it finds to value in the reality in which it lives and to which it is related — so that, correlatively, most perfect reality and most perfect valuing consciousness demand each other. And again we consider the direction of desiring, willing, acting: such consciousness is built upon value-holdings but is itself subject to valuation, and a possible world can be better and worse from the standpoint of the will-stance, the more favorable a field it offers for valuable action and creative deed, the higher the practical values, the goods, it permits to be realized. (Husserl articulates the structure in a footnote: first the valuation of knowing and its theoretical content; second the valuation of valuing, with the independence of the value of valuing from the valued; third the valuation of willing.)

Husserl reaches the culminating pair of ideas and immediately submits them to a long self-critical footnote. Assuming the apriori belonging to the value- and good-sphere were systematically developed like that of psychophysical nature, then in all the indicated directions constructions of the apriori possibilities and selections among them by highest value-viewpoints would be carried out. All these strivings evidently culminate in the explication and systematic development of a highest idea, or a pair of ideas: the idea of an apriori *most perfect among the possible "worlds"* and the idea, standing in correlative relation to it, of a *most perfect personality, a most perfect life*, which precisely as such apriori demands relatedness to a conceivably most perfect life-content and thereby to a conceivably most perfect reality as the field of most perfect knowledge, most perfect beauty-valuation, and most perfect will-activity. In the footnote Husserl marks the error of these considerations — valuable as they are — namely that everything is tuned to the most perfect "consciousness" as the most perfect life-stream or "life," while of the *I* (sleeping and waking), of the individual I and of the personal I-community, nothing is said: of the I that lives perfectly only when it knows of its perfect life, that must then freely perform a unified goal-setting of manifold goals into an infinite horizon, that must be an I perfecting itself and directed at its perfection, its ideal I; the error that I and lived-experience are not everything, but that with each act the I unfolds, proves itself, and develops, setting in itself a habitus. And he raises the sharper question whether the goal-setting of any I, directed at a perfect life, a perfect personality, hence at divine perfection, is rational at all — whether an unreachable goal is not irrational, an absolutely, essentially unreachable one not widersinnig, so that the idea of divinity itself would be essentially irrational, like that of an absolutely perfect world; and he asks what then is the highest practical goal of man, and what, as idea, the conceivably essentially most perfect personality and world are. He adds that it should also have been stressed that all considerations about such constructions can only be *formal*. The footnote is thus a standing reservation: the ideal of perfection may itself harbor a countersense, and the construction is in any case only formal.

Husserl closes the section with the cautious extension to the idea of God. From here it would be asked whether and how far all this does not join together into the idea of the most perfect being, the idea of divinity, insofar as it might turn out that being is not thinkable except in the correlation of being and consciousness — so that the apriori most perfect being must be grasped as the idea of the most perfect consciousness, perhaps necessarily demanding a division into a plurality of individually-unified consciousnesses in which a most perfect "world" constitutes itself, and perhaps also necessarily demanding in this constitution an immanent development, in which, with the further development of consciousness, a steady creative value-increase, a progressing realization of the idea of a perfect spirit-world in relation to a perfect nature, would take place. One would then have to distinguish between the absolute, development-guiding idea and the infinite immanent consciousness- and world-development itself — at which, Husserl notes, one will at once be reminded of Aristotelian thoughts. But these, as said, are no firm assertions, only intimating questions, no more. The conditional, interrogative register is essential: the whole theological culmination is offered as a horizon of problems, framed by the correlation of being and consciousness, not as a doctrine.

### **§ 4\. Apriori Philosophy and Metaphysics**

#### **(a) The Science-Theoretical Character of the Logical and Noetic Disciplines**

Husserl registers the striking result of § 3 and then divides philosophy into its apriori and aposteriori halves. What strikes us in this consideration as very remarkable is the circumstance that the unfolding of the idea of most perfect knowledge leads us to the idea of the most perfect consciousness and the most perfect world, and to the theological ideal of divinity. Since spirit-life is accomplished as consciousness of lower and higher form, we intimate that the consideration and development of the highest life-ideals, of the most perfect human existence, must lead from another side to an idea of philosophy that coincides with the one we won from our starting-point, the idea of absolute knowledge, and are now pursuing into its consequences. So far we went by following the teleology essentially dwelling in the knowledge-life, raising it to clear consciousness of its goal-directions; and this yielded necessarily a series of ideal disciplines relating to the formal essence of being in general and to the being-regions — and among the being-regions there appear not only nature and spirit but also the region of values and goods, as correlatively, among the constituting kinds of consciousness, besides sensible and psychological experience, also the grasping of values and goods on the ground of valuing and desiring, that is willing, acts. And now all these disciplines find their unity necessarily through those constructive, apriori teleologies, which find their highest union in the idea of the most perfect consciousness-life, the most perfect world, and the idea of divinity. But throughout it was a matter of ideas, of ideal possibilities, and not of factual actualities; and insofar as the ideal possibilities were generally valued under highest value-viewpoints, of pure ideals.

With this the idea of philosophy is plainly not exhausted; rather only the idea of an *apriori* philosophy is, an apriori philosophical doctrine of principles, the pure essence-doctrine and norm-doctrine of absolute being thought in formal generality — of all possible absolute being in general. To it we have to oppose the *aposteriori* philosophy, the philosophy of the absolute in the aposteriori, the science of the absolute knowledge of factual being according to its theoretical as well as axiological and practical value-content. The great division of philosophy is thus apriori principle-science versus aposteriori science of the factual — a division Husserl will name, in (b), as that between pure philosophy and metaphysics.

But this still does not quite suffice. The ideal sciences that have joined together for us move throughout in a sphere of *formal* generality, and in their formal concepts — nature in general, spirit in general, sensation in general, and the like — not all concepts are deducibly enclosed, namely not the *material* concepts and the apriori belonging to them. Thus, for example, the formal idea of sensation, of sensible consciousness, does not deducibly imply the idea of red-sensation or tone-sensation, as in the formal sphere the idea of cardinal number deducibly implies the idea of each numerically determinate number. Knowledge that belongs to the essence of the color-manifold, purely immanently, purely essentially — knowledge that is therefore apriori — would not belong to the ideal-philosophical ontological disciplines; still less, of course, would the experience-knowledge of any factual existence, for from essence-laws no facts are in principle to be derived, the essence-laws prescribing to the facts only possibilities, in the manner of ideal norms. The formal-apriori thus does not exhaust the apriori: there is a *material* apriori (the essence-laws of color, tone, and the like) that falls outside the formal ontologies, and a fortiori the realm of facts.

Husserl now shows how the new spheres — material-apriori and factual — gain unity through the ideal-philosophical disciplines, and so defines the science-theoretical office of the ontologies and noetic disciplines. It is easy to see that all scientific knowledge of the new spheres gains unity, and indeed principial unity, through relation to the ideal-philosophical disciplines. All stand under those ideal-philosophical cognitions as their last norms, norms of logical and axiological validity; and all that is known in the experience-sphere also gains relation to the ideals of being-teleology, which function, with regard to this factual total-existence — the factual nature, the factual humanity, the factual culture — as highest normative ideals. In these relations we have to say that all particular science, insofar as it is science in the strict sense at all, orders itself into the idea of absolute knowledge and is, under the viewpoint of this ordering, to be designated philosophical — so every particular natural science. Each factual existence orders itself into the total existence; and to bring this, under theoretical and axiological viewpoints, to most perfect knowledge is a philosophical ideal. Perfection of theoretical knowledge requires, for each given existence, its knowledge in the connection of a theoretical science; but theoretical science is an idea that realizes itself more or less perfectly in actual science-praxis, and an actual science is science in the strict sense only so far as it corresponds to the pure idea of science in general, and more closely to the idea of the pure science of that general form prescribed by the regional category into which the factual existence in question orders itself. For to the perfection of theoretical knowledge there belongs not merely true judgment, but available insight into the theoretical connections in which the judgment receives perfect grounding, and further into those in which the grounded state of affairs receives its ordering into the systematic being-connections and can be grasped, under highest theoretical viewpoints, as a necessary one. And to that there belongs the cognitive possession of the principial insights that belong to the form of theoretical science as such — that are constitutive for the idea of theoretical science as such — and no less of those that are constitutive for the division of the being-regions and the constitution of the ideas of the regional principle-sciences. In other words: with regard to the possible and actual particular being-sciences, the ontologies of which we have repeatedly spoken — the formal-logical being-doctrine extended to the *mathesis universalis*, and likewise the regional ontologies, the pure space-theory, pure time-theory, and so on — have the character of *science-theoretical* (*wissenschaftstheoretische*) disciplines, or, in an extended sense of the word, of *logical* disciplines.

He completes the architecture with the noetic disciplines and notes their self-referential character. The same holds, in another manner and direction, of the disciplines that, as *noetic*, relate to the knowing itself and to the norms of its rightness and wrongness grounded in its essence-shapes — be it in all-encompassing generality, correlative to formal logic and mathesis as the formal-most-general being-doctrine, or correlative to the regional being-disciplines, for example as an apriori doctrine of the right of nature-knowledge. Everywhere, science in the highest sense — science that is to satisfy the highest knowledge-interests in every respect — requires not merely right method and right results but fully conscious insight into the essence and the principles of rightful method, and that both in the noetic direction and in the direction of the meanings and the meant objectivities. That means that the pure idea of science in general and the pure ideas of the possible science-shapes relating to the division of the being-regions must be given in clear and full insight, in order that particular science, in constant normative relation to these ideas, can be a perfect and thereby absolute science. So perfect science presupposes these ideas, themselves developed in the form of sciences — as those described ontologies and noetic disciplines — which for their part, as sciences, have that peculiar back-relation upon themselves that belongs to the principial essence of genuine philosophical principle-doctrines. The ontologies and the noetics are thus not optional preliminaries but the constitutive framework within which alone a science can be "absolute," and they characteristically apply to themselves.

Husserl closes the subsection with the question of completeness and infinity. One imperfection in genuine science remains, it seems, insurmountable through all this: the imperfection with respect to extensive completeness. But it is not said whether in the theoretically knowable objectivity — for example in the idea of nature — an infinity of possible knowledge-progress is not necessarily enclosed, whether, that is, the idea of strict science is not the idea of a methodical unity that must, within infinite being-horizons, set and solve systematically ever-new tasks, advancing, in a systematically lawful way, from tasks to tasks, from theories to theories, from lower to higher stages. To establish this belongs, of course, in the domain of the pure philosophical principle-doctrines. The "imperfection" of incompleteness may thus be no defect but the very essence of a science whose object is an infinite horizon — a thought that prefigures the later idea of science as an infinite task.

#### **(b) The Consideration of Reality under the Teleological and Theological Ideals**

Husserl now adds the axiological and theological dimensions to the absolute being-doctrine and reaches the problem of the "real significance" of the idea of God. To the absolute being-doctrine there belongs not merely the knowledge of existence — the knowledge of the factual psychophysical reality under the philosophical regulation of the science-theoretical principle-doctrines; rather there belongs to it also the consideration of nature, in the narrower and wider sense, under *value*\-viewpoints, hence under the philosophical regulation of the axiological-practical principle-doctrines, and finally under the unifying *teleological*, or, as we could also say, *theological* ideals. We could express it thus: the natural-scientific problems, brought to solution under the absolute viewpoints of a theoretical philosophy, are not yet the last reality-problems; or: nature-philosophy as theoretical nature-philosophy is not nature-philosophy in the highest and last sense. The physical nature, the life, the spirit-world with its manifold historical culture-formations are not only givennesses of the theoretical consciousness but also of the valuing and willing consciousness; they therefore demand also a normative relation to the axiological and practical principle-doctrines, and require a measuring against the highest being- and life-ideals, which join together into the ideal of the most perfect being and consciousness, the absolute-theological ideal.

Here arises, with regard to the factually given reality, which we already think as theoretically known, a last being-problem to which humanity early found itself pressed: the problem of the *real significance of the idea of God*, or the problem of *creation*, the problem of the realizing power of absolute ideals. Can an idea at all — and now even an idea of God, as the highest normative idea of possible world-reality in general — regulate the factual existence of reality, its being, its being-thus, its further self-development, not merely *normatively* but *realizingly*? Can such a regulation have a sense at all, and which sense can it have? And again, if the latter, can one, and by what method can one, decide whether this factual world is factually a God-world? Husserl marks the question as one of whether a regulative ideal could also be a *realizing* (efficient) ground — a question of sense before it is a question of fact.

He develops the Platonic conception to bring out both the ideality and the apparent surplus of "realizing power." According to the Platonic view, God is the idea of the Good — hence an idea. Therein lies that God is, in the true sense, no reality, no thing among things, in no place and in no time, and naturally also not the All of things itself, being thoroughly different from the spatial-temporal total-reality. And yet this sun in the realm of ideas is, according to Plato, the last source of being. It is first the absolute source — so we could lay it out — of all the ideas of possible realities that order themselves into the idea of the most perfect world. But not only that: it is to be a *realizing power*, and accordingly, in a certain sense, "reality," even the last, absolute reality, the last being-ground for all empirical being, and a teleological being-ground — which would mean that every empirical being is, at its place, something demanded by the idea of the Good and, on the ground of this ideal demand, necessarily being and being-thus; or that every empirical being is necessary as a phase of the development that belongs unsublatably to being and that is a development toward ideal goals, a being-development under ideal norms. According to this, the idea of the Good would not be reality in the sense of thingly reality, of nature-reality, nor in the sense of the empirical, human-like spirit that is a member of psychophysical nature, but reality in a heightened sense, a *super-reality* that lets all empirical reality emerge super-empirically, supernaturally — that is, not in natural-scientific-causal fashion. What kind of remarkable convictions are these, Husserl asks, that have stepped forth again and again in the history of philosophy? Are they shadings of religious superstitions, or do necessary problems lie here? And can we not find, in their historical solutions, valuable anticipating intuitions? Does the Aristotelian doctrine of entelechies, his talk of the *poioun* (the *nous poiētikos*), perhaps give a pointer to possible solutions, or the Leibnizian myth of a central monad that lets all monads effulge from itself? The historical theologies are treated, then, as repositories of problem-anticipations, not as authorities.

Husserl resolves the matter into the now-familiar division of pure philosophy and metaphysics. Evidently these problems of the given reality, and the questions to be put to it from the highest teleological viewpoints, point back first to philosophical principle-doctrine — to a *pure philosophical theology and teleology*, in which beforehand all apriori questions of sense, all questions that move within the frame of pure idea, of pure possibilities, must be solved. But here too the question put to the given reality cannot be solved by pure philosophy. If we call the science related to factual being, insofar as it would be absolute science satisfying the highest interests, *metaphysics*, then it is clear that metaphysics is nothing other than the continuation of all the actual natural sciences and human sciences, their completion, perfecting, and philosophizing — namely according to the principles developed in the pure philosophical disciplines, according to the ideas and ideals purely shaped in them. Through the whole presentation, Husserl says, there runs the motive of the division between *existence* and *idea*. Philosophy as the science of the absolute must be divided: first into pure or apriori philosophy, which comprises a complex of principle-doctrines, of pure or rational disciplines — pure logic, pure mathesis, pure natural science, pure space- and time-theory, pure essence-doctrine of spirit individual and social, pure value-theory, pure praktik — and to these the noetic disciplines and the disciplines built higher upon them, last and highest the pure teleological being-doctrine and the pure doctrine of God (the latter also an apriori discipline, handling an idea and not a reality); and second, metaphysics as the absolute science of factual reality, grounded throughout in the pure philosophical disciplines, which give it, of course, not premises of being but principles of grounding and of sense-clarification, pure norms and ideals. The pure doctrine of God is thus, emphatically, an *apriori* discipline about an *idea*; whether this idea has "real significance" is a question for metaphysics, not for the pure discipline, and the pure discipline supplies metaphysics with norms, never with existential premises.

#### **(c) The Interweaving of All Kinds of Reason and Their Ideals**

Husserl corrects the one-sidedness of the foregoing by re-centering the whole construction on consciousness in general rather than on knowledge alone. Our consideration so far was one-sided, and precisely because it was led only by the idea of absolute *knowledge*. Starting from the natural empirical fact of human soul-life, we shall yet have to say that knowledge is only a single function within this soul-life, beside others, ever interwoven with it. If we then place ourselves on the ground of the absolute essence-consideration, we have to let drop the relation to the empirical fact of the existence of human beings. Knowing is then a title for a closed upper genus of manifold kinds of consciousness — for kinds of acts that are subject to reason-appraisal, an appraisal for which it depends exclusively on the immanent essence of the acts, on their immanent kind, and not at all on whether they are human acts, lived-experiences of persons within a nature, nor on whether the talk of existing human beings and of nature is right at all.
