For that a nature and a human-world is, and how it is constituted, knowing reason itself first establishes; this determines itself first according to the rationality of certain acts, and that purely out of their own essence. If we now reduce, like the knowledge-acts, so all acts of consciousness in general to their essence-kinds, under exclusion of all natural empiricism, then we have again to say that knowing represents merely *a* basic kind of consciousness, of acts subject to reason-appraisal. Beside the knowing reason there step the valuing and the willing; and all kinds of reason, all kinds of consciousness subject to reason-consideration, are most intimately interwoven with one another — so that one can say it is *one* reason with different regions. This is the same anti-naturalist, essence-oriented widening of "reason" that Part A's first Abschnitt established, here made the basis of the very concept of philosophy.

Husserl draws the consequence: the idea of philosophy is to be widened from absolute knowledge to absolutely perfect consciousness. Accordingly we can now widen the idea, hitherto guiding us, of most perfect knowledge into the idea of a most perfect *reason-life* in general, a most perfect reason-consciousness in general; and we can, exactly corresponding to the double consideration we carried out with regard to knowledge, on the one side develop the pure idea of reason-consciousness in general, and on the other side consider this idea with respect to the factual consciousness, with its factual consciousness-content, in relation to the psychophysical reality factually appearing to it. The thought lies near, then, to widen the concept of philosophy: to regard philosophy not as the science that represents the mere idea of absolute knowledge, but the idea of absolutely perfect consciousness in general, of absolutely perfect personality in general. The question is whether we then in fact come to a wider concept of philosophy, one that spans new disciplines. But so remarkable is the essence-kind of consciousness in general, so remarkable the ideal relations that interweave all the basic kinds of consciousness and all the corresponding reason-connections, that we are led back once more to the same circle of disciplines that we won, starting from mere knowledge. For example, the reason-ideal of a perfect will-life includes, by virtue of the essence-relations between willing and valuing, the ideal of perfect valuing; and no less the ideal of perfect knowledge, which directs itself in every direction upon existence, value, and good. A light consideration would show this, and would equally show, conversely, that the reason-ideal of consciousness with regard to values includes perfection of willing and knowing, and that so, in general, in the absolute total-ideal of a perfect consciousness-life the ideals of the different kinds of reason mutually penetrate and demand one another. According to the order in which one carries out the considerations to be made here, one will obtain the philosophical disciplines in a different sequence — starting, for instance, from the will-function, one will first strike the practical discipline — but the complex of disciplines itself remains the same.

Husserl closes the Einleitung's opening movement by announcing the deepening into phenomenology. He does not wish to enter into the closer execution of these hints, whose goal he had at least to indicate; he holds it more important to deepen the idea of philosophy in a determinate direction and to speak of the complex of disciplines earlier loosely designated as *noetic* — or, what says the same, the *phenomenological* — which are, in a special sense, specifically philosophical. They are so for the sake of the methodical function they have for all knowledge that is to be able to be philosophical; they are so because it is the phenomenological method by which, over against the naive-natural research-way of the pre-philosophical sciences, the way to properly philosophical knowledge is broken. (Husserl notes in a footnote a planned supplement on the idea of the noetic disciplines, phenomenology, with a recapitulation and "essential sharpening" of the train of thought.) The Einleitung thus turns, from its survey of the system of philosophical disciplines, toward its real center: the phenomenological method as the route from natural naivety to absolute knowledge — the theme of §§ 5–8.

### **§ 5\. Natural Science and Philosophy**

#### **(a) The Naivety of the Natural Sciences**

Husserl turns from the system of disciplines to the methodological core of the Einleitung: why the natural sciences, for all their exactness, remain "naive," and why a new dimension of problems is needed to make their knowledge absolute. Proper philosophical knowledge wills to be knowledge in the perfect sense, absolute knowledge; and the tendency toward such perfection lies, as has been shown, in all knowledge, in naive everyday knowledge as in scientific knowledge. The goal would plainly be chimerical if absolute knowledge were not in some measure attainable; indeed, even the knowledge of the goal of absolute knowledge, of its idea, would be powerless, unable to shine before all knowledge as norm-giving, if it could not itself be brought to perfect knowledge. If, then, we consciously pursue this idea and develop it first in the system of the pure philosophical disciplines, we raise with these the claim to absolute knowledge. For only then can logical laws, say, function as norms for all knowledge to be given in advance and rationally further us in the striving to perfect it — in the advance to insight-making grounding and theoretically unifying explanation — if they themselves are really rational, if they are themselves firm, absolutely insight-makable cognitions, not afflicted with whole dimensions of riddles, doubts, unclarities, discrepancies; for any such imperfection would at once transfer itself to the cognitions to be normed and perfected by them.

But is absolute knowledge really, and in any sphere, possible? Is reason not, at bottom, an empty word, a vague talk incapable of realization? Such doubt-questions, Husserl says, press themselves very vividly upon us in view of the fact that all knowledge which first appeared to humanity as fully satisfying, as already scientifically strict — and in particular also those cognitions which gave themselves out, in methodical reflection, as absolute and quite indispensable norms — led, considered under certain viewpoints, to difficulties, indeed seemed in fact to carry whole dimensions of riddles and contradictions with them. The naturally grown knowledge has a certain naivety that does not vanish when everyday knowledge transforms itself, under the influence of logical and in a certain sense already philosophical reflections, into scientific knowledge. The exactness of science is, then, no guarantee against this naivety.

Husserl describes the naive attitude. The naturally grown experiencing and thinking is naive: it finds before itself, as a self-evident givenness, the world, physical nature, the I, the social-spiritual world. To these self-evident pre-givennesses the theoretical interest turns; one pursues, in the theoretical attitude, the experiential constitutions of things and their causal connections, researches the psychic and psychophysical existence, turns the gaze of the theoretical interest upon the social formations, the culture-goods. The theories, theoretical disciplines, and sciences thus grown offer themselves, in the gaze directed upon them, again as givennesses; the very development of the sciences presses toward such reflection upon meaning and validity, and a discipline of higher stage grows up — a logic, a doctrine of valid inferences, proofs, theories, disciplines. The principles of logic state something that lets itself be grasped insightfully without further ado, something findable for everyone, a self-evident reason-norm for all thinking. And it is the same when, in the consideration of human actions and dispositions, of human valuations and aesthetic estimations, value-judgments are uttered — judgments about beauty and ugliness, practical rationality and irrationality — where too scientific consideration may lead beyond everyday reflection and set up general principles that posit as valid what offers itself as self-evidently valid. In this way the constitution of sciences and the advance from scientific cognitions to new ones proceeds in the widest compass. What, then, should be lacking? Should we take offense at such grand, such exact sciences as the mathematical, the mechanical, the astronomical? May we dare to speak here still of a naivety of the thinking?

Husserl answers that the naivety is real and lies in an untouched dimension of problems. Throughout the whole line of the natural sciences the thinking does in fact remain in the state of naivety, insofar as a whole dimension of problems remains untouched in it, on whose solution depends the last sense-demonstration of these cognitions and their transformation into fully sufficient, absolute knowledge. The naively scientific thinking gets at once into embarrassments as soon as one brings it out of its natural objective attitude and forces it to face the relations between consciousness and being that belong to all knowledge — relations that can be weighed at every place of the science but that, in the science itself, do not belong to the theme of its research and theorizing. The drawing-in of the knowing consciousness, which precisely as knowing has things over against it, entangles one in difficulties, in contradictions, which it will not succeed to solve by means of the natural conceptual categories; rather one strikes here upon theories that again and again cancel themselves through countersense. The "new dimension" is thus the one in which the relation of consciousness and being itself becomes thematic — precisely what natural science leaves out.

Husserl spells out the first and most important pre-givenness — that we knowers are humans within nature — and shows how the deceptions of perception generate the modern theories of knowledge. Natural knowledge is determined by certain pre-givennesses taken as self-evident; the first and most important is the self-evidence that we who know are human beings within the nature that stands before our eyes, partly really experienced, partly experienceable — a world with the one space and the one time spanning me and all other psychic beings, in which, living and determined by its effect-rich existence, we get into manifold inner states and perform manifold psychic acts, among them the knowledge-acts, the perceptions, memories, judgments. Knowing, we can turn ourselves to the things of physical nature, to our own and others' soul-life, to the formations of the social world; everything in this world has its manner of being experienced by us and known in thinking about it, and systematic science researches methodically how all these realities are in truth, what hidden properties they have, what laws hold for them. Of course there are here all kinds of deceptions; everyone knows that the senses occasionally deceive, and one noticed very early that, considered in principle, every perception we trust could subsequently prove a deception, an illusion — every single one we may pick out. (Husserl notes that this last passage was later struck, with a marginal note pointing to the strife of methods and theories and to skepticism.) One then considered whether they might not all together be deception; one told oneself that perception and perceived thing are evidently different things, the one "subjective," the other "transcendent" to subjectivity; one fell into doubt whether human knowledge could get beyond itself and ever really assure itself of the being of the transcendent. One spun out all kinds of theories to meet such doubts, and then again theories to make comprehensible that adaptation of human nature to experiential reality which expresses itself in the fact that man, in his psychic acts of knowing, is able to gain valid presentations of what lies outside these acts, outside his consciousness — in short, that he is able to know "objective" truth. These theories gained, with the advances of the reality-sciences, an ever-new content: one came to know physical nature, in an ever more scientific and so content-rich way, with its tone-vibrations, ether-vibrations, energies, and likewise the physiological constitution of man; on the psychological side scientific psychology and psychophysics were added; and so the theories gained an ever more complicated, learned content. By the natural standpoint they were psychological or psychophysical theories, for it is a matter of an explanation of the relation between outer nature and certain occurrences appearing in man, called cognitions.

Husserl now makes the decisive observation: these theories are skeptically self-undermining, and, remarkably, the actual sciences develop without them. In the most general type these theories do not differ from those that were sketched and skeptically turned at the beginnings of science and philosophy. But while at the start it seemed as if the possibility of science depended on the mastering of such problems and on the warding-off of skepticism, the development showed, remarkably, that natural sciences and sciences of reality of every kind could form themselves and develop to a much-admired height without troubling themselves about these problems. The possibility of science proved to be a problem that by no means had to be solved for the enabling of actual science. It is striking, too, that the attempts to solve the problem of the possibility of science again and again take their theoretical material, their grounding premisses, from the already-developed actual science; these theories thus followed after the science but fell out of its proper frame. Physics with all its disciplines, physiology, natural-scientific anthropology in general treat no problems of the possibility of knowledge; they have their well-developed methodology, in whose handling it decides, in the given case with sufficient understandability, what may count as real experience and what not, and how objective knowledge is to be ascertained from experience. It is the same at the higher stage. Into the development of reality-knowledge toward the theoretical natural sciences there intervened decisively the development of certain ideal sciences which, by our orientation, should properly belong to the philosophical sphere — the purely logical and mathematical sciences. In them certain objectivities called ideal — truth, proposition, inference, theory, law, necessity, number, order, geometrical figure, and so on — enter the circle of the theoretical interest and theoretical research. About these objectivities, which with the pure laws belonging to them functioned methodologically and normatively for all reality-knowledge and were, in this normative function, indispensable instruments for the enabling of strict science, one made one's thoughts from the natural standpoint: one reflected on how they could be and yet hold for all reality, actual and possible; how laws might manage to function, as it were before all real being and then in all real being, as ruling powers, putting demands upon it; in what way they stand to the knowing consciousness. But in the development it turns out again that pure mathematics, in all its disciplines, can research these objectivities and laws without troubling itself about such problems concerning their essence, their normative function, the possibility of their knowledge; appertaining theories are indeed not lacking, but they stand outside the line of the mathematics that develops itself further in infinite fruitfulness.

Husserl concludes the subsection by naming the "dimension of imperfection" that no natural unfolding can overcome. All sciences forming themselves in this way naively — epistemologically naively — have a dimension of imperfection that hinders the cognitions they offer from being allowed to count as last, really concluding, as absolute cognitions. It lies plainly in the essence of the sciences that they have lines or planes of possible theoretical unfolding that do not demand a transition into the new dimension; and the fundamentally different intellectual dispositions required for the research-way in the one and the other dimension explain, too, why different specialists took over the knowledge-work in these and those. But the main thing is the insight that — as the image of a new "dimension" can suggest — it is a matter of problems that influence the knowledge-value of the natural science from the start and at every step, and that can never come to solution even at an infinite unfolding of the natural science, so long as it remains natural, that is, in its own plane. The two dimensions are thus not contiguous regions of one plane but orthogonal: no amount of progress within the natural plane reaches the critical dimension.

#### **(b) The Knowledge-Problems. The Hidden Countersense of Every Natural Theory of Knowledge**

Husserl states the genuine problem and then demonstrates, by the self-cancellation of natural epistemology, why it requires a non-natural method — the route to the phenomenological reduction. It is a matter of the problems of consciousness and consciousness-objectivity and of the problems, most intimately interwoven with these, of the last sense-clarification of all being: namely the radical clarification of the relation between real and ideal objectivity, the clarification of the possibility of how the ideal is norm-determining and enabling for all the real, a constitutive principle for the possibility of the real. The genuine problem is thus not whether an outer world exists, but how being of every kind has its sense in and through consciousness, and how the ideal can be constitutive for the real.

He lays down the foundational thesis: all objectivity is possible knowledge-objectivity. All that natural knowledge sets over against itself as being from the outset and then to be known, more closely to be determined, is precisely posited, *in the knowledge*, as being in itself and as thus and so to be determined; and in this there lies, on closer consideration, much: in principle knowledge and knowledge-objectivity are not to be separated, as if the one came contingently to the other. If one says that perceiving, presenting, thinking, knowing is something merely subjective, which can be right and false, to which something can correspond or not, and to which, if it is right, precisely something actual corresponds — that is quite good; but the knowledge itself distinguishes between fiction and reality, distinguishes between "mere" self-thinking, mere "imagining," falsely-assuming, and valid thinking, correct judging. And how does it distinguish? Through the measuring of the relevant act, the relevant knowledge-pretension, against the givenness of the object. But objective givenness is itself again a consciousness. So consciousness measures itself against consciousness. (Husserl glosses "consciousness" in a footnote as the concrete lived-experience of supposed and given objectivity.) The criterion of being is thus internal to consciousness — not because being is "merely subjective," but because the very distinction between the merely supposed and the genuinely given is a distinction *within* the field of consciousness-modes.

Husserl works the point through perception, the natural scientist's own ground. The natural scientist says all his nature-knowledge rests on direct experience, that is, on perception, hence on a consciousness. If it is then said further that the perception is not the perceived thing but only presents it, that the same thing is perceived in many content-different perceptions — once from this side, once from that, once in these causalities, once in others — that it need not be perceived, that it is in reality and merely presents itself in the various perceptions: if it is said so, then we naturally have again to say that, if that is true, it can demonstrate itself only in knowledge. Passing from perception to perception, perceiving again after experience-pauses, the knowledge says in itself: that is the same; one and the same perceived presents itself once so, the other time so. And again the knowledge says: it need not have been perceived; the knowing gaze is to be directed now upon the perceived, now upon the various perception-appearances; evidently the one is to be distinguished from the other. The analogous holds for all objects: however self-evidently an object is given, standing there in its being itself, we can always, by a principial necessity, direct the reflecting gaze upon the consciousness and bring ourselves to the insight that the differences we make between true being and merely supposed being, between apparent and real givenness, between mere thinking and self-grasping, are differences of the *modes of consciousness*, and that in principle no object is to be met with otherwise than in the form of such consciousness-formations related to it. And the being-related-to-it is itself a character, or a "pretension," of these consciousness-acts; and this concerns not merely some particular constitutions of objects but them through and through, into their pure being. If the talk of a being and of an in-itself-being over against knowledge has a sense, it demonstrates itself within the connection of the knowledge-consciousness, and exactly in the sense in which it demonstrates itself it has a sense; and if one divides between the being of the consciousness itself and a being external to it, this division too is performed within consciousness. This is the constitutive thesis of phenomenology stated as the result of an immanent analysis of the sense of "being."

Husserl now stages the natural mind's recoil into solipsism, in order to expose it as the *countersense* of the natural attitude. Of course, coming from natural knowledge, one finds in all this one's difficulties. Is not the latter division a division between the psychic lived-experience — a psychological fact of the I — and the outer world, being outside the I, outside the human being, be it physical nature, be it fellow human beings? And now this whole world, which was for natural knowledge the given and self-evident, suddenly seems to become problematic as to its real being, and at once problematic the principial possibility of a knowledge, in the consciousness, of something outside a consciousness. One will now be inclined to say: when I perform any consciousness, a perceiving or thinking, it is absolutely doubtless that I perform it, that it is in me — and that is intelligible, for here my knowing and intuiting gaze is one with the known, my consciousness is related to itself and grasps something that is really immanently one with it. When, on the contrary, I posit in a consciousness something transcendent to my consciousness, an outer thing for instance, when I gain knowledge with regard to it, make distinctions between correct and false knowledge, real perception and illusion, when systems of cognitions characterized as correct then join for me into a natural science — these are yet all occurrences of my consciousness-sphere. It is of no help here to point to a feeling of evidence, to the character of thought-necessity, for that too is a consciousness-character; beyond my consciousness I simply do not get. So really I can only say: I am, and consciousness-interweavings characterized thus and so are in me. Solipsism is therefore irrefutable, as for instance Schopenhauer too holds — an impregnable fortress whose garrison can, however, also make no sally, since by him the opposite view, which assumes extra-conscious things, is as little provable as it is refutable. Husserl reproduces the solipsist's reasoning at full strength precisely because it is the consistent dead-end of the natural epistemology he is about to convict of countersense.

Husserl delivers the verdict: every natural theory of knowledge cancels itself through a hidden countersense. By a *natural* or *naive* theory of knowledge we understand the theory of knowledge that, under the aspect of natural knowledge, wants to solve the problems of the relation between being and consciousness from its presuppositions — be it even from only a single one of its presuppositions. Of such naive theories of knowledge it is to be said that they struggle with naive, and that is at once skewed and falsely posed, problems; that they all suffer from a hidden countersense, to be brought out in each case through critique, and accordingly cancel themselves through countersense-consequences. This is to be said of all empiricist, but also of almost all "rationalist," theories of knowledge; and an exemplar of a fundamentally perverse problem is just the one last indicated in a solution-form, the highly famous problem of the *possibility of a knowledge of the outer world*, or of the *ground of our belief in an outer world*. Not a few epistemologists would even today declare this problem — which has misled modern philosophy since Descartes and Berkeley — the specific basic problem of epistemology, and engage with great seriousness with the directions of "realism" and "idealism" that diverge in the manner of its solution: as an exemplar of idealism there may serve either solipsism or the doctrine of Berkeley, who dissolves the material outer world into connections of ideas, of lived-experiences of experiencing spirits, but holds the existence of God and of a plurality of human spirits justified through rational inferences; while "realism," on the other side, believes it can infer, through rational inferences, though as a rule only probability-inferences, from the being of one's own consciousness not only to a foreign consciousness but, prior to that, to a material outer world. Both parties, that is, share the perverse problem; the dispute between them is internal to a countersense.

Husserl points to Hume as the consistent endpoint and then universalizes the result to the ideal objectivities. The countersense of such problems and theories, and likewise the countersense of the handled methods of solution, comes to light in the most consequent development of naive "idealist" theory of knowledge, that of Hume. The thought, which presses itself so strongly, that all being must dissolve into consciousness, hence into the psychological, leads here to a consequent psychologism carried through in such purity that it becomes conscious of its own countersense, becomes skepticism, and is thereby at the point of turning over into its opposite — where it is, to be sure, the problem what this opposite is. (Husserl notes that this closing passage was tentatively struck in the manuscript and marked with a question mark.) What is especially important for us, he adds, is the circumstance that the nature-related problems of knowledge, of the relatedness of consciousness and being, are by no means the only and the privileged ones; rather, self-evidently, the very same problems come into question in like manner for all objectivities, and even concern, in the most radical way, precisely the *ideal* objectivities, which with the ideal laws belonging to them enclose in themselves the conditions of the possibility of real being according to all its real determinacies. In our time of a prevailing naive psychologism these problems do not step forth so prominently, because the ideal objectivities seemed to set up no resistance to psychologization and one could be done with them very quickly. The hidden countersense of natural epistemology, then, is not a difficulty about the outer world only; it is the standing absurdity of trying to ground, on natural-psychological presuppositions, the very being-sense and validity that those presuppositions already presuppose — and its remedy is the turn to a non-natural, phenomenological method.

### **§ 6\. The Significance of the Principle-Doctrines for the Critique of Knowledge**

#### **(a) The Principle-Doctrines Offer the Clues for the Phenomenological-Epistemological Investigations**

Husserl recalls the opposition between natural and philosophical science and then makes the decisive methodological point of the Einleitung: the *Prinzipienlehren* — the formal and regional ontologies — are the clues (*Leitfäden*) along which the phenomenological critique of knowledge must proceed. In the last lecture, he says, a certain opposition between natural and philosophical science was discussed; it was said that a naive surrender to all kinds of self-evidences and pre-givennesses characterizes the stage of natural science — pre-givennesses such as this world into which we order ourselves with all our knowledge-acts, or, on the other side, certain ideal objectivities such as the logical concepts, propositions, inferences, laws, or the numbers in arithmetic, the pure space-figures in geometry. The theoretical consideration of these and similar givennesses leads to the formation of sciences that can come to higher theoretical completion, but a completion that does not satisfy the highest theoretical needs; a dimension of problems opens itself here whose consideration, from the naive standpoint at first held fast, sets knowledge into the greatest confusion and carries with it theories that, under prejudice-free critique, cancel themselves through countersense — whence, at consequent thinking, the inclination to skepticism.

He names the dimension and explains why the problems first showed themselves as problems of nature-knowledge. The dimension in question is the phenomenological-reason-critical; it concerns the puzzling essence-relations between being and consciousness — one can also say between being and the knowledge of being, since that is only apparently a restriction. Since natural man and his natural knowledge is natural above all in this, that he takes the being of psychophysical nature not merely as pre-given but is inclined to let only the objectivities comprised under this title count, at first to see them alone and, when he has seen others, to reinterpret them into nature-realities, it is understandable that the problems in question first became felt alone or predominantly as problems of nature-knowledge: how nature, being in itself, as something transcendent to the knowledge-consciousness, is yet to be knowable, how an objective validity, to which all natural science lays claim, is to become possible and explicable. But these problems are neither the only nor even the most radical. From the standpoint of nature-knowledge itself, the problems attaching to the *ideal* sciences are plainly more radical — the sciences that give natural-scientific knowledge its methodical principles and function in it as instruments of theorizing: the problems of geometric knowledge, of pure chronological and phoronomic knowledge, which Hume had treated in a psychologistic-skeptical sense and which Kant left in complete confusion in his transcendental-psychological doctrines; and again the problematic of pure arithmetical and, in general, formal-mathematical knowledge, and most of all that of the most general principle-knowledge, the analytic-logical. Everywhere the prevailing tendency of recent times went to raise the original naive naturalism to a fully conscious principle and to naturalize all the ideal objectivities that appear as objects of these disciplines — that is, to reinterpret the pure knowledge of these sciences into empirical knowledge; and this happens in the form of psychologism and biologism.

Husserl argues that these principle-attached problems are the most radical because a countersense at the principles infects everything normed by them — the same a fortiori argument as in Part A's refutation of logical psychologism. That the knowledge-problems which attach themselves to the principle-sciences — to the knowledge-possibility of the objectivities and principial laws researched in them, to the clarification of their genuine sense as it announces itself in their knowledge — are in fact of a more radical nature follows plainly from the fact that every countersense that is attached to these principles and their normative function by this or that theory concerns, a fortiori, also all the cognitions that owe their knowledge-value to the norming by these principles. This holds everywhere and is to be made especially evident with regard to the laws of analytic logic. If analytic-logical knowledge is so interpreted that its principial significance becomes a countersense, then eo ipso every knowledge whatever becomes a countersense. So, for example, it is clear that the psychologistic reinterpretation of the ideal objectivities of pure logic — the meanings, propositions, truths — and, hand in hand with it, the interpretation of the logical principles as psychological laws of thought, leads to wholly absurd and self-canceling consequences. Husserl refers the hearer to his *Logische Untersuchungen* for the detail, and reproduces the temptation: logical laws serve as norms of all thinking, they are laws prescribed to thinking, a psychic activity; laws of thought — what else should that be than psychological laws? Does not logic, traditionally regarded as the Kunstlehre of knowledge, function as a hygiene of the knowledge-life, a doctrine of "normal" thinking, as the ordinary hygiene is the Kunstlehre of healthy bodily life — so that, as the latter is a branch of physical, so logic would be a branch of psychological natural science? But if laws of thought are psychological laws, natural laws of human thinking, at best technical turns of such natural laws, then we come upon peculiar countersenses: as natural laws they would hang on the factual constitution of the world, and could therefore also be otherwise — indeed the possibility of another world with other laws of thought is conceded by the psychologists. But the sublation of the laws of thought means, as one easily sees, the sublation not merely of all factual laws but of all *possible* laws and all truths whatever, and correlatively the talk of being, of nature, and so on, loses every sense. Husserl declines to pursue these thought-series here; the theory of logical knowledge is not to be won as cheaply as psychologism thinks, and the merit of its countersense-interpretation of the sense of the pure logical principles consists only in making it acutely felt that here lies a dimension of problems that must be unanimously and clearly formulated and solved by the method appropriate to them — which is at any rate not the psychological one.

Husserl now states the constructive thesis: the epistemological problems are essentially attached to the principle-sciences, which therefore furnish the clues for the phenomenological work. The given indications make clear, at the same time, that the knowledge-problems are, by their essence, in a special way *attached to the principle-sciences* — so, in the line starting from the natural sciences, especially attached to the disciplines of pure natural science and, at a higher stage, to analytic logic. This shows already in that every step the particular sciences take measures its right against the general principles developed in those principle-sciences. In full generality, with regard to the total domain of possible sciences, the following consideration can be made: let us think all the apriori ontologies developed and linked by a universal category-doctrine that systematically carries out a division of all possible being-regions. We take together, then, the apriori philosophical disciplines of which we spoke earlier — only the *noetic* are to be lacking, for it is these that we are now first to construct, since in their union they make up precisely the phenomenological consciousness-doctrine and reason-doctrine of which we now seek to gain a conception. Of the ontologies we can say that they still belong to the lower science-stage, which still lets itself be built out in a systematic furtherance of the motives of natural thinking with its natural sciences. What would now be achieved with the perfect clarification of all these ontologies with respect to their knowledge-possibility? Evidently the absolute sense of all kinds of ideal objectivities and of all the general laws relating to them would be fully illumined: if the "possibility" of pure logical knowledge is clarified, that means we have full clarity about the absolute essence of meaning, proposition, truth, of all specifically logical ideal objectivities in generic generality; if the problems of general arithmetical knowledge are clarified, the general sense of cardinal number, ordinal number, and so on, is fully clarified.

But Husserl insists that clarifying the *ideal* objectivities does not yet clarify the knowledge of *particular, material* being — and this opens the program of regional phenomenologies of givenness. How, then, does it stand with the possibility of a particular, non-principial, non-formal, non-logical-ideal knowledge? To bring the idea "object" to essential clarity does not yet mean to bring the epistemological essence of a *thing* to givenness and to understand how knowledge of any arbitrary thing is "possible," how the consciousness-relation of the knowledge to a thing as such comes to essential clarity. It will therefore be a matter of studying, for each of the being-domains and each category belonging to it, the *givenness-consciousness* in which any particular being of such a category is to be "given," and of studying the actual knowledge-ways in which the mediate knowledge-progress is accomplished. It is a matter of gaining clarity, too, about how the principial knowledge must function as a necessarily valid norm for every objectively valid knowledge of a particular kind — how, in every respect, the sense of the objective validity of knowledge and the sense of objectivity itself, which in each category is to be bound to its particular laws, is to become understandable. So one sees that these inquiries too are oriented at the ontological principle-doctrines, at the category-doctrine, and at the individual disciplines belonging to the categories. The epistemological-critical problems do not play themselves out simply between consciousness and object, but between consciousness, real objectivity, and ideal objectivity; the problem everywhere is not only that the being is what it is as a possible object of knowledge and must receive its sense in the knowledge, but also that, in this, all being stands under ideas and under principles as norms.

Husserl closes the subsection by showing that this inquiry is bounded, not infinite, because each particular being falls under a finite number of categories with a finite number of principles — and so the regional ontology offers a clue and the regional phenomenology a correlate. That the inquiry cannot lose itself into the unlimited and splinter according to the individual particular objects, of this one soon convinces oneself at a right grasp of the problematic. Each particular being falls under a limited number of being-categories, being-regions, to which a limited number of principles belongs; and that gives the general inquiry its limits in one direction. On the other side, each particular being has its particular givenness-way, but as a being of the category it has its categorial givenness-kind, which is principially, that is, according to the general essence, the same for all particularities of the same category. So no epistemological inquiry of the knowledge relating to each pointable determinate thing is needed; rather, the idea of the thing as a spatial-temporal-substantial being, which is separated off in the category-doctrine and to which the principial laws of thing-ontology belong, offers a general clue of the inquiry. Correlative to the idea of the thing in general there belongs the idea of thing-givenness; the givenness-consciousness is apriori essentially alike for all particular things, and we call it "experience" in the pregnant sense. So a *thing-phenomenology* attaches itself to the thing-ontology, having to solve the problems of the knowing thing-consciousness, above all the problems of thing-givenness, that is, of the essence of thing-experience — and so for each of the regional ontologies; while at the same time, correlative to the formal mathesis preceding them all and overarching them all in formal generality, there corresponds a body of most general inquiries that relate to knowledge in general, to the givenness of something in general, to the formal categories of being in general and the possibility of their knowledge in general. If all these general epistemological inquiries are carried out in their orientation at the categories-doctrines and ontologies thought as already constituted, then thereby eo ipso every particular scientific knowledge of the lowest stage — the one lying below the principle-doctrines — is clarified, and thereby too the sense of every concrete being and every concrete being-kind which, through the unclarities about the essence of the correlation of knowledge and being, falls into confusion and, for example, makes one inclined to psychologize all being. The ontologies, in short, are the maps along which phenomenology charts the constitution of every region.

#### **(b) The Stages on the Way to Absolute Knowledge. The Necessity of the Critique of the False Theories of Knowledge on the One Hand and of the Construction of the Principle-Doctrines on the Other**

Husserl lays out the three stages by which knowledge ascends to the absolute, and the double task — critical and constructive — that the present situation of philosophy imposes. In the historical course of knowledge toward absolute, fully philosophical knowledge, two main stages are to be distinguished, a lower and an upper (the first stage falling again into two superposed stages that historically interweave). First the knowledge directs itself upon concrete *nature* — and we may add, upon the concrete human world and the concrete culture with their concrete pretended value-formations — and as the theoretical interest turns to this, natural science of the lowest stage arises. The higher stage consists in the *ontological principle-doctrines*. For the purposes of constituting concrete science there is need of reflective clarity about being- and knowledge-norms; the noetic reflections, as too difficult and confusing, lead as yet to no pure science, whereas the reflection upon the ideal meanings of assertions in their relation to the analytic-categorial being-forms — object, constitution, state of affairs — leads to the beginnings of the pure logic of meanings and objectivities in general; the look upon certain general categorial being-forms — number, order — leads to the pure arithmetic, combinatorics, and so on, that are closely connected with, indeed one with, pure logic; and the look upon the necessary constituents of real being, the consideration of the idea of the thing in general, leads to pure space-theory, pure time-theory, the beginnings of a pure thing-doctrine. The ideal in this respect would be that in this way all the ontological principle-doctrines should constitute themselves and undergo as relatively perfect a development as has proved possible, with regard to the formal and material mathematical disciplines, without any critique of knowledge whatever; de facto, of the ontologies only those unconditionally needed for the constitution of exact nature-knowledge have undergone such a development, and in really completed form only formal mathematics, geometry, and phoronomy.

Husserl introduces the third stage, the critique of knowledge proper, and explains why it is forced upward by the very development of the exact sciences. In the third stage — with which alone real knowledge in the absolute sense can arise, be it in an ever so small domain — the *critique of knowledge* constitutes itself. The inclinations to noetic reflections, awakened very early and at first leading to skeptical currents, cannot in the long run be suppressed; some of them even constantly accompany the course of knowledge — for example, one makes clear to oneself that the knowledge of nature ultimately grounds on experience, that it needs observation and experiment. But such reflections move within narrow limits and remain without principial and scientific development, while, as soon as one wants to go further and gain a general understanding of the essence of knowledge with regard to possible knowledge-objectivity, countersense-theories appear unavoidable. One pushes these general problems aside in the wide circles of the specialists, pursuing the fruitful problems of the lower stage that lead, in this stage, to fully convincing theories and practically useful successes; but in the long run the noetic problems cannot be pushed aside, precisely because the philosophical heads see that without them there is no talk of a real knowledge — and so, parallel with the progressing higher-formation of the exact sciences, the critique of knowledge, and the metaphysics that strives with its means for absolute being-knowledge, presses upward, wrestling for scientific constitution.

Husserl diagnoses how the false epistemologies, and the naturalism bred by natural science's success, damage the value-sciences in particular. The confused and countersense theories of knowledge, which at first grow up in ever-new shapes, not only promote anew skeptical conceptions — on the ground of a countersense metaphysics, an, as it were, unconscious one that denies the possibility of any metaphysics — but in a certain way also hamper the development in the lower stages. The grand development of the natural sciences as sciences of *mere* nature — of individual thinghoods considered and constituted in abstraction from all axiological determinacies — breeds the inclination to see only nature everywhere, that is, to know everywhere in the natural-scientific attitude and to reinterpret naturalistically what is not nature. In consequence, the *value-sciences* in particular suffer — for example the development of concrete culture-sciences as cognitions of value-formations active on a natural-scientific basis; and then, at a higher stage and, what especially concerns us, it does not come to the pure and systematic development of the axiological ontologies and, connected with that, to no development of a pure category-doctrine. The neglect of the value-disciplines is thus a direct consequence of the naturalism that the success of natural science fosters.

Husserl states the double task and casts the philosopher as both strategist and architect. What is needed in this situation of philosophy hardly needs saying. Had the series of pure ontologies developed itself completely, in natural fashion, beside and parallel with the series of concrete being-disciplines, then the theory of knowledge, as the general and discipline-oriented consciousness- and reason-doctrine, could have attached itself to the latter. But as things lie, there is need, on the one hand, of the *critique* of those false theories of knowledge, which bar to knowledge not only the path to the philosophical goal of absolute knowledge but also the path to the pure constitution of principle-doctrines, of category-doctrines and ontologies of every kind; and there is need, on the other hand, first of the actual *constitution* of the still-lacking ontologies as pure philosophical disciplines of the lower stage. In this respect the philosopher has, even with regard to formal logic, still himself to make a clean table and to perform the work of its pure and perfect constitution as a pure discipline, or to fulfill the preconditions for the technical execution of this pure discipline — insofar as the technical here, as it turns out, is in wide measure the affair of the mathematician. Since philosophy represents the idea of absolute knowledge, of absolute reason, the philosopher must therefore function, as it were, both as a *strategist* in the battle against unreason and, on the other side, as an *architect* who designs the systematic building-plan according to which, in the field of reason, the possible sciences appropriate to its own essence and demanded by it are to be established.

Husserl ranks the urgency of the constructive tasks and, with that, fixes the goal of the lectures. Of the very greatest importance here is the architectonic design of the system of the category-doctrine and the ontological disciplines, which is presupposed for the constitution of a fully comprehensive doctrine of knowledge and thereby of an ideal absolute being-doctrine and a concrete metaphysics. This is not, however, to say that a theory of reason would have to wait until all these ontological disciplines were finished; the historical course of science-development mirrors in a certain way the build of the reason-consciousness, and to the closedness of natural science there corresponds the relative independence of the nature-constituting consciousness, over against the higher and more complicated kinds of consciousness, the axiological and practical. Nothing stands in the way, then, of studying the thing-consciousness, and first the simple experience-consciousness, and seeing in what sense it is givenness-consciousness of the thing, with what sense the thing as experienced announces itself in it. Although a wide circle of inquiries opens with this, it does not yet lead to concluding metaphysical results, for the reason that a science says, on the ground of experience, *what* nature is; and what it means, in the most general way, that there is "a science," what it means, in the most general way, that "an assertion establishes a proposition, a truth, grounds truth through inferences and proofs" — that needs clarification. The analytic logic, soon widening itself into the formal mathesis, stands at the head of all the pure ontologies, and at the head of all knowledge-clarifications and efforts toward last, that is metaphysical, knowledge, even in a restricted existence-sphere; therefore the battle against psychologism in analytic logic has a quite unique significance for the enabling of philosophy, as does the philosophical striving to bring out systematically the idea of this logic, its essential tasks, its essential layers and stages, and then to help this discipline to actual execution. In the second place stands the critique of psychologism with regard to the material mathematical disciplines, which does not first have the function of enabling the pure discipline of geometry; more is already to be achieved for the apriori of proper thinghood, of the real that fills space and time, since here the pure discipline has only the meagrest beginnings to show, although attempts at its constitution were repeatedly undertaken at least in the ontology of the eighteenth century and in Kant as a pure natural science. But most of all is to be achieved in the axiological and practical region, for there everything whatever is lacking — the consciousness of the goals to be set for knowledge here, the first beginnings of scientific sketches, even the most general ideas for the lacking disciplines. To improve the situation here somewhat is precisely the goal of the further lectures; and with this it is indicated in what direction lie the fundamental problems of which Husserl had spoken in the announcement. The Einleitung thus arrives, by way of its critique of natural knowledge and its architectonic of the disciplines, at the task that the rest of the course — and Part A — carries out: the construction of a formal axiology and praktik.

### **§ 7\. The Theoretical-Objectivating Reason-Sphere as Analogical Clue for the Investigation of the Axiological and Practical Reason-Sphere**

Husserl states the method of the constructive part: to proceed as presuppositionlessly as possible while letting the relatively developed sphere of objectivating reason serve as the analogical clue for the value- and will-spheres. In our further proceeding — which is a matter of creating a free path for the pure disciplines that are to belong to the idea of the valuing, striving, acting reason — we want to proceed as presuppositionlessly as possible, but on the other hand to let ourselves be led by the analogy with the theoretical, the objectivating reason and with what lies before us, in relative further-development, in its sphere. It is unavoidable, in doing so, that we operate at first with very little-clarified concepts. Even the distinction of theoretical, valuing, and practical reason, to which we tie on, results for us in a mere general survey of the act-kinds with reference to which reason is spoken of. Husserl recalls the most general sense of the distinction for common understanding: as representatives of the theoretical or *objectivating* acts, examples suffice of acts in which any objects are meant to us as being — a perception, say, in which an objective existence is meant, a relating or connecting positing in which a constitution is grasped as belonging to an object, or the obtaining of a relation between two objects becomes conscious; the whole sphere of the acts that are called judgments and cognitions belongs in this circle. By contrast, there does not belong in it every liking turning to an object, every disliking being-repelled from it, the acts of joy and of sorrow and all that resemble them; objectivating acts may underlie these, but they are not themselves such — they are *valuing* acts, and they likewise stand under a reason-question, as shows itself in that we can ask after, and judge about, the right and wrong of a joy or a sorrow, an aesthetic or other liking or disliking.

Husserl marks the load-bearing distinction (the same that Part A's third Abschnitt guarded): the valuing act is not itself an objectivating act, even though an objectivating act can be built upon it. If we call the acts belonging here value-holdings, value-meanings, it is to be noted that these acts themselves must remain distinguished not only from the objectivations that go into them as substrates, but also from those that ground themselves upon them in such a way that now values, or value-predicates, become objectivities. In mere valuing the value is no object, it is no objectivating consciousness; but at any time an objectivating consciousness can establish itself that, on the ground of the valuing, sets the meant value as object — so when, on the ground of an aesthetic liking, we objectivatingly mean the object as beautiful and thereupon assert that it is a beautiful. And it stands similarly with the acts of longing, wishing, desiring, in which something posited and value-held as non-being, or as possibly, presumably non-being, is conscious as ought-to-be — as with the parallel negative acts. Here too we have to say that the desiring has the character of a meaning; one can ask after reason and unreason, right and wrong of the desiring or fleeing, and in the objectivations built upon acts of such groups something stands objectively as ought-to-be, as a good, and we say it is really good if the question of right finds a positive and well-grounded answer.

Husserl takes these distinctions provisionally and frankly flags the depth of the difficulties their true clarification would entail. These distinctions we take provisionally; their really deep-going clarification — the question how far, in what sense, with real right essential demarcations are to be made here, or in what sense one may here really speak of reason and objective validity — leads already into the phenomenology and theory of reason itself, and that is a true primeval forest of difficulties. For the present we avoid this forest and all the monsters lurking in it; corresponding to the factual situation and the historical development, we take the domain of objectivating reason as the better known, and let ourselves be led by the analogy of all reason-consciousness — so that what is highlighted and known in the domain of objectivating reason is to give us an analogical clue for the seeking-out of parallels in the other domains. The history of philosophy teaches, Husserl notes, that this analogy has been operative, whether spoken or unspoken, with almost all the great thinkers who have given their efforts to the reason-problems.

Husserl pauses on a historical complication that makes the analogy "iridescent and often misleading" — the entanglement of the theoretical with the axiological and practical, and the resulting ambiguity of "theoretical reason." With regard to the historical situation the following is to be stressed: to theoretical reason, as the domain of science, there sets itself over against, very early, practical reason as the domain of general — both individual and social — praxis. The valuing feeling, the desiring, the willing and doing are so intimately interwoven in the unity of praxis that they are at first taken, without division, in their mutual involvement; and only very late does aesthetic reason divide itself off over against the practical, where again it is not valuing in its full extent that sets itself over against striving and doing, but valuing restricting itself as aesthetic valuing. As concerns the theoretical reason-sphere, the involvement of the theoretical with the axiological and practical works confusingly. The field of theoretical reason in the widest sense is the field of every possible objectivation, and that is all-encompassing: values, goods, deeds, creations too are objects, and accordingly we have also sciences of values, of goods, of technical creations. This is to be clarified through the circumstance already touched, that every non-objectivating act can become the substrate of an objectivation that takes from it a value-object or an ought-to-be-object. If one now speaks of the field of theoretical understanding or of theoretical reason, one can on the one hand have in view the objectivating consciousness in general; but on the other hand the temptation lies near — since one sets objectivating consciousness and, in short, emotive consciousness over against each other — to restrict objectivation to the sphere where the emotive does not have to function as its substrate. Then the understanding-sphere, the sphere of theoretical reason, is the mere nature, the sphere of the value-free or extra-value being, the being that is also set over against the ought-to-be and is what it is even if all valuing were thought away, while on the other side stands the beautiful and good as such. Through this the concept of theoretical reason has taken on a very harmful ambiguity, and the analogies one lets oneself be led by become iridescent and often misleading. The consciousness of the all-encompassing generality of logic — which has established itself as an apriori discipline with regard to theoretical reason already with Aristotle — is by no means lacking; but on the other hand there is the recurring inclination to relate logic preferentially to the sphere of natural being. And this shows itself, too, in the way one carries out the parallelization of ethics and aesthetics, of practical and aesthetic reason, with the logical, the judging and knowing reason. Very furthering here was the inclination to naturalism, well comprehensible from other grounds — the one-sided preference of the natural existence, the inclination to trace all being back to nature, hence to reinterpret the correlation between objectivating consciousness, and specifically knowledge, and objectivity into the correlation between knowledge and nature, where then the knowledge itself was again interpreted as nature, namely in the form of psychological existence. The ambiguity of "theoretical reason," then, is itself a symptom of naturalism, and it corrupts the very analogy Husserl wishes to use.

Husserl draws the constructive demand: recognizing a pure formal logic as the correlate of objectivating reason, consistency requires seeking the *analogue* of formal logic in the value- and will-spheres. With all this it hangs together that already in the sphere of theoretical reason one did not want to recognize the division between formal, analytic logic and mathematics on the one side and the material logic, in particular the disciplines of the ontology of nature, on the other. But if one recognizes a pure formal logic and sees that it is the necessary correlate of the general idea of an objectivating reason, then consistency demands that in the parallel reason-spheres — insofar as one grants them an own right and seeks analogies in them — one seek precisely the analogue of formal logic. To the forms of objectivating consciousness, and more closely to the forms of the judging and knowing consciousness, there will surely correspond forms of the valuing and practical consciousness. As we must distinguish form and matter in all knowledge, so in all valuing, in all striving and doing; and as in the knowledge-sphere we can on the one hand bring the lawfulnesses of the form into relief and then ask what belongs to the matter, how far it has its kinds and lawfulnesses, so we must seek parallel formal lawfulnesses and put the analogous questions with regard to the valuing- and striving-matter. The most important postulate of philosophy, accordingly, is to answer the questions here posed — to realize the idea of a formal axiology and praktik, insofar as it has a right, and to answer the problems of the axiological matter. As the constitution of pure logic is a fundamental piece of theoretical philosophy and fundamental for the carrying-through of a noetics as a theory and critique of objectivating reason, so the constitution of a formal axiology is foundational for an axiological and practical philosophy and for the carrying-through of a phenomenology and critique of the emotive consciousness. The whole apparatus of Part A is thus here re-grounded as a demand of the program of a phenomenology of reason.

### **§ 8\. The Distinctions Underlying the Logical and Noetic Disciplines: between Act as Meaning and as Meaning-Content, between Proposition-Matter and Proposition-Quality, between the Primal Mode of Belief and Its Modalizations**

Husserl, having been told that some hearers found the phenomenological route too hard, chooses the easier historical route but first lays out the fundamental distinctions on which the whole parallel rests. In the last lecture, he recalls, he had started from a general, if at first provisional, survey of the various kinds of consciousness-acts that stand under the reason-question — the distinction of objectivating or theoretical, of valuing, and of longing and willing consciousness; the point being, from this side, to seek stimuli for the constitution of analogous ontic and noetic principle-doctrines in the domains of the non-objectivating kinds of reason, such as are constituted, in the sphere of objectivating reason, as formal logic and mathesis. If one already has, with regard to the objectivating sphere, an insight into the correlations between the act, the objectivity meant in the act and categorially grasped thus and so, and the objectivity simpliciter — if one has, in this sphere, already clarified the phenomenological relations in the main points and clearly formulated the problems — then most fruitful possibilities open themselves for the method of analogy with regard to the other act- and reason-spheres. Since he had occasion to hear that to a part of his hearers his presentations so far had been somewhat too difficult, Husserl will not now carry through the phenomenological way that starts from the consciousness-side, but will rather prefer first an easy series of considerations that takes its start from the historical situation and demonstrates, at a lower stage, the necessity of an analogue of analytic logic in the emotive sphere. As in the theoretical sphere one can convince oneself of the necessity of a pure and formal logic, and press to its objective constitution, without any deeper-penetrating consciousness-theoretical analyses, so one can do this also in the emotive sphere with regard to the parallel disciplines, drawing benefit from the considerations already carried through in the theoretical sphere and using them as a clue; and the idea of the new discipline once won, the next step would then be to the parallel noetic problems, for to the theory of objectivating reason there must run parallel one of the valuing and practical reason.

Husserl introduces the distinctions decisive for apophantic logic, beginning with belief and its modalizations. For the carrying-through of the parallel between pure and formal logic and formal praktik it is of special importance to bring into relief some fundamental distinctions that are already decisive for the right conception of the apophantic-formal logic — which for its part lets itself evidently be turned into a formal noetics of judging. Since these distinctions are by no means already common property and he touched them only fleetingly in the last lecture, Husserl devotes a small special consideration to them. *Judging is meaning, believing meaning*; we highlight the *Urmodus* (primal mode) of belief, namely certainty, or rather being-certain. Of it there are modifications, modalizations: belief can take the mode of being-uncertain, for example in the consciousness of the mere solicitation that it is so, instead of the certainty that it is so; a mode related to this is that of the soliciting-as-probable; doubting belongs here, and finally also the negation-consciousness, the consciousness of rejection. How these modifications are to be ordered — which, as one easily sees, by no means lie in a single series — is a question of its own.

Husserl develops the doctrine of the proposition (*Satz*) as the ideal content of the act, and its two senses. Considering, then, first the judging in the narrowest sense, that of believing-in-certainty or being-convinced: naturally and essentially every belief is belief *of something*; so too the predicative belief that we are accustomed to call "judging" and that fixes itself linguistically in the assertion. Every judging thus has its judgment-content; and this content is nothing other than what is called, in the logical sense, the proposition (*Satz*) or also the judgment — it is the asserted in the asserting, but under abstraction from the word-sounds. The word "judgment," accordingly, has a double sense: it is used now to designate the lived-experiences of judging, so especially by the psychologists, but also by the logicians where they consider the "judging-activity" in a normative intention; and now to designate the judged What, that is, the logical proposition. This What is constituted, consciousness-wise, in very different ways: one and the same proposition can be conscious clearly or unclearly, can be given in a distinct or indistinct setting-off of its members, where the members themselves can appear, as concerns clarity or intuitiveness, very differently. One and the same proposition, Husserl stresses: the proposition is a unity that can be conscious in very manifold "appearance-ways," as we may say in a figure. The same holds for all the judgment-modalities, and so we would have, in analogous fashion, to speak of propositions for them too: we would have to distinguish, besides the judgments or propositions in the narrower sense — more exactly, besides the *certainty*\-propositions — the solicitation-propositions, the possibility-propositions, the probability-propositions, the doubt-propositions, the question-propositions, the negation-propositions; everywhere we have to distinguish similar, and of course more closely to be researched, differences of intuitiveness and non-intuitiveness, of distinctness and indistinctness in the givenness. Over against all such givenness-modes — from the side of the acts of the various ways of experiencing and performing — the proposition is everywhere also to be designated the *identical meaning* (*Meinung*); whence the double sense of the word "Meinung," which says now as much as the meaning (the judging, surmising, questioning) and now as much as the meant-as-such.

Husserl extracts the two components of the proposition — matter and quality — and fixes the noetic/noematic terminology. Once one has made clear to oneself the over-against of act and act-meant, of proposition, in this series of judgment-modalities, it soon emerges that the proposition can vary in two directions, or that it has two components: the identical proposition-content (the *matter*) and the proposition-*quality*; more distinctly, the *sense* of the proposition and the proposition-quality, the thetic qualification. The certainty-proposition "an S in general is P" can be modalized; then we have, say, "an S in general is presumably P." The same that is once conscious as certain is another time conscious as probable, as possible. Two concepts of content thus appear here: on the one hand the proposition as the "content" of the act, the meaning as the correlate of the meaning; on the other hand, different propositions can have an identical content, an identical propositional sense, but a different qualification, which runs parallel to the modal differences of the meaning — believing-with-certainty, surmising, doubting. To meet better the double sense of act as meaning and of meaning as the meant-in-the-act as such, Husserl uses the expressions "noetic" and "noematic": the former relates to the act as the intending, the meaning of the pure I; the latter to the What, to the side of the correlate. The introduction of the *noesis/noema* terminology here, in 1911, is the same conceptual apparatus that *Ideen I* would systematize.

Husserl completes the apophantic doctrine with the distinction of proposition and state of affairs and the privileged position of certainty-truth. Propositions, with their senses and thetic qualities, stand under predicates of *validity*: a judgment, a certainty-proposition, is either true or false. From the judgment we distinguish the judged, the appraised objectivity; the judgment judges about subjects and refers predicates to them, refers this or that object to them, and to the whole judgment there corresponds an objectivity under the title *Sachverhalt* (state of affairs). If the judgment is true, the state of affairs is; if it is false, the state of affairs is not; but the ideal objectivity that we call the proposition is in both cases, and so is not to be confused with the states of affairs and the objects entering into them. What holds for the certainty-propositions holds also for their modalizations: they have predicates of validity and non-validity, and we could say that to each kind of propositions there corresponds a kind of truth and untruth. On the other side it is to be seen that the truth belonging to certainty takes a privileged position, that over against it all the other truth-forms prove to be modalizations, and finally that, apriori and equivalently, to each modalized truth there corresponds an unmodalized one with changed sense — this by virtue of the law that to each modalized proposition there belongs a certainty-proposition with modalized sense. For example, instead of surmising that S is P, I can perform a belief-certainty with changed sense, namely the certainty "it is presumably S-P"; the original surmise-proposition, the correlate of the surmise-consciousness, is then transformed into a certainty-proposition, into a judgment in the normal sense, into whose matter, into whose sense, the "presumably" has now entered. The whole apparatus — primal belief and its modalizations, matter and quality, proposition and state of affairs, the privilege of certainty — is thus laid out as the apophantic model whose analogue the formal praktik must build.

Husserl finally transposes the entire apparatus to the will and the emotive acts, and so closes the Einleitung's preparation. Passing now to willing and to the whole wide realm of the emotive acts: here wholly new qualities, new kinds of theses, appear over against the belief-theses and their modalizations. With regard to pure logic we had considered belief-theses in the predicative sphere; pre-predicatively such theses appear in the perception-sphere, the memory-sphere, in the sphere of the sensibility of the lower intellective consciousness, of what lies before the specific thinking and conceiving. But this lower consciousness is to be led over into a higher one, into the one that is the domain of the specifically thinking, knowing reason: we do not content ourselves with mere experiencing, and where it is a matter of bringing out objective validities, of knowledge and science, we lead the experience-consciousness over into a predicative one that has, so to speak, taken up into itself all the right and wrong of the lower consciousness and, in the case of its justification, also justifies the lower consciousness with it. So too we proceed in the sphere of the emotive acts with their new-kind emotive theses: we lead over "what" we value or will into predicative formulations, as when we say "A may be B," "A shall become B." Everything that we have now carried through in the belief-sphere holds also for the sphere of the emotive acts. Belief is meaning — meaning that something is, in certainty, surmising meaning, and so on — and the correlates were the certainty-propositions, surmise-propositions, and the rest, which we thought as predicatively formed and grasped. Willing too is meaning, wishing too is meaning, and correlatively the will-meaning, the wish-meaning corresponds to them; only the meaning here is not a being-meaning, a meaning that something is, but a meaning that something shall be. Husserl points to an ambiguity of the words "will" and "wish," the same ambiguity that is found in all act-spheres, even the sensible: "will" can mean the willing, the act, but also the willed as such, as when we say "that is my will"; and likewise "that is my wish" — the wish-act, the wishing meaning, has its What, that is, the wish as wish-meaning. With this transposition of the apophantic apparatus to the will, the 1911 Einleitung has set in place exactly the act/meaning, matter/quality, primal-thesis/modalization distinctions that the formal praktik of the Schlußstück, and of Part A's §§ 17–18, presuppose.

## **Schlußstück. The Formal Idea of Rational Choice**

### **(a) The Practical Realm and the Categorical Demand Related to It**

The Schlußstück is the 1911 formulation of the formal theory of rational choice that Part A presents, expanded, as §§ 18–21; it should be read alongside them, and the absorption-law, the practical realm, and the formal categorical imperative reappear here in compressed form, with a sharpened treatment of *realizability* and of the limit-cases. Husserl opens in the midst of the distinction between the objective and the noetic. The chooser holds, for example, something for a possible practical goal, for a practically realizable, which it in fact is not; objectively, then, this is to be excluded from the choice, and with regard to it he in truth does *not* have the choice, although he means to. Conversely, it can be that he objectively has the choice with regard to much that is realizable for him, but he does not notice it or does not draw it into account. The objective practical realm and the realm the subject takes himself to face can thus diverge.

Husserl flags a conflict in the talk of reason that the section will have to balance — the same objective/noetic doubling familiar from Part A. On the one hand it is objectively unreasonable to include in the choice what does not belong (so far as it is in truth not realizable) and not to include what does belong (so far as a realizable is, say, not recognized); on the other hand it is said that it would be unreasonable not to include in a choice anything held by the chooser for realizable, and to include what is not so held. We have, he notes, the same difference in the relation of objective logical demands and laws to the noetic ones: objectively, of two contradictory assertions one is true and one false, so to perform both is unreasonable; but on the other hand it is reasonable, when one is in the conviction that two assertions are contradictory and in the conviction that the one holds, to reject the contradictory other — although the conviction may be a false one. The objective law governs the matter; the noetic norm governs the rational subject acting on his convictions, even mistaken ones.

Husserl returns to the ethical field on the objective standpoint and exposes a tempting illusion — that the "must" precedes the law. It has here an objective sense to say that this and that stands in choice, that the chooser has the choice; but we often use such turns of phrase so that it could seem as if an objectivity of the *demand* preceded all law. We say someone is in the situation of having to decide choosingly, and the reason-question then seems to be merely *which* of the practical possibilities of his situation he should realize; in advance, it seems, lies the "must," in advance it is sure that a way of acting is thetically demanded, and not merely the relative rationality that, *under the presupposition that he acts*, this or that would be the better or worse. But what can determine the thesis in advance? Evidently it can itself only be determined on the ground of the practical possibilities and their relative values, and so on the ground of just such laws as we now try to bring out and bound. The categorical "must," that is, is not a datum prior to the value-laws but their result.

Husserl defines the *practical realm* (*praktischer Bereich*) as a complete and exclusive disjunction and derives the categorical character of the good in the one-good case. To gain clarity, we fix first that for a will-subject a circle of practical possibilities can be objectively determined, namely as a *complete and exclusive disjunction* of the practical possibilities existing for it at all; this we call the "practical realm," and it determines the disjunction within which the willer objectively has the choice. The proposition "he has the choice" has objectivity with reference to this complete — and always to be taken completely — disjunction. Fictioning a will-subject in whose practical realm only a single practical good belongs: it can will-realize it or, not willing, omit it. Objectively, then, this one good is not merely a hypothetical will-good but the *thetically or categorically* good. For if the subject is a willing subject, if it wills at all, be it in the form of positive willing or of omission, then it either wills the good — and then it wills the best it can will, the only good — or it wills otherwise, and then it wills the bad, as the lesser or the worthless; while the omission of any doing whatever is, as omission of the good, likewise bad. Whether the subject knows the good or not, whether it consciously subordinates it or merely does not do it and does something else or nothing instead — that is not in question for this value-consideration. The categorical character of the good is thus established purely objectively, independently of the subject's awareness.

Husserl generalizes to any disjunction of goods, evils, and Adiaphora, and states the conditions for a categorical demand.
