It stands the same, of course, if we take into the practical realm any disjunction of goods, of goods and evils, and so in general of axiological possibilities. A will is then always the objectively demanded, provided only that a value in the narrower sense occurs in the realm — always either a positing willing or an omission is demanded. Only if a will-realm were thinkable in which all practical possibilities were Adiaphora would nothing be demanded, omission as little as positing willing, all actions being in like manner indifferent. If only negative values occur in the will-realm, then the omission of the realization of everything is the demanded — which is still a will-demand. In any case, as soon as we find values in the narrower sense in the practical realm, a decision, a doing or omitting, is not only relatively better than every other, but *one* is thetically and not merely hypothetically demanded, one is the content of a categorical demand; and this hangs plainly on the relation between will and will-content (namely the value to be realized), and on the fact that the correlates of the realizing willing and of the omission stand under a law analogous to the law of contradiction and of excluded middle of logic, and finally on the fact that, according to the law stated at the outset of our consideration, in an exclusive practical disjunction the lower, over against the higher, "forfeits" its value, even if the lower is a positive good. Husserl closes the subsection with the very doubt that drives the rest: can one, in this objective consideration, come to a "categorical demand," a real "ought" — or has one already tacitly slipped in that the subject behaves not merely "rightly" but "rationally," that it makes rational deliberations about what is realizable, what could come into practical account, what is good and what the best in it?

### **(b) The Formal Laws of Rational Choice and the Formal Determination of the Highest Practical Good**

Husserl resumes the laws of choice and adds the key existential axiom linking collective omission and individual doing. Where compatibility for doing exists — where several can be done collectively — the combination of the summation-laws with the law of value-absorption yields the known laws, that within the choice a sum of goods deserves the preference and the single goods vanish beside it; while for omission it follows that to omit all the goods standing in choice together is, considered in and for itself, unconditionally bad. He then draws into account the axiom: *if the collective omission of a plurality — or, as we may also say, the omission of an allness — is bad, then the doing of at least one of this allness is good, and conversely*. From this it follows that, where several goods are in choice, surely at least one of them is a practically to-be-willed, the *bouletic* good; and this becomes important for the case of an exclusive choice between several equivalent but mutually exclusive goods, for if no practical preference for a determinate one of them is present, the law now says that the demand nonetheless exists to will, or do, some one of them, no matter which. So the law already stated earlier receives a deeper grounding. If only evils are drawn into comparison within a choice, the omission of each is good, hence the collective omission of all is good — and properly an axiom is contained in this too, for collective omission and the single omission directed at each single one are two different things. Such axioms make up, so to speak, the *formal logic of the will*.

Husserl marks what these axioms achieve and introduces the *summum bonum formaliter spectatum*. With all this, he says, he has not merely repeated the old axioms and enriched them by the addition of some important new ones, but has very essentially cleaned and improved them. In the axioms nothing is yet said of the highest practical good, but they are the foundation for the determination of the highest practical good, namely in the *formal* sense. It is the remarkable thing about formal praxis that it reaches its conclusion in a *summum bonum formaliter spectatum*, to which, as to a formal norm, the respective *summum bonum materialiter spectatum* — that is, the material highest goal of action — must necessarily conform. In the last regard the question is after the materially fully determinate, and indeed categorically, demanded in each given case of action; whether a common material principle, or several such, can be given for all agents and all cases of action is a question that would then still have to be raised, and it is usually this that is aimed at in the question of the summum bonum. But before all searching for a summum bonum it must stand fast that such a thing can and must exist at all; and closely connected with this is that necessarily a *formal* categorical imperative can be formulated. The formal discipline thus secures the *form* of a highest good and a categorical imperative; the material content is left to a further inquiry.

Husserl works out the relation between knowing the categorical demand and the formal principle. If we already knew that in every possible case of action — more exactly, in every case where a practical decision can be made — some determinate, if still unknown, decision should be made, that among the possible decisions, the doing or the omitting ones, there is one and only one that is the categorically demanded, then a formal principle would really already lie in the proposition: *do the categorically demanded in every case of possible action\!* But whence do we know anything of a categorical demand? When does such a one exist, or does it exist everywhere? Does it exist in restricted or in unconditioned generality? These questions, Husserl says, decide themselves on the ground of formal ethics. First it is to be said that the practical imperatives of reason, and in particular the talk of a categorically practical demand, refer to cases of *choice*. This is no restriction, insofar as every case of possible willing and acting can objectively be regarded as a case of choice, and insofar as apriori every question "What shall I do?" has relation to a field of practical possibilities in or to be brought into consideration — possibilities between which the choice obtains. De facto we often will without choosing; but if, after the willing, we deliberate how we should have willed or done, we deliberate subsequently how far we had a choice, what could have been chosen beside what was factually done — we *construct* the practical realm belonging to the case of action, the realm of practical possibilities belonging at all to that case, and perform subsequently a choice and, where possible, a rational insight. We judge the factual action by whether it could have been choosable in this rational choice, and that gives it itself, if not in every sense, then in a determinate sense, a rationality: it receives the character of the objectively demanded. It is not, to be sure, the action subjectively recognized as right by the willer in the moment of the deed, but for that reason it is nonetheless the objectively right action — exactly as we often pass a judgment uninsightfully and, examining it subsequently, ground it as objectively true in a grounding. That the judgment is true and that it is groundable in an insightful grounding belong essentially and correlatively together; and so here too the voluntaristic truth, so to speak, normally finds its grounding in a *choice*, which therefore belongs essentially to the form of such grounding.

Husserl establishes that every will can be ordered into a choice, and that the grounding choice must show the willed as the absolutely best. That every will — taken of course not as the factual psychic act but ideally, as essence — can be regarded as the decision of a choice, ordered into a possible choosing decision, is apriori clear; for at least the omission always stands over against the positive willing of an A, so that even where nothing else beside A is in question, the positive willing of A can be grasped as a decision in the choice between doing A and omitting A. A will is "more valid," it is so to speak *bouletically truer*, if a rational choice is apriori possible that grounds it as valid, as objectively demanded. And what kind of rational choice is this? It must be so constituted, we can say, that it grounds the relevant will — or rather the willed as such — not merely as a relatively best but as a good and, in that, as the *absolutely best*. Turning to the objective correlate of this insightful grounding, Husserl says: to the possible insightful grounding there corresponds the objective of the grounding, which has its form and the demands of the form; and here this says that the will must have various objective properties that are purely formal properties. First, in order that it can be at all a valid, a positively valuable will, it must go upon a *realizable*; that is the first formal property of the practical summum bonum — or, since at first no summum is in question, of the practically good in the absolute or categorical sense. And second, apriori there obtains the possibility that none, or one, or several practical realizabilities exist. The condition of realizability is thus made the first formal mark of the practically good, and the three cases (none, one, several) organize the rest of the analysis.

Husserl works through the limit-case of *no* realizability, showing that it makes the will a "dead faculty" and the very notion of a practically good senseless. If the first case were thinkable — that nothing is realizable — there would be no categorical imperative, no practically good; the will would, subjectively spoken, be a dead faculty, namely for every such supposed case. One could say neither that doing nor that omitting is demanded, but precisely that no demand exists. One might indeed object that a willing can also go upon a non-realizable, but if it does so it is unreasonable, so that in this case omission would be the demanded; but omission too can go upon a non-realizable, and would do so if this demand were valid, while the omission of a non-realizable is likewise unreasonable, since rational omission too presupposes realizability — so that the demand, in the supposed case of a lack of realizabilities, would in general be an unreasonable one, namely this demand to exercise omission. There seems then to remain only to say that the only good here is *not to will* in the literal sense, that is, in the sense of privation; but does it make sense to say that the not-to-be-willed is a *good*, namely a practically good, of which here precisely the talk is? A practically good is something rationally to be done or to be omitted; but here, in our limit-case, both are unreasonable and worthless. Husserl notes in passing that the axiom "if the doing of A is good, then the omission of A is bad, and conversely" already presupposes a practical sphere of realizability — that A is at all a realizable; first it is presupposed that the willing of A, positive as well as negative, has at all a "rational sense," and only within this sphere of rational practical possibility does the axiom hold.

Husserl then treats the asymmetry between realizable disvalues and non-realizabilities — the latter being "absolute zeros." If non-realizabilities are nonetheless drawn into a choice — and the field of the non-realizabilities is infinite — then, if a realizable also belongs in the choice-realm, everything turns out, for its result, as if the non-realizable were not reckoned at all. For a will directed upon a non-realizable is radically and absolutely unreasonable, and that means unreasonable in yet another sense, in yet another potency, than, say, a will directed upon a realizable bad — for such a will could eventually become a right one when further motives are drawn into the choice, in case, say, overridingly good consequences attach to the realization of the bad, whereas for the non-realizable this is excluded and senseless. In the connection of a choice the realizable disvalues are eventually only *relative* zeros, but the non-realizabilities are *absolute* zeros: they cannot take on value by way of value-derivation, and the propositions relating to this too must be formulated in a formal praktik. So it stands when we consider the non-realizable beside the realizable.

Husserl examines the further limit-case — that *nothing* realizable exists — and finds it a voluntaristic nonsense, then states the central result via absorption. But if we admitted as a limit-case that there were at all nothing realizable, then there would be no practically good whatever. This limit-case, however, is only logically constructed; voluntaristically it is, one may say, a *nonsens*: if a will is at all possible, then a sphere of practical realizability too is there — I can always do something, can at least direct the gaze there or thither, the outer gaze or that of attention. (With this, Husserl concedes, we overstep the purely formal sphere, which knows nothing of the material involvement of the will.) If, then, further, there are in the will-realm one or several realizabilities, it comes, in order to determine the objectively and absolutely good, to staking out precisely the *total*\-realm in which the willer has the choice, as he set out last time. Thinking the totality of the realizabilities existing at all for the will-subject objectively determined, there results, by the law of the value-absorption of the lower-values by the higher-values and by the appertaining other laws, that a good is there that objectively has the absolute preference, indeed that has the only absolute practical value — namely the one that absorbs the practical value of all the rest and is itself not absorbed. The highest good in this realm — be it a single good or a collection, which has to function practically, by the summation-laws, as a summatorily unified good — is the positively to-be-done, the absolutely practically good; and it here deserves the name *summum bonum*, insofar as it is the best among the goods and, beside them, the practical values in general.

Husserl marks the residual indeterminacy that the purely formal discipline cannot resolve, and adds the no-positive-good and infinity provisos. In the purely formal sphere, to be sure, it cannot be decided whether the case is not thinkable that into a practical total-realm nothing positively good might ever fall, in which case the demand would be to do nothing, or rather to omit everything — which then yields a reflexively good, the good will directed upon omission. Since the formal praktik draws nothing of the matter of praxis into consideration, it is here helpless; it can only say that if there is at all a will-realm for a will, and this is thought in objective completeness, and if then, further, positive goods are found in this realm, then the best of this realm is the only absolutely good, the only objectively demanded. But yet another presupposition is to be added: that the realm is not an infinite one, with infinitely many goods so constituted that to each one a greater is still constructible in infinitum — as, for example, in the case of the will of an infinite being that can not merely do infinitely much, but so that the infinite goods it can do, singly or collectively, have no good above them able to absorb them by its objectively overriding value. The formal idea of the highest good thus presupposes a *finite* (or at least non-cofinal) practical realm — a presupposition that, with the theological constructions of the Einleitung's § 3 in view, is anything but idle.

Husserl gives the popular formula and insists on the depth behind it, with a pointed correction of Brentano. Popularly we could designate the result thus: the formal praktik leads to a highest formal principle, which grounds itself above all on the principle "the better is the enemy of the good." It says: *Do the best among the attainable\!* — that, to be sure, is a noetic expression; objectively the expression would be: the best among the attainable in the whole practical sphere is not merely comparatively the best but the only practically good. In a few sentences, after setting up his summation-laws, Brentano comes to his principle "Do the best among the attainable\!"; but our considerations show that this can only be a provisional, popular expression, and that very careful and deep-penetrating considerations are needed in order to lay out systematically the sense, the limits, and the formal presuppositions of the principle that solicits so self-evidently in this expression. To achieve that appears to Husserl a true fundamental piece of all pure ethics, and so the first and basic piece of a systematic research of the essence of practical reason, and more closely of the *formal* practical reason. Against Brentano, then, the point is not the slogan but the rigorous formal theory — practical realm, absorption, realizability, the limit-cases — that alone gives it sense and bounds.

Husserl closes the Schlußstück with three supplements needed to unfold the principle fully. For the perfect unfolding of the sense of the highest formal principle — to which noetically the formal categorical imperative corresponds — and accordingly for its normative application, some points are still to be attended to. *First*, already come to validity but again and again to be sharply stressed: within the realm of practical possibilities every practical *collective* of such possibilities — that is, every concept of practical possibilities to be realized together in the unity of a collective willing — must count as an own member of the realm, and so comes essentially into account for the ideal choice-consideration, and that in consequence of the summation-laws. In connection with this point Husserl now gives a certain justice to the idea of the *complete disjunction*, which at the absorption-laws he had excluded again as an unnecessary restriction: the idea of the practical good as the categorically demanded refers to the idea of the practical realm that comprises all practical possibilities in itself, and this practical total-realm we can now regard as a complete disjunction such that the disjunction-members practically exclude one another. For the concept of the practical possibilities, the peculiar *voluntaristic form* is to be heeded: it is not a matter of the goal alone, so far as it is thought as realizable, and also not of the goal thought in the determinate attaining, in the determinate action. As Husserl has repeatedly pointed out, analogously as in the judgment-sphere we have logical forms as meaning-forms through which the thing is meant, so similarly in the will-sphere voluntaristic forms; one understands at once what he means by designating such forms here for the purpose of the disjunction in question — for example, I will not merely A in general, but I will *A alone*, that is, I will A and omit everything else; and beside this it is another case if I will *(A and B) alone*, this will being, in comparison with the previous, *not* exclusive, although on both sides A is willed. One can then think the whole practical realm, which contains all the realizable, articulated into an exclusive voluntaristic disjunction, of the form: A alone (as willed), B alone, A and B together (so far as A and B are compatible together), and so on. It is apriori clear that such an exclusive voluntaristic disjunction must be constructible and that it belongs to the essence of the choice that we ideally relate to the whole realm; one can then, according to the given laws, as the next methodical step pose the value-question and strike out all Adiaphora, and further, in the interest of the practical good, strike out all the negative-valued members of the complete disjunction, and then the same members so far as they appear within the collections. Theoretically considered, the complete voluntaristic disjunction underlying the choice must in this way reduce to one between only positive-valued members, only "goods," whose best then represents the practical good, the *prakton agathon* in the specific sense, as the one good of the rational decision of the choice.

*Second*, the practical value depends on the *being-value*, the mere good-value lying before the will-domain; the practical good is a *founded* one. The being-value can be a value in itself or in itself an Adiaphoron, and again it can be a derived value — a value that derives itself, say, from valuable being-consequences, which for their part can again be in themselves valuable or derivedly valuable, and so on. We know from earlier laws that derivation-values and own-values sum algebraically, that value-increases or value-decreases can arise here, and that these then determine total-values for the substrates of the practical values and thereby these values themselves. So one must take into account the lawfulness — which, if not as a primitive axiom then as a theorem, must be stated in the formal praktik — that the relative value of every possible will-goal is determined not by the own-value of the goal but by all the values accruing to it from valid transfer, hence accruing to it also with regard to all the objectively necessary consequences in the connection of reality. *Third*, connected with this, it is to be heeded that for the value of a will-goal there comes into account the value of the *way* to it; in the ways too there ground value-differences, and these too can, the identity of the will-goal presupposed, determine differences of practical values. Properly, then, in the objectively explicated realm of practical possibilities, on the side of the positive will-possibilities, goals and ways must be distinguished, and every possible action that, with the same goal, chooses different ways must be reckoned as a practical possibility for itself. Here is an own field of formal considerations and of formal laws relating to them: ways can be unmediated, simple ways, or mediated, the latter being ways that terminate in self-setting-off mediating goals and lead, through their realization, to the end-goal; accordingly actions are distinguished into simple and mediated actions, the latter having a determinate form of composition out of mediating actions, whose goals are called *means* and the goal of the whole action the *end-purpose*; and by a complete system of means is understood the total concept of means belonging to the unity of a possible determinate action. Generally the law holds that, with the same end-purpose or will-goal, the "most convenient" way is the best, or that the demand exists to reach the goal on the "best" way; and therein lies, too, that between better and worse means is to be distinguished and that the demand exists to prefer the best means, to replace worse by better as far as possible. These noetic demands are correlates of the value-estimation of the ways and means, which for their part will again have advantages according to the ways leading to them, or conversely disadvantages. In itself every way has its actual or hypothetical value, it is a practical good insofar as it leads to the good, or hypothetically presupposed-good, goal; but the value-absorption of course makes itself felt here too, so that beside the also-possible better way, beside the better means, the less good loses its value, and the overall best — so far as value-differences between the ways obtain — remains as the only categorically demanded. Wherein the eventual value-advantages of the means and ways in general consist, formal ethics does not concern itself, and probably not even the value-difference — which, Husserl notes, hangs on tacit presuppositions to be more closely analyzed — that consists in the way leading fastest to the goal being in and for itself the best, a difference that is itself only a consequence of the fact that the earlier practical realizing of a good has its advantage, and that under the tacit presupposition that, by the summation-law and the time-law, the sum of the being-good widens and is temporally extended. Husserl ends the Schlußstück by stressing the ineliminable role of time: time plays from the outset an essential role in the will-sphere and that of the practical goods, since we stand throughout in the sphere of reality; the willing subject acts, hence realizes, and time plays for the subject's practical sphere the determinate role that always a determinate time-point of action — if, in the laws, in indeterminate generality — is presupposed, the sphere of the subject's practical possibilities being co-defined by the time-stretch running unbounded into the future from the time-point of action. The formal praktik thus closes, as in Part A, on the threshold of the material and temporal conditions it deliberately abstracts from.

### **Beilage III (to § 2): The Biological Significance of Reason**

This short research-note supplies what § 2 set aside under the title of the "biological significance of reason" — and it is important to read it as a careful *non*\-reduction: Husserl is asking how rational rightness can have biological value without thereby being explained biologically. Not considered in § 2, he says, is the biological significance of reason, the biological significance of right, possibly insightful, judging, valuing, willing. Here the will and the action come in the first line into account, and serving them the right valuing and the right judging — about things, values, possible actions. Here knowledge, in its rightness and possibly insightfulness, appears as "on the average" furthering self-preservation, vouching for the relative durability of existence, or "useful" for it, for individuals and for the species; on the height of the perfection of knowledge depends the perfection of the preservation, and so the "adaptation of thoughts to facts" has a biological significance. The question is: what are the determining viewpoints of the consideration?

Husserl locates the problem in the *double* nexus in which the rational act stands — the psychophysical and the intentional. The psychic lived-experience of willing, with the underlying acts of valuing and judging and presenting, is not merely present in the connection of the soul-life, the consciousness, of the relevant individual, and does not merely have its psychic connections in this; it also has an "efficacy" in nature. Now every psychic may have psychophysical connections — connections with facts of material nature, and thereby, mediately, with the lived-experiences of other living beings — but we have here a *double* connection: the connections that are called psychophysical, and the connections between the acts and the objects meant in them; and if the meaning is "right," then these objects "are" objectively, or they become, as deeds, objective in the meant objective time and world. Here lie the greatest problems: the psychic and nature, psychology and the human sciences. But clear at any rate is this: if in the individual consciousness — which itself belongs to a communicative connection — a world objectively constitutes itself, then this relation must obtain, that rational willing must have significance for the course of "reality." There must, then, be these two kinds of connection: on the one hand the connections of intentional lived-experiences in the consciousness-flow, on the other the fitting-together of rational intentional connections with the course of reality. Mere being-questions and reason-questions interweave in all the human sciences, for a world, a piece of the world, a realm in which rational beings live who let themselves be led by reason-motives and so act rationally, looks of course different than a world in which such beings are lacking. The biological efficacy of reason is thus parasitic on the intentional correctness it cannot itself explain — which is why the question is a problem of the relation of the psychic and nature, not a license for biologism.

### **Beilage IV (to § 3): God as Idea, Philosophy as Correlate of the God-Idea. Teleological Problems**

This note expands § 3's theological culmination, and again the controlling fact is that "God" figures throughout as an *idea*. God as idea, as the idea of the most perfect being; as the idea of the most perfect life in which the most perfect "world" constitutes itself, a life that develops creatively out of itself the most perfect spirit-world in relation to a most perfect nature. Philosophy, correspondingly, as idea, as the correlate of the God-idea, as the absolute science of absolute being, as the science of the pure idea of divinity and as the science of the absolutely existing being. Measured against this idea, it is to be weighed whether and how far the absolute being can be regarded and known as an existing God or as the self-development of the God-idea in existence; and there belongs here, too, the consideration whether an absolute being at all, and in idea, must necessarily bring to unfolding within itself ideals that order themselves into the God-idea, and conversely whether an existing God can be only in the form of a development that, in its developmental course, brings the absolute ideals to ever more perfect unfolding. Husserl poses the conditions of the possibility of the being — the realized being — of the absolute God-idea: whether this can be an absolute point, an absolute goal in the sense of a resting being or of a flux persisting in ever-same-staying formations, or whether to the essence of the existing divinity it belongs that it is a development of value-stages, such that an absolutely last value-stage is not thinkable at all, but rather the highest value that is thinkable is so only in the *progressus* in such a development. But would we not then come, he asks, to the Aristotelian metaphysics — to the *poioun* as the ensouling purpose-idea of the whole world-development, which would at the same time have to be grasped as a Platonic idea? Yet not so, insofar as no ideal goal would properly be set, after the schema of the completed tree as the end-limit of the development.

Husserl names the deepest difficulty and the residue of "irrational" matter. The greatest difficulty is the understanding of the *original teleology* that belongs to the absolute consciousness. Causality is a constituting reality-form within the constituted being, and so belongs at once, under the aspect of consciousness-constitution, in the teleology; but does the idea, the ideal form, regulate the *matter* of consciousness — does it regulate, in other words, the stuff that determines the content of being and of the being-forms? Here we strike again the riddle of the Platonic and Aristotelian matter, the "irrational" *hyle* (which, Husserl notes in a footnote, in Aristotle does not mean the same as for us). He closes by ordering the disciplines: the sciences of what the idea of the most perfect being presupposes are the formal and material ontologies; grounded on them, the apriori construction of the idea of the most perfect world or reality — under the title of the ontologies comprising also the axiological and practical disciplines. And so philosophy is, first, the science of the idea of the most perfect being and the most perfect knowledge, a philosophical idea-science; and second, the philosophical fact-science, existence-science, philosophy as metaphysics. The theological problems are thus left standing as problems — the regulation of matter by idea, the possibility of a developmental rather than resting divinity — within an explicitly twofold (apriori idea-science / metaphysics) framework.

### **Beilage V (to § 3): Philosophy as the Science of the Mutually Interpenetrating Reason-Ideals**

This note carries out the "other thought-series" promised in § 4(c): that philosophy represents not the idea of absolute knowledge alone but the idea of perfect consciousness, of perfect personality, in general. Of considerable interest, Husserl says, would be the carrying-through of a thought-series in which it would turn out that philosophy represents not only the idea of absolute knowledge but, by virtue of the essence-relations that link all the basic kinds of consciousness-functions with one another, the idea of perfect I- and reason-life, or, more simply, the idea of perfect consciousness in general. (He at once corrects himself in a footnote: not "consciousness" but the *achieving* and the *achievement*, the abiding acquisition of knowledge-truth — the abiding habitus of the personal I that unfolds itself as free and thereby develops itself — the same self-criticism, focused on the I and its habituality, that the long footnote to § 3 had pressed.) The reason-ideal of a perfect will-life includes, by virtue of the relation of willing and valuing, the ideal of perfect valuing, and no less a perfection of knowledge — knowledge that directs itself in every direction upon being, value, and good, upon nature, upon the fields of actual and possible values, which are thereby fields for actual and possible goods as creative will-formations; and one could equally show that the reason-ideal of perfect consciousness with regard to valuing includes in itself perfection of willing and knowing, so that in general, in the total-ideal, the ideals of the different kinds of reason mutually demand and interpenetrate one another.

Husserl sketches the route from the highest practical question to the whole system of value-disciplines, and on to a practical ideal science. The considerations naturally take their start from the factual willing, valuing, knowing of man, who is a member of the spirit-world related to the given nature; and they take their start, say, from the highest practical question to be posed, with reference to the given world and spirit-world, for the individual spirit, for the individual man: *What shall I do; what is, for me* — the "for me," Husserl notes in a footnote, being ambiguous, with the danger of egoism — *or what is, in a generally circumscribable way, for man the practically best, the highest practical good?* That leads back to the question of the good in the wider sense, of valuable being in general, how it is to be determined according to its value-gradations, under what value-laws it stands. Generalized, the questions can be raised to the highest ideality if, not in restriction to man and the factual nature but with reference to subjects, monads in general, spirit-life in general, one poses the questions of the most perfect and then absolutely perfect willing, valuing, and so on; and it is then easy to see that pure philosophy answers all these questions, and conversely that all it researches must be researched for the answering of these questions. That a perfect life under the given factual existence-relations, related not merely to the individual man but to the human community — linked to the unity of a conscious mutual relation — demands, on the side of perfect knowledge, metaphysics, is self-evident; but further would be demanded a scientific consideration of the possible ideal formations of human *personalities* and the ideal *forms of the life-community* of humanity, of those possible in the given nature, and of the ideal ways in which the factual nature, under preservation of the psychophysical lawfulnesses, could contribute to the raising of the value of human consciousness-life; and finally would be demanded the *practical ideal science*, built on pure philosophy, on metaphysics, and on the ideal consideration of individual and social spiritual being and of a beautiful and good formation of nature — the science of the highest practical ideals with regard to the factual nature and spirit-world: which practical goals of individual human formation, of the formation of sociality, of the transformation of nature can and must be set, and which ways of realization chosen, so that the given life, in progressing perfection, could become conformable to the highest practical ideals realizable within the frame of the given reality. With this, new disciplines open: individual ethics, social ethics, the aesthetics of the various arts and of nature, the practical aesthetic Kunstlehren and nature-doctrines, pedagogy, politics.

Husserl shows the mutual inclusion of the three reason-ideals and concludes that their research is philosophy itself. The most perfect life with regard to valuing will be not only that which, when it values, performs right valuing, but also that which is in possession of the highest values, that therefore also *finds* highest values in its valuing, and so, with regard to willing, the highest life-forms of willing; so the perfect life in valuing spans also the perfect willing, and the value-ideal and the will-ideal interpenetrate, the practical life-ideal belonging in the frame of the axiological life-ideal, while conversely the practical life-ideal is determined by the axiological, since the practically best must be an axiologically valuable and most valuable. And, considering the ideal of knowledge: the ideal of knowledge, the highest ideal of spirit-life in the direction of knowledge, belongs into the content and frame of the highest axiological ideal; while conversely perfection of valuing presupposes perfection of knowledge, since knowledge-acts belong to the reason-presuppositions and possibly to the contents of valuing; and of course it belongs too into the frame of the highest practical ideal. In general, then, we see that the different kinds of reason interpenetrate and constitute a single reason with essential sides. And reason is also related to a *content*: to the highest knowledge-ideal there belongs not only validity of knowledge but a certain constitution of the known — a knowledge-life is only perfect if it stands in relation to a "world" that comes to meet the highest knowledge-intentions in a fitting way, that has certain virtues of knowability — just as, axiologically, the highest axiological life-ideal of the axiological world prescribes rules to the valuable being, for a world can be better and worse in view of axiological ideals: the value-life will be right and good if rightly valued, but, rightness presupposed, it will be the more perfect the more the world itself gives ground and occasion for high and highest value-positings, and likewise in the practical respect. The research of these ideals — what it implies, and into what science it belongs — is, Husserl concludes, philosophy itself: to the striving for a rational life, philosophy is coordinated as the corresponding science of the ideas and ideals belonging to the idea of reason and of rational life-existence.

### **Beilage VI (to §§ 3 and 4b): The Idea of Absolute Knowledge: Pure Philosophy as Doctrine of Ideas and Metaphysics as Absolute Science of Factual Reality**

This note re-derives, compactly, the cardinal division between pure philosophy and metaphysics from the two senses of the idea of absolute knowledge. We started, in conceiving the idea of philosophy, from the idea of absolute knowledge — we followed the ideal that holds sway, as a tendency, in all knowledge-striving; and it is grounded in the essence of this ideal that, for its systematic determination, it leads to the two groups of disciplines that we have separated as pure philosophy and metaphysics. First, the ideal of absolute knowledge can from the outset be understood as the conception of a *pure idea*, namely the idea of a most perfect knowledge in general, apart from all facticity; then we come, as a light consideration teaches, to the system of the pure, the apriori philosophical disciplines (which, Husserl notes in a footnote, are *formal* disciplines). Second, the ideal of absolute knowledge can also be understood as the idea of the most perfect knowledge *in relation to the fact of this world*. This need not mean that we already presuppose that a nature is, in the sense of the manifold natural-scientific cognitions we learned at school, as if we anticipated a stock of sciences whose development is precisely the first goal of absolute knowledge; rather, we take in the fact of determinate experiences, determinate perceptions, memories, and the like, so that experience-cognitions are given as a starting-point, on whose ground a sphere of factual existence lets itself be determined in progressing measure — in the advance of science, a nature with such and such natural laws. The ideal of absolute knowledge, with regard to the existence to be scientifically worked out from the factual experience, then leads to *metaphysics*, and of course demands from itself the scientific research of the pure ideal of absolute knowledge, that is, the constitution of the discipline of pure philosophy.

Husserl articulates the three strata of the science of factual reality. Absolute science of factual reality, taken in full extent, would be understood, first, only as absolute science of physical and psychic nature — the science that gives the last theoretical completion to the disciplines factually grown in the course of humanity's development (of physical nature, of the soul-life of humans and animals, of social-historical reality, of the manifold formations of human culture), namely by recourse to the last knowledge-sources and knowledge-norms of pure philosophy; but the total-unity of existence, the word taken in the sense that excludes all regard to possible value-predications, does not span the field of all the sciences. Second, new scientific domains, though related to existence-reality, grow up when the givennesses of existence are considered under the viewpoints of axiological and practical reason — and humanity has in fact always pursued value-sciences, aesthetics, ethics, pedagogy, politics, valuing culture-sciences of every kind — and with regard to all these sciences too, which are occupied not with the merely actually being but with values and practical goods, the goal of the last grounding, clarification, insight-making, in short theoretical completion, is to be set, the pure philosophical disciplines belonging to the pure ideas valuing and value, striving and practical good offering here the norming principles and theories. Third, to factual reality there belongs a highest philosophical science — the empirical teleology and theology of which Husserl spoke more fully earlier — the last being the perfecting, possibly the new-formation or new-grounding, of the naturally grown teleologies and theologies, to be accomplished by recourse to pure theology and the other pure disciplines; and as the highest reality-science, related to the unity of the total reality considered under all reason-viewpoints, it presupposes all the previously named reality-sciences and their philosophical completion. The designation "metaphysics" for all these reality-sciences is, Husserl grants, not quite fitting, but we have unfortunately no other name at our disposal except the name "philosophy"; the main thing is the *cardinal division* between pure philosophy — we could also say pure doctrine of ideas — and reality-philosophy as the absolute science of reality in the fully comprehensive sense.

### **Beilage VII (to the Schlußstück): Analysis of a Choice between Two Possibilities**

This is the fullest of the Teil B Beilagen, a phenomenological analysis of the elementary case of choice that grounds the formal idea of rational choice in the structure of the will-act itself. Husserl lays out the case in numbered steps. Here is present: I can A, I can B; and in the "I can" lies that each is realizable — or both at once, about which nothing is said — each being an in-itself possibly existing thing, each possible will-goal being possible as possibly being and at once willfully realizable, that is, possible as the endpoint of an action of one and the same person. I ask now, choosingly: Shall I A or B? Asking so, I have not only thought A and B as possible will-goals, but the actions are thereby thought as *separate*: the A-goal does not lie on the way to the goal B, and conversely. Further there lies in it that I think A as done, but nothing else to it — not B and nothing else at all — and think B as done in its stead; each I think as the *solely* done.

Husserl then carries out the will-analysis that grounds the choice, recovering the doctrine, central to Part A's § 15 and Beilage I, that the will is a positing of an *ought*. In every doing lies a practical holding-for-value: the done appears as a realized value, where the action is already thought as a possible action; but "A I shall do" means not merely that the will-goal A is conscious as a value in this sense. To the willing there underlies a value-holding: if I think myself into a willing, the willed stands there as value-held, and the "will-meaning" directs itself upon it — and it is not a value-meaning but a *will*\-meaning. Yet one can say that the same says that in the willing the willed stands there as *ought*; and the ought, the ought-being, says a positing-correlate that is analogous to "being": it can be a truly ought and not, and in this it is indifferent whether the goal is attained or not. In this way every willing is a "positing," and indeed a positing of an ought; it is in itself the consciousness of an ought. "That shall be" is a peculiar proposition-kind, the will-proposition considered from the standpoint of the willing; it is a "realizing" positing, and the posited as such is the supposedly-ought, and if the positing is in the mode of "executing," then the ought — meant as an ought-being — is at the endpoint of the action a being: out of the ought-being and not-yet-being there becomes a being that is and is "as it ought" — supposedly, of course. If the will-consciousness is a reason-consciousness, then to the "supposedly" there corresponds a "really"; for in every willing, every deciding and doing, there lies an ought-positing that can be right and wrong, and it implies as its foundation a valuing positing, a holding-for-good, that for its part and in its way can be right and wrong. The will, that is, has two superimposed thetic layers — the valuing and the ought-positing — each subject to its own rightness.

Husserl carefully separates the *will-doubt* from the theoretical doubt, and so the choice from a factual disjunction. What does "Shall I A or shall I B?" say? I can also say "Will I A or will I B?", and sometimes we express ourselves so; but we notice that this expression is ambiguous and is probably for that reason avoided. It can be understood psychologically-factually: then we have a disjunction between two facts — either there takes place in my soul the fact that I will A, or the fact that I will B. But of that there is no talk in the sense here in question; I am now not interested in the positing of facts, I perform no "theoretical" doubt, no doubt whether it is or is not — no being-question is here posed — but a *will-doubt*: I live in the willing and do not make the willing into a matter of fact. Living in the willing, the held-for-value is conscious to me in it as "ought," as ought-being, and that in the pure sense of a correlate such as belongs to every "positing-form." And now, just as in the sphere of the "theoretical positing," the being-positing, we have the known modifications — the proposition as thetic proposition, as hypothetical antecedent and consequent, as disjunctive proposition that is a member of a full positing disjunction — so too here: we have the willing thesis, the thetic "I will," the "shall," and the hypothetical and disjunctive — one of A and B shall be, one of them I will — and likewise the corresponding theoretical and will-doubts, "Is A or B?" (possibly, for the will as psychic fact, "Did he will this or that, will he will it?") and "Shall A or B be?" (in the other sense, "Will I A or B?"). The whole apparatus of apophantic modalization, that is, has its voluntaristic parallel.

Husserl identifies the will-disjunction as the basic character of choice and unfolds choosing as a kind of questioning that terminates in a weighted decision. In the choice there lies a *will-disjunction* — that is its basic character — and the correlate is the disjunctive ought-question; choosing is at first a kind of questioning, only not theoretical questioning or doubting. But that is not yet the full choosing: by it we understand a deciding precisely on the ground of such a doubting, more closely of such a to-and-fro deliberating and considering; and with "deliberating" there is already designated the direction upon a solution of the doubt by a decision, and with the "weighing" that "weights" are in play — similarly as with the surmise-doubt and the surmise-decision that terminates in the decision following the "preponderance." At every doubt, at every disjunctive question, one must consider whether the *exclusive* disjunction does not belong to its sense; and it seems that this is the case, for the decision is always a decision for a member of the exclusive disjunction. Living into a choosing in which a realizable A and a realizable B are conscious to me, each as the end of an action and each thought in some value — for in real acting the goal is always held-for-value, and indeed predominantly positively, though logically I can think an action where this is not the case, which is no theoretical but a *bouletic countersense*, so that I can think that I value what I do not hold for value and conversely, and can so weigh possible actions directed even upon the negative-valued — assuming now that A is the better according to its value: then evidently the will-question is decided for the better, that is, if it is in question whether A shall be or B shall be (in the practical sense "shall," will I A or B?), then apriori the answer runs "A shall be\!" if A is of greater value than B. The rational will follows, in a choice, always the greatest value; or, to the greatest value in an exclusive disjunction standing in choice there corresponds the ought.

Husserl sharpens the difference between this and the complete-disjunction formulation, and connects it to the claim that choice is the form of volitive grounding. This says *not*: if one of A, B, C, which form a complete disjunction, is the ought, then it is the one that is the best; but rather: if between A and B the choice hovers, if the will-doubt — the exclusive will-question, "Will I A alone (shall I) or B alone?" — *holds* (which, Husserl glosses in a footnote, means that the question is an *objective* question, not that the fact of doubting takes place, but that it is objectively valid, with its reason-presuppositions), then there belongs to it the "answer" of the will: the ought is the best. It is to be considered, he adds, whether it is right, as he said last in the lecture, that the choice is the form of the *volitive grounding*, the grounding of ought-truth. He then probes the phenomenology of considering "A alone": if I present to myself a positive-valued A as the end of an action, as practically realizable, and have only this A alone in view, then the will thereupon appears as right, it belongs to the valuing, and the meant ought is real, the willed as such is "really" ought. What does it mean that I have only A in view? It can say nothing other than: assuming A alone is practically in question, belongs alone in the will-realm — does it not lie therein that, supposing A were alone there, as the only one I can realize, I *abstract* from the existence of other practical possibilities? And with regard to a choice that begins with a pair and has as yet no survey of the will-realm, the matter is so: I look at first only at A and B, but I leave further possibilities indeterminately open, so that I have the will-question "Will I A or B, or what shall I otherwise?" Seeing now that A is inferior to B, it is already evident that I shall it not. The value-absorption consists, one could say, in this: that I can indeed say that A comes into question, into consideration, by virtue of its positive value, and that if it were alone it would be an ought, but that it drops out of consideration again, since against B it no longer weighs; the better stays in consideration in relation to the unweighed members, the lesser is already excluded as in any case a not-ought. It is, Husserl admits, not easy to penetrate here to full sharpness; the matter gives itself so that one would like to say that the positive-valued A, considered for itself, wakes an inclination of the will toward it, appears as practically good, as ought-being — but only considered for itself, only relatively; for as I look further, I recognize that it vanishes against the better. The phenomenology of the elementary choice thus exhibits the absorption-law as a description of how the apparently-ought of the lesser is revoked the moment the practical realm widens.

Husserl closes the Beilage, and with it Part B, by reaffirming the distinction between the value of a willing as fact and the rightness of the willing — the same act/value distinction that governs Part A's § 20 antinomy of insight. It is also to be noted, he says, that the willing of the good, the realizing of a good, is as a *fact* a good — for what realizes a good has the significance of a derived value, and so far also the willing; but it is a quite other question whether a willing is a *right* willing, exactly as it is one question whether the existence of a judging in the world, for whatever grounds, has a value, and another question whether the judging is a rational one. With this distinction — the value of the act as an event in the world versus the rightness of the act as a position-taking — the 1911 Schlußstück and its appendices close, having grounded the formal idea of rational choice in the thetic structure of the will and set it within the encompassing program, drawn in the Einleitung, of a phenomenology and critique of the emotive and practical reason.
